Referring to your telegram of Jen. 15 after friends we had
disoussion with Kurusu he consulted with Yoshino and has now
replied substantially as follows FIRST They greatly appre-
ciate your willingness make proposals on lines suggested and
they emphasize Gover ment does not wish drive foreign in-
terests from Japan but at the sane time cannot change law
SECOND Kurusu points out that elthough Janan legislation might
be said to set precedent of six months stock holding it elso
creates precedent for co..pensation to which principle Govern-
ment is now committed and is willing to consider proposals
from our side if we regard contemplated compensation is in-
adequate SToP This he considers alters situation and from
business standpoint should entitle Japan to prior considera-
tion so long as supplies for stock holding on ecual terms are
obtainable and while recognizing force of our argument that
general practice of stock holding might make it physically
impossible for industry to find supplies and thus defeat object
of Japan Government he indicated that Japanese Government must
take this risk and suggested that we on our part should safeguard
ourselves by introducing stipulation that we should be relieved
of stock holding obligation in the event of such contingency STOP
Kurusu also emnhasized that recent European developments while
justifying fears that Japan legislation might spread also were
serving to strengthen conviction of Military that stocks as pre-
soribed by Law must be accumulated against emergencies never-
theless Friends and we feel that Government still is prepared
to receive suggestion for obviating increasing stock holding
obligation whioch in turn would tend to make precedent of Japan
Law less objectionable but in view of Diet dissolved today and
not likely to be reconvened until early April Government has now
more time for negotiation and attitude likely to be stronger at
least for the time being.

February 25, 1936.
me
Referenoe Tokyo's telegram No. 34
of February 24, 8 p.m., in regard to
the eil situation in Japan, which should
be read in its entirety, --
It would appear that the Japanese
authorities, although remaining adamant
in their refusal to change the law re-
quiring the storage of stocks equivalent
to six-months' supply, are nevertheless
making a serious effort to arrive at a
ecmpremise measure acceptable to the
foroign eil interests. In this conneo-
tiem partioular note should be made of
subseetion (e) of paragraph one.
Preparedness on the part of the
Japanese authorities to consider as
acceptable to them a certain number of
gallems of oil (equal to a six-months'
supply in the years 1954 or 1935)
rather than a flexible amount equal to
cme-half of the previous year's supply
is merely a face-saving device which in
the leng run may or may not be of ad-
vantage to the foreign oil interests.
As the New York office of the
Stamdard-Vacuum Oil Company has been
infermed of the developments outlined
in Tekyo's telegram under reference, no
getiom is at present required of the
Department.

PS
2-No. 34, February 24, 8 p. m. from Tokyo
(a). Mitsubishi and Nippon Oil have applied for
permission to add to their refining facilities, probably
having in mind an eventual refusal of the foreign oil
interests to comply with the law and consequent reduc-
tion of their quotas. The Vice Minister is now holding
up these applications but will be compelled to grant
them if the foreign oil companies do not agree to carry
six months stocks.
(e). Therefore, the Vice Minister recommends that
the foreign oil interests present a concrete proposal
offering to store six months stocks under the following
or somewhat similar conditions: (one)-that six months of
1934 r 1935 be taken as the maximum storage requirement;
(two)-that adequate compensation be paid by the govern-
ment; (three)-that adequate guarantees of future business
be given.
(f). If the Vice Minister has some such proposal,
he can continue the present quotas, at least until June
30, 1936, and can refuse the applications for additional
、refining capacity, on the ground that the foreign oil
companies have not refused to comply with the law but on
the contrary have agreed to store the oil under cortain
conditions. The foreign oil companies objections to the
establishment

Two. The local representatives of the foreign oil
interests have telegraphed the substance of the above

Mr. Teagle said that he would send me a copy of that
telegram also.
Mr. Teagle said that his people were trying to get

There followed a brief disoussion, in the course of
which Mr. Teagle mentioned the possibility of agreeing
upon a fixed amount of oil to be stored, whioh amount would
remain constant even though the business might inorease,
in which case there would be a progressive reduction in
the ratio. I reaffirmed our hope that the interests will,
in whatever deoision they make, preserve a united front.
Mr. Teagle said that they must "hang together or be hanged
separately". I said that I felt that that was correot.
Mr. Teagle said that when they talked with London he would
let me know the upshot of their conversation.
Expressions of thanks were erohanged, and the conversa-

Mr. P. W. Parker)
THE UNDEM SEGRETAKY

Jolayo-
Mr. J. C. Goold ) Oil Company, New York.

250
Mr. Hornbeck
DEPARTMENT DE STATE
B1:1936
894.63
Subjeot: Oil Situation in Japan.
Eopartmenr of Stale
In New York, on Friday, February 7, I had a confer-
363
ence with Mr. Parker and Mr. Goold, at Mr. Parker's
request, in the course of whioh Mr. Parker gave me the
/2
papers here attached.
5
O
From these papers, and from what was said to me,
it is apparent that at the present stage there is a tendency
toward divergence of view, with regard to advisable course
for the oompanies to pursue, between the British interests
and the American interests. The view of the British inter-
ests is preoisely and definitely set forth in the four-page
memorandum dated January 18 and entitled "J A P A N". Put
briefly, the British interests want to present a stirf front
without making either threats or commitments of oonoessio
The American interests lean toward the idea of making a
oompromise with the Japanese Government and conoluding with
that Government an agreement. In the paper dated Febru-
ary 7 and entitled "M E M O R A N D U M", they give an
outline of the thought of the British interests and of
their own thought. This they bring to a conolusion with
the

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 -
the statement that they wish to take no steps which are
contrary to the interests of this country and they there-
fore seek the opinion and advice of the Seoretary of State
on two questions. Having read over these papers, I made
the observation that I assumed that we were disoussing
the whole situation informally and that the questions on
which the American interests sought opinion and advice
wore being put informally and might in due course be
answered informally. To this Mr. Parker and Mr. Goold
gave assent.
There followed a general discussion of the situation.
In the course of this disoussion I developed in particular
two points. First, that in our view (in the Department)
it is highly desirable that the four parties which are con-
fronted by the Japanese Petroleum Law and regulations
with greatest consequent conoern -- namely, the British
interests, the British Government, the American interests,
and the American Government -- persevere in the maintenance
of a united front: with the British Government and the
British interests in agreement, and with the American Gov-
ernment having already made representations to the Japanese
Government indicative of its.disapproval of the position
which the Japanese Government has taken with regard to the
matter, it will be unfortunate if the American interests
docided to follow one course, a course of compromise, and
the British interests decided to follow another course,
that of "standing pat." Second, I developed the point that,

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.97)
一 3 -
if the Japanese Government succeeded in driving a wedge
between the British interests and Government and the
Amerioan interests and Government, and if the American
idtyte oitn the ti
interests concluded an agreement with the Japanese Govern-
ment, the American interests would be taking a substantial
risk: they could by no means assume that an agreement thus
ooncluded, satisfactory though it might be to them when
concluded, would absolutely ensure their position; it would
be an agreement between two parties which are not equals,
an agreement not enforoeable in any court, an agreement which
one of the parties (the Japanese Government) could disregard
at its own option and convenienoe and which, in the light
of history, would probably be effective only so long as
the Japanese military authorities felt it to be in Japan's
interest for them to permit the carrying out of its pro-
visions.
Mr. Parker made the point that, as things exist, the
British and American interests will soon be in the position,
in Japan, of lawbreakers. I pointed out that there are
different kinds of breaches of law: there is such a thing
as a positive breach, and there is such a thing as a
negative breach. The Japanese law under consideration re-
quires that the companies assume certain unusual burdens
which the companies and their Governments respeotively and
colleotively feel are unwarranted and unreasonable; the
question of complianoe is a subjeot of current discussion;
failure to have complied by a certain date may put the
companies

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4-
companies in the position of being lawbreakers, but their
i1
breaches will be breaohes of a negative charaoter. It may
be presumed that none of the Governments ooncerned would
is nshcass
feel toward the companies as the said Governments would
feel if the companies were committing positive breaches of
law. I added, for precaution, that I was expressing
merely a personal opinion.
It was agreed that I would bring to Washington the
papers which Mr. Parker had handed to me and would study
the questions on which he sought opinion and advice and
would see what answer, if any, might be made thereto, it
boing understood that the answer, if made, would probably
be made informally.
The question asked reads as follows:
"Can it be construed as prejudicial to the
interests of this country under existing laws
or under the present policy of the Government,
if:-
"A. We adopt the policy proposed by our
British Friends which may involve deliberate
non-complianoe with the Japanese Law?
"or
"B. Comply in principle with the 6 months'
storage provision provided suitable compensation
and guarantees can be negotiated?"
Comment.
It is my opinion that the plan of aotion proposed by
the British interests is preferable to the alternative which
the American interests have under consideration. The British
interests have definitely declared their position and the
British

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.93)
-5 -
tate
British Government is in acoord therewith. I do not believe
that Japanese susoeptibilities will be outraged by the
"non-compliance with the Japanese Law" contemplated in the
British plan. The taking of a different position by the
Amorioan interests would weaken the position both of the
British and of the American interests, and that development
would play right into the hands of the Japanese Government.
It would not, in my opinion, in the long run ensure either
property or profits to the American interests.
I feel that we should advise the American interests,
informally and orally, that, although the decision must be
entirely theirs, we incline to believe that the course which
the British interests have declared their intention of
pursuing has more to commend it than any alternative course
with regard to whioh our opinion has been sought.
SKr

Our British Friends propose to agree to carry three

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
J nu ry 13, 1936
JAPAN
We note that friends are pressing for our views
as to what should be done in case our hopes of settlements
along the lines of the 5 point Memorandum are not realized.
First of all we want to say very definitely
that we ourselves ti ink it is a mistake at tnis stage to
endeavour to formulate a plan of campaign based on such
a future happening, as there always may be circumstances
then that we do not realise now, and we would much prefer
tc continue at present with ne ain cnl: in view, and that
is sui table modification of the Law on the lines of the
5-Points Memorandum.
Nonetheless we have attempted to visualise
what might happen if all our efforts are unsuccessful.
Here are the possibilities as we see them:-
(1)
Compliance with the Petroleum Law subject to any
modification that we can secure.
(2)
A face-saving device whereby tie capanese Government
get the stocks they want in the country without
expense or obligation to ourselves.
(3)
To adopt a purely passive attitude; to go on trading
so long as, and to the extent that, we are allowed
to trade without complying wi th the Law.
(4)
Definitely to withdraw from the country, coupled
perhaps with arrangements for c.i.f. supplies under
long term contracts.

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
2
aling with these four heads we have the following remarks
to mako:
(1)
We have made up our minds that we will not comply
with the present Law, and we would fur ther point
out that we wculd certainly not agree to a
suggestion, which nes been thrown out by the
Japanese Government, that we mi-ht comply wi th
the Law by doing a very much smaller trade wnile
still maintaining our present scale of stocks in
the country. It is ur intention that if our quota
is cut down we will reduce our stocks to such
quantities as are reasonably necessary in our
opinion to carry on our then trade. In other words,
if our quota is cut we will correspondingly reduce
our stocks in the country to the present ratio of
storage to sales, namely 2/3 months, even if this
results in idle tankage.
From the above it is evident that in no
circumstances will we comply with the six-months'
stock provision, even if this means withdrawal from
Japan, and further we will not put war stocks
at our expense in any country.
In addition, even if we secure reasonable
modification of the Law concerning six-months'
stocks, we s till would never agree to maintaining
stocks wh ich the Government at all times has an
option to purchase at their price and which we

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
3
are constantly required to replenish.
(2)
If it were possible that the Japanese Government
as a face-saving device should arrange for a
Japanese Company to rinance the addi tional tankage
necessary and the additional stocks required under
the Law, we  ight be prepared to be a seller of
products to such Coipany on purely conmercial lines.
We would not, however, be parties to any arrangement
under which we would be fcrced suppliers at this
Company's or a t the option of the Japenese Government.
Moreover, this does not mean that we will enter into
any partnership with Japanese interests to w ich we
are opposed.
(3)
It is hardly necessary to elaborate this point, but
we wish to make it clear that our idea here is to
throw the whole onus cn to ti.e apanese Government
to decide the extent of our withdrawal and the rate
of our withdrawal. Realising, as we do, that we may
not find ready buyers for our properties in Japan, we
feel that we s hould continue to trade so long as we
are allowed and to the extent that we are allowed, as,
in our opinion, we are not likely to be driven
hurriedly frm the market since, in our view, the
Japanese Government and the Japanese public would
require considerable time to develop other methods
of supplying the country and an organisation for
distributing in replacement of ours.

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
(4)
From (3) above we have made it clear that it is not
our intention, should the Japanese Government refuse
to modify the present Law, to tell them that we will
trade no longer in Japan and immediately withdraw.
It is, of course, always possible tnat in the end we
may be forced to witndraw entirely, and then and at
that time the uestion of c.i.f. sales will arise,
but it is hardly necessary to consider the point now.
We might make it clear, however, tiat in our view
c.i.f. business carries no goodwill and that it is
always open to the lowest bidder.

(2)

Subjeot: Oil situation in Japan.
NOTED
Mr. Parker, by means of a telephone call from New
894.6363/25
York, referred to his conversation with Mr. Hornbeok on
February 7 (see FE's memorandum of February 10, 1936) in
regard to the oil situation in Japan and inquired whether
Mr. Hornbeck was in position to reply to the two ques-
tions set forth in the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company's
memorandum of February 7 (copy, together with other
papers, handed to Mr. Hornbeck in the course of the
above-mentioned conversation) and repeated on page 4 of
FE's memorandum of February 10.
Mr. Hornbeck then gave as an informal reply the sub-
stance of that portion of FE's memorandum of February 10
FILED
(see pages 4 and 5) which appears under the heading "Com-
ment".
Mr. Parker said that he hoped to be able to keep the
discussion of this phase of the company's difficulties on
a purely informal basis but inquired whether the Depart-
ment, in the event that a formal statement of its views
should

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
should seem desirable, would be prepared to make such a
statement.
Mr. Hornbeck replied by expressing the opinion that
the Department would not wish to state formally and in
writing its views in regard to the subject under disous-
sion whioh is one for consideration and decision by the
concerned companies. At this point Mr. Hornbeck again
stressed the desirability of the American and British oil
interests continuing to present a united front to the
Japanesé Government and of endeavoring to avoid any seri-
ous eleavage of opinion between the two interests which
might enable the Japanese Government to play one against
the other.
Mr. Hornbeok inquired whether Mr. Teagle planned to
be in Washington within the near future. Mr. Parker re-
plied that he did not know. Mr. Hornbeck said that at
the time of the inception of the oil difficulties in
Japan he had disoussed the situation with Mr. Teagle and
that beeause of this and of other faotors he had of late
hoped that an opportunity might be found for a further
oral disoussion of the matter. Mr. Hornbeok added, how-
ever, that he did not wish Mr. Teagle to make a special
trip to Washington in order to make possible such a dis-
cussion.

LONDON, FEB. 24, 1936.
Referring to your telegram 18th this month we feel very strongly that as the whole
issue is not directly between head offices of the oil companies and the Japanese
Government it would be very unwise for you to make definite suggestion-s even
though you emphasize that they were merely local FULusToP What we want is mome
clear cut proposal from the Japanese Government on which we can give decision here
FULLSTOP For your private information only and also to enable us to have your
opinion and the opinion-s of friend-s representative-s we might be prepared to
consider a scheme which in fact would be as advantageous to the Japanese Government
as the scheme-s outlined in your cable of Feb. l8th but being turned round the other
way would be much less obnoxious to us from the world wide point of view FULLSTOP
We would emphasize that one main objection to our complying with the petroleum law-s
is the precedent such compliance would set to other government-s FULLSTOP
e
therefore tentatively suggest a scheme might be devised whereby the Government
themselves financed and owned for the time being extra tankage end extra stock-s
necessary to comply with the law on some modified cuantitative basis as now en-
visaged and that these tanks be erected on or neighboring to oil company-ies
property-ies so that we could look after stock-s etc. for the government and so
that the government could give us right to purchase from them tanks and stock-s in
whole or in part as increasing trade should make them commercially useful to us
FULLSTOP For such right we might be prepared to pay an annual sum equivalent to a
low rate of interest on the loan proposition put forward by you but this would not
be called interest and it would not be dangerous as a precedent for the rest of the
world seeing that the non commercial stock and storage would be provided (for) by the
government FULLSTOP It is needless to say that this proposal would have the added
merit providing an incentive to the Japanese Government to give us the largestpossible
quotas in order to recover their capital as soon as possible SToP If storage were
built in locality-ies satisfactory to us in connection with our present business we

own and also to supervise erection if Government so desired FULLSTOP Please
cuss with friend-s representative-s and give us your views on such proposal-s but
on no account make any suggestion-s concerning same to the Government at present
FULLSTOP Your telegram Feb. 24th received since writing the above but we will
not reply until we receive your answer to this present telegram of ours.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 1653, dated January 24, 1936, and to previous corres-
pondence on the subject of the difficulties being experienced
in Japan by the American and other foreign oil interests
since the promulgation of the Petroleum Industry Law, and
to report the following developments in the situation.
FILED
Conversations.
been proceeding steadily between Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the
Bureau
F/FG

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Orfice, and the
looal representatives of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
and the Rising Sun Oil Company, but as yet without any de-
finite result. On February 5, 1936, the two oil companies
1/
received identic telegrams from their head offices(enclosure 1),
stating that the oil interests appreciated the frank and
fair-minded way in which the discussions have progressed,
but pointing out that no concessions as yet made by the Japa-
nese Government have relieved the companies of the burden of
carrying six months' stocks, which they feel will cause them
to depart from their position as bona fide traders and will
create a dangerous precedent. They therefore asked the local
representatives of the oil companies, in consultation with
Mr. Kurusu, to endeavor to find some solution of the diffi-
culty either on the basis of the five-point memorandum of
last April, or in some other manner.
The message of the oil interests was conveyed to Mr.
Kurusu by the local representatives of the oil companies on
February 8, 1936. A memorandum of the conversation of that
2/
day is enclosed. After some discussion of what constituted
a"bona fide trader" and the effect of the stook-holding ob-
ligation upon the companies' position as bona fide traders,
Mr. Kurusu suggested that perhaps the objection to the stock-
holding obligation might be overcome if a "ceiling" were put
upon the amount of the stocks. Instead of keeping each year
stocks to the amount of one-half of the previous year's
imports, the amount to be carried could be fixed at one-half
of the imports of a certain year. As the trade increased, the
non-commercial part of the stocks would be gradually absorbed
into

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
into the commercial stooks, so that in the course of time
the companies would be carrying only the normal amount of
commercial stocks. Mr. Kurusu also expressed disappoint-
ment that the headquarters of the oil interests had not
presented any concrete proposals, in accordance with his
request of January 11 (see despatch No. 1653), but stated
that he would discuss the message of the oil interests with
Mr. Yoshino, the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry.
Mr. Kurusu also discussed the oil problem informally
with r. Dickover of the Embassy on several occasions. He
is obviously worried concerning the situation and is sin-
cerely endeavoring to find a solution which will satisfy
the militarists in their demand for emergency stocks of
oil while avoiding the necessity of placing an undue burden
upon the foreign oil companies. At one time he suggested
that it might be possible to interest Japanese capital in
some proposition of acting as storage agents for the foreign
oil companies. The foreign oil compenies would retain their
Japan business but their non-commerciel storage under the
law would be carried by Japanese interests, who would obtain
their interest and depreciation charges from the compensa-
tion which will be given by the Japanese Government. Upon
looking into this proposition, however, it was found that
the compensation allowed by the Government would be insuffi-
cient to pay interest and other cherges on the capital in-
volved. r. Dickover at one time suggested to Mr. Kurusu
that it might be feasible for the Government itself to
finance the extra stocks of oil by issuing to the companies
bonds to cover the costs involved, the properties to be
held by the companies until the bonds were liquidated, when
th

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
the Government would become the owner of the storage faci-
lities and the stocks of oil. Mr.Kurusu examined this plan,
but reported that the Finance Ministry was opposed to the
issuance of more bonds than absolutely necessary at the
present time. Mr. Kurusu is now concentrating on his plan
of putting a "ceiling" on the amount of the stocks, and
perhaps of specifying the amount to be carried in terms of
quantity(i.e., as so many gallons) rather than in terms of
time (i.e. as six months' stocks). He thought that this plan
would to some extent relieve the oil interests from the
danger of other countries copying the Japanese system. He
hopes that some such arrangement as this, with adequate
compensation and guarantees of future business at profitable
prices, will satisfy the oil companies. As yet, however,
he has not succeeded in formulating concrete proposals to
lay before the foreign oil interests.
Liquefaction of Coal.
It will be recalled that in December last the American
and British oil interests offered their patents for the
hydrogenation of coal to the Japanese Government in exchange
for a return to the program outlined in the five-point memo-
randum of last April, but that the Japanese authorities did
not show any marked enthusiasm in the project, claiming
that research in Japan had gone beyond the process offered
by the oil interests(see the memorandum of the conversation
of December 27, 1935, transmitted with the Embassy's despatoh
No. 1629, dated January 10, 1936). In January there was a
a meeting of experts from various interested Ministries at
May
Tokuyama,

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5-
Tokuyama, where the Naval Fuel Depot is located, to study
the results of the experiments on the liquefaction of coal
which have been conducted there for some years past. Ac-
cording to the NIKKAN SHOGYO SHIBUN (Daily Industrial News)
of February 15, 1936, the Minister for the Navy reported to
the Cabinet on February 14 that the experiments had been
successful and that it had been decided to entrust the South
Manchuria Railway with the task of using the process in
actual production. According to the plan, the South Man-
churia Railway is to establish a plant at Fushun, Manchuria,
with a capital of about Yen 15,000,000, to be completed in
Thry
1938. The production is to be 20,000 metric tons of oil
per annum. The Navy will purchase the entire output and
will also subsidize the undertaking. According to the news-
paper, however, the project will not be commercielly profi-
table and is being undertaken purely as a matter of national
policy. The process, it is claimed, will produce half as much
oil as the weight of the coal used for raw material, but as an
equal amount of coal must be used to manufacture the necessary
hydrogen, the actual production of oil will be only 25 per
cent of the coal used.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch No.
1692 of February 20, 1936 from
the Embassy at Tokyo.
5th February, 1936.
CABLED MESSAGE RECEIVED WITH REFERENCE TO INTERVIEW WITH
MR. KURUSU on 21st JANUARY 1936.
We greatly appreciate the frankness with whioh the
discussions with Mr. Kurusu have been conducted as we are
anxious to meet the views of the Government wherever this
can be accomplished without departing from our position as
bona fide traders.
The principle of compensation demonstrates the Government's
fair-mindedness but does not really assist us because we are
still placed in a position which is not that of bona fide traders
by reason of the fact that we have to provide and finance emergenoy
stocks, together with the tankage and other plant required to
accommodate them. Neither should we be assisted by the suggested
safeguards, i.e. stipulations on our part that we should be re-
lieved of stock-holding obligations in the event of the prece-
dent of these obligations being followed by other countries,
because the damage would have been done before the suggested
remedies could be applied.
As regards Mr. Kurusu's point that recent developments
in Europe have served to strengthen the view of some people that
the stocks stipulated by the Law must be accumulated against
emergencies- we appreciate its soundness but the same arguments
show how hopeless our position as bona fide traders would be if
we were to try to undertake to finance such emergency stocks.
We would ask the Government to appreciate our world-wide position
and realize that our freedom of action as bona fide traders most
certainly would be restricted when it became known that we had
departed from a purely trading position and, apart from our
own views of the matter, we are strongly of the opinion that this
position would not be advantageous on their side.
Therefore while we greatly value the Government's assurance
that they do not wish to drive foreign interests from Japan
and while on our part we are most anxious to continue trading
in Japan, we sincerely trust that with the help of the Govern-
ment some means can be devised which will make these two ex-
pressions of opinion capable of realization: the Five Point
Memorandum was designed to that end and so far we have not been
able to suggest an alternative which would meet both points of
view. Therefore we should like you and Friends' representative
in consultation with Mr. Kurusu to try to find a solution either
by means of the Five Point Memorandum or in another manner.

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No.1692 of February 20, 1936
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
NOTES OF MEETING AT FOREIGN OFFICE
on February 8th. 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. Kurusu (Chief of Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Office)
Messrs. Ely (R.S.P.Co.) and Meyer (S.V.O.C.)
Messrs Ely and Meyer said that they had received a
cabled message from their principals which they would like
to show Mr. Kurusu and discuss with him.
Mr. Kurusu then read through the attached paraphrase
of London's and New York's cable dated 5th February. He
commented that it was "rather vague" and professed not to
understand fully the argument that the Companies would not
be bona fide traders. Was it not bona fide trading if the
Companies were paid the cost of carrying additional stocks,
plus interest on the capital invested for that purpose?
Anyhow, to trade in a country one had to conform to the laws
of that country: in Japan the law prescribed that to be a
petroleum trader one had to carry certain stocks. Moreover
the Japanese Companies had financed and accumulated stocks
as prescribed: the foreign Companies were being asked to
do no more than the Japanese Companies and this was in accord-
ance with and not in any way contrary to the Treaties.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely pointed out that, even if they
received compensation as planned by the Government, plus a re-
turn on the investment, the Companies would still have to find
the money to supply and finance a large quantity of non-
commercial stocks: they were oil traders, not financiers,
and as such were not prepared to tie up such large sums-
which at the same time they might require for bona fide
trading - in investments on which they would not be free
to realize when they wished to do so. They had not claimed
that they were being asked to do more in Japan than the Japa-
nese Companies: both were being asked to bear a non-com-
mercial burden in respeot of their trade in Japan, but their
own situation was entirely different from that of the Japanese
Companies in that the latter's burden would remain only local
when similar obligations were imposed by other conntries,
whereas their own burden would be increased to an impossible
extont.
Mr. Kurusu commented that the Japanese Government could
not be expeoted to diotate to other Governments what they should
or should not do. Messrs. Ely and Meyer agreed, but suggested
that it would be possible and reasonable for the Japanese
Government to take into consideration the likely effects of
the precedent created by the stock-holding obligations of
the Japan Law, especially as London and New York had shown
how these effects might eventually defeat the Government's
fundamental objective.
近.

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. Kurusu then read through the message again and
said he was disappointed that it made no concrete proposal.
It referred to a solution by means of the Five Point Memo-
randum or "in another manner": did not London and New York
have some thing definite in mind? The Five Point Memo-
randum was of course not binding: it had been mede clear in
conversations with the Embassies and had been noted in writing
that a change in the Law could not be promised- and it had
been found impossible to make any change. Mr. Kurusu did not
see why London and New York could not make some other definite
proposal, say on the lines he had already suggested.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer said thet Mr. Kurusu had asked for
concrete proposals in conformation with the Law as it stood. It
seemed quite clear to them that on that basis London and New
York could not adduce any terms or conditions which would
overcome the important and far-reaching point which had been
brought out in their recent discussions with Mr. Kurusu. The
Five Point Memorandum had been designed to overcome that point:
if, as Mr. Kurusu had explained, the Law and Imperial Ordinance
could not be altered could not some other means be found to
achieve the intended effect of the Five Point Memorandum and to
dispose of the dangers which their Principals foresaw?
Mr. Kurusu said that he would have to discuss London's
and New York's message with Mr. Yoshino, but he went on imme-
diately to suggest that the whole situation would be changed
if a "ceiling" were put on the stock-holding obligation.
At present, according to the Law and Regulations, stocks
would have perpetually to increase. This in his opinion, as he
had already told Messrs. Ely and Meyer, was impracticable and
ridioulous. What the Government really wanted, he thought,
was to have in position a certain quantity of Oil rather than
any particular proportion of the annual consumption. He would
therefore like the Companies to think of the stock-holding
obligation in terms of gallons of Oil. The Imperial Ordinance
said that stocks were to be one-half of one year's imports:
suppose the year taken was l935; that would mean so many gallons...
Messrs. Ely and Meyer commented that that figure would
involve a large quantity of non-commercial stocks. Mr. Kurusu
agreed but said that for the present, with a comperatively
small and rapidly increasing consumption, Japan needed to have
in stock against emergencies a larger quantity of oil than
that represented by ordinary commercial stocks. Japan had only
about l40,000 motor cars. In other countries with many more cars
and muoh larger and more stationary offtakes ordinary com-
meroial stocks were quantitatively large and, moreover, in times
of emergency such more-developed countries could economize
in consumption and so create, as it were, additional stocks.
Japan could not do this and consequently tor the time being
was in a special position. Nevertheless, with a quantitative
maximum set on stock-holding and consumption goind up rapidly,
the non-commercial stocks now required would eisappear in a few
years - Mr. Kurusu did not know quite how many, but Messrs.
Ely and Meyer could work it out - and the additional tankage
involved would by then be needed for ordinary stocks and working.
MOs

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
Messrs. Ely and Meyer said that, although they could
not estimate off-hand the exact period, they knew it would
take a long time for non-commercial stocks to disappear
in the way suggested by Mr. Kurusu and during that period
the Companies would be put in the position of not being
bona fide traders. There was also the risk that later on a
higher quantitative maximum might be demanded. Moreover if
consumption stopped inoreasing, the result envisaged by Mr.
Kurusu would never come about. Mr. Kurusu rejoined that
there was in his opinion little fear that consumption would
fail to go on inoreasing - and increasing rapidly - and he
intimated that the Companies could ask for some sort of
guarantee in regard to the ceiling to be put on stock-holding.
In regard to the Companies' position, he felt that in the cir-
cumstances which would be brought about by a quantitative
maximum it could not be contended that they were not bona fide
traders provided they received adequate compensation for
the amount of stock carried in excess of three months:and
he considered that, with adequate compensation and the guarantees
he had previously suggested, they ought to be prepared to
supply and finance temporarily a diminishing quantity of non-
commercial stocks.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer pointed out that, even in the
circumstances suggested, there was no getting away from the
fact that the Companies actually would be financing non-com-
mercial stocks for a period of years and this would still
create a dangerous precedent. Mr. Kurusu expected the
offtake in Japan to continue to increase rapidly: therefore
could not the quantitative level desired by the Government
be achieved by the inorease in commercial stocks?
Mr. Kurusu said that the Government could not risk
waiting for commercial stocks to increase to the quantity
desired. He had explained the special position in whioh Japan
was placed and he felt that what Japan wanted could be justified
from the point of view of the actual quantity of oil involved.
In the countries likely to want to copy Japan consumption and
ordinary stocks were, he thought, quantitatively large and,
as he had pointed out, they could be utilized more effeotively
in time of emergency. He felt therefore that the Companies
would be able to resist the introduction by such countries
of obligations involving non-commercial stocks: in fact, if the
quantity of Oil involved was taken into consideration, no
dangerous precedent was being created by Japan.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer agreed that Mr. Kurusu's
"quantitative" point might hold good in some instances but
it was in their opinion most unlikely that it could be used
by the Companies to prevent other countries following the
example set by the Japan Law. Even if they were made appli-
cable to l935, the very words "one half of one year's imports"
were a dangerous precedent and, if they could not be altered,
could not the Government itselr finance the diminishing
quentity of non-commercial stooks which would be temporarily
required and so obviate the precedent to which the Companies
objected and the far-reaching dangers which they foresaw?

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. Kurusu said this definitely could not be done
and he concluded the interview by stating that what he
had suggested was his own idea. He would have to disouss
the Direotors' message with Mr. Yoshino and he did not know
'what the latter would have to say. There was still the al-
ternative solution that the Companies should cut down
their trade so that they could carry the stocks required
by the Government in their existing tankage. Messrs. Ely
and Meyer emphasized that this would be no solution as it
would still involve carrying non-commercial stocks. Mr.
Kurusu admitted the point and said he had thought of a
reduction in trade rather from the point of view that it
would obviate further capital expenditure on the part of the
Companies.

Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1723,
March 6, 1936, gives 1nformation with
rogard to negotiations between the American
o11 interests and the Japanese Government,
chiefly on the subject of the storage
requirement of the petroleum law. In view
of Tokyo's telegram No. 78, March 23,
7 p.m., reporting the manner in which the
Japanese Government received the subsequent
news of negotiations between the oil
interests and representatives of Japanese
capital, this despatch now has smali
interest.
The despatch incidentally reports
that agents from The Hague are attempting
to sell to Japanese companies a process
for the hydrogenation of coal.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,

FILED
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
255
No. 1692, dated February 20, 1936, and to previous corres-
pondence on the subject of the difficulties being experienced
in Japan by the American oil interests under the Petroleum
Industry Law, and to report the following developments in the
situation.
Negotiations.
As was stated in despatoh No. l692, the local representa-
tives of the American and British oil interests called on
MT.
F/FG

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the
Foreign Office, on February 8 and conveyed to him the message
from the head offices of the oil interests, to the effect
that the oil companies were unwilling to depart from their
position as bona fide traders by carrying noncounercial stocks
of oil, in accordance with the Jipanese law and regulations.
Mr. Kurusu later discussed the question with Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Conmerce and Industry, and on February
22 invited Lir. Dickover of the Embassy to his o rice to obtain
1/
the reply to the message oi the oil companies. A memorandum
of the conversation between Mr. Kurusu and r. Dickover
is enclosed. In brief, r. Kurusu steted that, although
the six months' storage provision would have to stand, it
might be possible to place a maximum on the quantity of
oil which would have to be stored, instead of increasing
the quantity as the trade increases. However, in order that
the question of quotas for the second quarter of 1936 might be
settled, and in order that r. Yoshino might be enabled to con-
tinue to refuse applications from Japanese refining companies
for the installation of additional refining capacity, r.
Yoshino recommended that the foreign oil companies submit a
concrete proposel to the Japanese Government, offering to store
six months' stocks of oil under the conditions(1) thet the
maximum be made a half of the imports of 1934 or 1935, (2)
that adequate compensation for the storage of oil be paid
by the Japanese Gover ment, and (3) that sufficient guarantees
of future business be given. The primary idea, as r. Dickover
gathered it, was that r. Kurusu and Lir. Yoshino were most
anxious that the foreign oil companies agree to store the
required

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
required quantity of oil, under any conditions which they
might care to impose, in oruer to clarify the issue, to
permit the Japanese Government to give the seme quotas to the
oil companies for the second quarter f the year, and to
enable the Ministry of Comaerce and Industry to refuse
to grant aoplications for additional refining capacity.
Any conditions which the foreign oil companies might impose
would, of course, be subject to negotiation later.
The oil companies conveyed this message to their prin-
cipals on February 24 (February 22 being an American national
2/
holiday and February 23 a Sunday) (Enclosure No. 2). In the
meantime, however, the head offices of the oil companies
had devised a plan which they cabled to the local orfices
on February 24, for the consideration of the local ofrices
but not for transmission to the Japanese Government. A
3/
copy of the telegram conveying this plan is enclosed. Ac-
cording to this plan, the Janenese Government would erect
the storage facilities and buy the oil for storage and the
foreign oil companies would agree to purchase the storage
equipment and the oil, as such equipment and oil became
necessary for their normal trade. After some discussion
of this plan, the local representatives of the oil interests
replied to the effect that, while the plan had points of
advantage, it seemed to imply too great a loss of prestige
on the part of the Japanese Government and would therefore
probably cause the Government to increase Japanese refining
facilities and to reduce the guarantees which might other-
wise be given to the foreign oil companies. The plan would,
moreover require the Japanese Government to give the same
financing facilities to the Japanese companies, which would be
too expensive a scheme to consider. A copy of the telegram
ent

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
sent by the local representative of the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company to the New York head office on this subject
4/ is enclosed.
Liquefaction of Coal.
Two representatives of the International Hydrogenation
Process of The Hague, vetnerlands, have arrived in Japan to
conduot negotiations for the sale of the so-called "I.G."
coal liquefaction process to the Japanese. It will be remem-
bered that this process, which, although German in origin,
is in fact owned principally by British and American oil
and chemical interests, was offered to the Japenese Govern-
ment in return for e reversion to the Five-roint program
evolved last April after protracted negotiations between the
special representatives of the oil interests end representa-
tives of the Japanese Government. After discussing the situation
vith the local representatives of the oil companies, the two
representatives of the coal liquefaction process company have
decided to confine their conversations on the coal hydro-
genation question to technical discussions with the Ministry
of the avy and the Mitsui interests, with a view to con-
vincing them that the process which they wish to trade to
the Japanese Government is the best on the market. In the
meantime, the discussions on the question of the storage
of oil will continue, and it may be possible that later the
hydrogenation process can be used as a means of coming to
terms.
The

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
The newspapers report that the Mitsui interests
are also negotiating for the Fischer process for the
hydrogenation of coal.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatoh No.
1705 of March 5, 1936 from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
MEMORANDUM
Conversation.
February 22, 1936.
Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of
Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office.
Mr. Dickover.
Mr. Kurusu asked ir. Dickover to call at 1l:45 this
morning. He stated that Mr. Yoshino, the Vice Minister for
Commerce and Industry, had conferred with him that morning
on the oil question, and that Yoshino's reply to the latest
representations of the foreign oil interests was as follows:
1. That it would be impossible to change the law and
regulations to conform with the wishes of the foreign oil
interests and that, consequently, the six months' storage
provision would have to stand.
2. The plan of placing a maximum limit on the amount
of the oil storage, instead of increasing it each year as
the trade grew, might be feasible, but Yoshino himself could
not himself offer to propose this change in the regulations.
The proposition must come from the foreign side, after which
Yoshino can discuss the matter with the military and others
interested.
3. The Ministry of Commerce and Industry must soon
consider the quotas for the quarter beginning April 1, 1936,
and if the foreign oil companies definitely and finally
refuse to store six months' stocks of oil, the Ministry will
be compelled to reduce their quotas.
4. Some of the.Japanese oil companies, notably Mitsubishi
and Nippon Sekiyu, have applied to the Ministry of Commeroe
and Industry for permission to erect new refineries, probably
having in mind an eventual refusal of the foreign oil com-
panies to comply with the law in regard to stock-holding
and subsequent reduction of their quotas. Yoshino is holding
up these applications, on the ground that the agreement
made with the oil companies last April gave to the domestio
oil companies only enough of the natural inorease in demand
to take up the existing idle refining capacity. If the
foreign oil companies should definitely refuse to store six
months' stocks of oil, Yoshino would be compelled to grant
the applications for new refineries. (Kurusu added that "this
would be bad for everybody" but he did not state whether or not
this was his or Yoshino's opinion, nor in what respeots it
would be bad for everybody)
5.

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
5. Yoshino therefore recommends that the foreign oil
interests present a concrete proposal, offering to put in
six months' stooks under the following or somewhat similar
oonditions:
(a) That six months of, say, 1934 or 1935, be taken
as the maximum oil storage obligation.
(b) That adequate compensation for the oil storage
be paid by the Japanese Goverrment.
(c) That sufficient guarantees of future business be
given.
Mr. Dickover here asked what Mr. Yoshino meant by "ade-
quate compensation". ir. Kurusu said that he meant that com-
pensation sufficient to pay off the cost of the storage faci-
lities in a reasonable time would be paid, so that the com-
panies would then have their tanks free of cost to them. "Let
the companies ask for what they would consider the proper
compensation". Mr. Dickover then asked if the guarantees of
business would be for a lengthy period, say twenty years.
Mr. Kurusu said that that might be done, but anyway, "Let
the companies make the proposition".
Mr. Kurusu proceeded to state that, if Yoshino has some
concrete proposal, he can continue the present quotas, at least
until June 30, 1936, and can refuse the pending applications of
the Japanese oil companies for additional refining capacity,
on the ground that the foreign oil interests have not refused
to store six months' stocks of oil, but on the contrary are
willing to store the oil under certain conditions. Moreover,
the foreign oil interests' objection to the establishment
of a precedent of storing six months' stocks of oil will be
overcome by the fact that they will be storing a fixed quentity,
in gallons, not six months' of the previous year's imports.
Mr. Dickover said that he would convey this information
to the local representatives of the oil companies, but as today
is an American national holiday and will be followed by a
Sunday, he doubted that it would be feasible to cable the
information to the United States or Englend before Monday.
However, he said, he would see Mr. Meyer that evening and would
give him the information at that time, and that r. Meyer
would convey it to r. Ely of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company.
Mr. Dickover obtained the impression from the above
interview that the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry
and Mr. Kurusu desired above all to obtain the oil companies'
agreement to the storing of six months' supplies of oil, under
whatever conditions the companies might wish to impose. The
purpose seemed to be to keep the conversations open and to keep
the foreign oil companies in business in Japan under their own
conditions, subject, of course, to revision after negotiation.
E. R. D.
The above information was given to Mr. Meyer, of the Standard
Vacuum Oil Company, at six p.m., February 22, 1936.

Enclosure No. 3 to despatoh No.17e3
of Maroh 5, 1936 from the Embassy at
Tokyo.
Copy of cable from London dated February 24,1936.
Referring your telegram of l8th February we feel very
strongly as the whole issue now is direct between head offices
of Oil Companies and the Japenese Government, it would be very
unwise for you to make definite suggestion even though you em-
phasized that it was merely local. What we want is some clear-
cut proposal from Japanese Government on which we can give
decision here. For your private guidance only, and also to
enable us to have your opinion and opinion of Friends' repre-
sentative, we may be prepared to consider scheme which would be
in fact as advantageous to Japanese as the scheme outlined in
your telegram of l8th February but, being turned round the other
way, would be very much less obnoxious to us from the world wide
point of view. We wish to emphasize that one of the main ob-
jestions to our complying with the Petroleum Law is the pre-
cedent such compliance would set to other Governments. Therefore
we tentatively suggest that ssheme may be devised whereby Govern-
ment themselves financed and owned for the time being extra tankage
and extra stocks necessary to comply with the Law on some modified
quantitative basis as now envisaged and that these tanks to be
erected on or in the neighborhood of Oil Companies' properties
so that we could look after stocks, etc. for the Government and so
that the Government could give us the right to purchase from them
tanks and stocks in whole or part as increasing trade should make
them commercially useful to us. For such right we might be
prepared to pay annual sum equivalent to low rate of interest
on the loan proposition put forward by you, but it would not be
oalled interest and it would not be dangerous as precedent for
the remainder of the world seeing that non-commercial stocks
and storage would be provided by the Government. Needless to say
this proposal would have added merit of providing incentive
to the Japanese Government to give us largest possible quota in
order to reeover their capital as soon as possible. If storage
were built in locality satisfactory to us in connection with
our present business we could take obligation to absorb such
storage before erecting any new facilities of our own and also
to supervise erection if desired by Government. Please dis-
cuss with riends' representative giving us your views on such
proposal but on no account make any suggestion concerning same
to the Government at present. Your telegram 24th February re-
ceived since writing above but we will not reply until we have
received your reply to this telegram.

Enclosure No. 4 to despatoh
No. 1723 of March 6, 1936
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
C O P Y
To: New York Office
From: Standerd-Vacuum Oil Company, Yokohama
February 27, 1936.
Referring to your telegram of February 24th plan sug-
gested in ondon's telegram would be feasible from point
of view of being within letter of the petroleum law but
Friends we and both Embassies are of opinion that it is
impractical as a solution for following reasons: (one)
It would, if accepted, constitute a defeat for the Govern-
ment of such magnitude that only the minimum in guarantees
on future trading could be expected and would most likely
incite Government to accelerate refining (two) Goverrment
will strongly resist any scheme which does not lend itself
to application to Japanese companies as well as to foreign
companies and since Jepanese companies have already accu-
mulated six months stocks the transfer of ownership to
Government of their non-comnercial stocks and tankage
would not only involve unnecessary expense to Gover ment
but also serve to emphasize the submission of Government
to pressure from foreign companies with consequent serious
loss of prestige. Friends we end both Embassies are not
at all confident that Gover ment will consider any form of
Gover ment financing and therefore we ourselves believe that
any plan involving complete Government financing of non-
commercial stocks must in order to receive Government
serious consideration be so designed as to convince Govern-
ment of our earnest desire for practical solution and at seme
time permit minimum loss of face to Government which we believe
plan as per our telegram of February l8th offers. The above
represents our views under conditions obtaining prior to
yesterday's political incident and as character of Government
of immediate future cannot yet be forecasted with certainty
we cannot venture en opinion as to whether or not our
problem will now be intensified. Friends are telegraphing
substantially the same but are inclined to favor offering
London scheme as first step towards effecting compromise
along lines our telegram rebruary l8th but pointing out
attendant risks. LEYER.
GREW.

Notes on Moeting at Foreign Ofrice on
March 1l. 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. Kurusu (Chief of Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Ofrice)
Mr. Yoshino (Vice Minister of Commerce and Industry)
Messrs. Ely (R.S.P. Co.) and Meyer (S.V.O.C.)
Messrs. Ely and Meyer informed Messrs. Kurusu and
Yoshino that their Principals have now telegraphed that,
since receipt of the cable giving Ir. Yoshino's views as
presented by Mr. Kurusu through Mr. Dickover, they have
been giving constant study to those views; that their Prin-
cipals are sympathetic with the difficulties that would be
involved in changing the Law and Regulations to conform
with the wishes of the Foreign Oil Companies; nevertheless
from their own standpoint they are faced with compliance
with similar legislation elsewhere in the event that a
precedent is set in Japan and therefore they cannot depart
from the views which they have previously expressed on that
aspect of the problem. Messrs. Ely and Meyer then set
forth their Principals' ideas as to a method of solving the
problem, stating that the proposal which the two companies
have in mind embodies
(1) A fixed quantitative maximum for stock-hold-
ing
(2) Guarantees and security in regard to future
business on the lines of the Five Point Memo-
randum
(3)An arrangement so that a dangerous precedent
would not be set for other countries.
Amplifying (3) Messrs. Ely and Meyer stated that their
Principals consider that a possible means of meeting the
cost of stock-holding to comply with a fixed quantitative
maximum and overcoming the danger of setting a precedent
for other countries would be the establishment of a new
10001

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
local storage company; such a company to be independently
owned and operated; to be financed preferably by Govern-
ment arrangement through banks or alternatively by publio
money; and to carry suitable Government guarantees. The
loans, bonds or stocks involved in such an arrangement
would be gradually retired by the foreign oil companies
as the non-commercial stocks of oil were reduced, by in-
creasing trade, towards the equivalent of commercial needs;
and the connection of the two foreign companies with the
storage company would be limited to a contractural obli-
gation for the retirement of the loans, bonds or stocks.
Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino listened very attentively
up to this point whereupon Mr. Yoshino interjected a remark
to the effect that a plan such as the storage company men-
tioned would not be possible: such an arrangement could
not be made to take care of the stock-keeping obligations
of the foreign companies only: to which Mr. Kurusu added
"such a company would have no income". Messrs. Ely and
Meyer saw no reason why the arrangement could not be appli-
cable to the Japanese Companies and pointed out that the
income of the storage company would consist of compensa-
tion from the Government, which presumably would be ade-
quate to cover interest and carrying charges plus a fair
return on funds invested. Messrs. Kurusu and Toshino
were apparently unprepared at the moment for a suitable
reply to this assumption and Mr. Yoshino diverted the
talk along lines of his own, stating that he desired to
take this opportunity to speak to the foreign companies
O

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
very confidentially and, with their kind permission, he
would speak in Japanese for interpretation by Mr. Kurusu.
He then launched upon a very lengthy and obviously pre-
pared message, the substance of which as interpreted by
Mr. Kurusu was:
The Commerce Department must consider next quarter's
quota before March 2oth and it also must doal with applica-
tions for additions to refining capacity from Domestic
Companies, notably Mitsubishi and Nippon. If the Foreign
Companies refuse to comply with stock-holding requirements
their April-June quotas will have to be cut. It is true
as interpolated by Messrs. Ely and Meyer that they will
be within the law technically until June 30th, but the
Companies have made no preparation to comply and on and
after July lst they will be law-breakers and, as such,
will be liable to fines and to having their quotas cut in
half. The Commerce Department must know well in advance of
July lst what to expect on the part of Foreign Oil Companies
so as to make appropriate plans. If the Commerce Department
is placed in a position where it seems necessary to approve
of additional refining capacity, the Commerce Department
cannot thence-forward be held to any obligations in regard
to allocating a natural increase 50-50 between refiners and
importers. If the Foreign Companies when receiving cuts in
their quotas are unwilling to sell finished products on a
wholesale basis or if the Foreign Companies elect to with-
draw from Japan nd to refrain from selling both finished
products and crude, the problem may get beyond a commercial
ono

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
one and Japan will have to make certain of oil supplies
from new sources. In such event, of course, progress
will be accelerated in fuel substitutes, etc. In fairness
to the Commeroe Department, however, the Foreign Companies
should make known what they intend to do and Mr. Yoshino
would like Messrs. Ely and Meyer to pass on their request
to London and New York.
Reverting to the storage company proposal, Mr. Yoshino
saw something in the idea, but the Companies would have to
arrange it independently: they should be able to interest
Japanese capital and pay for it by allowing the Japanese
storage company to do part of their trade and obtain its
profit therefrom. If the storage company were a joint
affair, including also the Japanese companies, it should embrace
distribution as well and tlus obtain an adequate income -
there was a great deal to be saved by rationalizing distri-
bution and sales by means of the joint company. The proposal
as advanced by Messrs. Ely and Meyer meant that in effect
the Government would have to finance the stock-holding:
this would necessitate bonds which could not be issued
for that purpose.
After interpreting Mr. Yoshino's remarks as above
Mr. Kurusu reiterated his views concerning the fears of the
Foreign Companies regarding the setting of a dangerous pre-
cedent for other countries. Mr. Kurusu felt that the
Foreign Companies should forget that the stocks desired
by the Government constituted so many months requirements
and should look upon the stock-holding obligation as a
8pecif10

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
specific quantity and, moreover, should not isolate
new expenditure for stock-holding from existing invest-
ment but should rely upon prices to provide a satisfactory
return upon their total investments. He added that in
the last analysis the cost of stock-holding must come
out of the country and he thought the Foreign Companies
ought not to mind creating storage facilities somewhat
ahead of the time when demand would necessitate the same
facilities. Messrs. Ely and Meyer countered that since
in the last analysis the cost of carrying non-commercial
stocks falls on the country, it is proper that the cost
should be defrayed from general funds rather than imposed
upon the consumers only; also that the Foreign Companies:
attitude in regard tofinancing the carrying of non-commer-
cial stocks even though only temporarily, is founded on
the fact that such a precedent would immediately result
in other countries adopting similar legislation. In this
connection Messrs. Ely and Meyer again contrasted the posi-
tion of the Foreign Companies to that of the Domestic Com-
panies whose limited field of operation entails no risk of
this nature.
Mr. Kurusu also expressed the view that the local
storage company proposition would put unnecessary burdens
upon the Government inasmuch as Domestic Companies have al-
ready complied with the law under the present scale of com-
pensation and he again inferred that other Japanese business
men could be found who would serve similarly in the event
of Foreign Companies relinquishing a part of their quotas.

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Mr. Kurusu asked that the Foreign Companies bear in
mind the potential benefits to establ shed companies
incidental to the closing of the Japan market to new-
comers.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer developed at some length the
feasibility of the storage company proposition and re-
quested that, since the proposal represents the outcome
of long and careful study on the part of their Principals,
Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino should give the proposition
more thought and that whenever convenient to them another
discussion could be had. Mir. Kurusu in accepting a note
outlining the above-mentioned proposal of the companies
undertook to let Messrs. Ely and Meyer know if he and Mr.
Yoshino thought another discussion on the subject would
be worth while.
Mr. Kurusu when bidding Messrs. Ely and Meyer goodbye
made a casual and vague reference to recent reports from
Japan's naval attaches which implied that certain coercive
measures are being exercised in connection with Japan's oil
difficulties. Mr. Kurusu also expressed the hope that no-
thing in the nature of an economic war will develop from
the present situation.
Mr. Yoshino repeated that the Commerce Department will
expect to have adequate notice of the Foroign Companies' in-
tentions commencing July lst.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer as they were departing mentioned
to Mr. Yoshino that word has been received fpom Korea
that under no circumstances will foreign companies receive
any of the natural increase in consumption in 1936, that
Domestie

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.97)

MEMORANDUM
Maroh 13, 1936.
Conversation: Mr. Kurusu, of the Foreign Office.
Mr. Macrae, of the British Embassy.
Mr. Dickover.
Mr. Macrae and r. Dickover called upon r. Kurusu at
1l:30 this morning by appointment. Lir. Dickover opened
the conversation by stating that the American end British
oil interests had made a definite proposal to the Japanese
Gover ment which was designed to meet the needs of both
parties, and that it was hoped that from the negotiations
which would presumably be conducted in regerd to the com-
panies' plan, there would flow a solution of the problem
which wouldrove mutually satisfectory to all concerned.
Mr. Kurusu said that r. Yoshino, the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry, was very pessimistic of the
outcome of the conversations. ne said that ir. Yoshino
was now studying the companies' proposal and consulting
with his fellow officials in the Linistry in en effort
to ascertain if it were worth while to continue the dis-
cussions with the oil companies.
Mr. Macrae said that he thought that the plan of the
oil companies had merit and deserved careful consideration
by the Jepanese Government. r. Kurusu said that the great
trouble is that the time is so short. He seid that a definite
decision must be made by Maroh 20, when it would be necessary
to set the quotas for the next quarter and to prepare for the
quotas after July 1. He said that the oil companies, if
they did not store six months' stocks of oil, would not
become

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
become law-breakers until July 1, but that if their
quotas were cut in half at that time, the Japanese
industry would not be in a position to take up the
slack so quickly. Therefore preparations must be
begun soon, and licenses to increase refining e uip-
ment would have to be issued.
There ensued a long discussion of the companies'
suggestion that an independent storage comnany he formed
to carry the non-commercial portion of the stocks of oil.
Mr. Kurusu said that he and Yoshino had looked into the
matter and found that the scheme was not feasible, because
the compensation to be paid by the Government was in-
sufficient to meke it an attractive commercial propo-
sition, while the Government could not finence the non-
commercial stocks of the foreign companies without doing
the same for the Japanese 'companies. He said that the
foreign compenies, however, could do whet the Japenese
companies had done and borrow money from the banks to
finance the extra stocks.
Mr. Dickover and Mr. Macrae pointed out that that
was exactly what the foreign oil companies did not wish
to do, as to arrange their own finencing would be to con-
sent to the carrying by themselves of six months' stocks -
a precedent which they did not wish to esteblish. Mr.
Macrae said that the companies hed real grounds for their
fears about a precedent, as he had just heard thet the
Portuguese Government was now planning a petroleum law
modeled after the Japanese lew.
Mr.Kurusu said that he had exemined the storege
company scheme from all angles, but that every time he
ran

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
ran into en impasse. The scheme was after all a sort of
camouflage, whereby the companies would in reality be con-
senting to the storage of six months' stocks of oil but
would not a pear to be storing the oil. However, in order
to avoid the anpearance of storing six months' supplies of
oil, they wanted the oil to be held by an independent com-
pany. That would mean either financing by the Government,
by the oil companies through the banks, or by private capital.
The Government would not finance the scheme, because to do
so would mean that the Government would in reality be buying
its stocks of oil - a contingency which the Government had
tried to avoid. The oil companies would not finence the
scheme, even through Jenanese banks, because to do so would
create an undesirable precedent. rrivate cepital would not
finance the scheme, because it could not be made remunera-
tive. Therefore if the oil companies insisted upon the
formation of an independent storage company to carry the
oil before they would consent to carry even a fixed quantita-
tive maximum, the situation was at a deadlock. Moreover,
the carrying of the oil stocks by an independent company
would reguire alteration of the regulations.
Mr. Dickover pointed out that he did not understand
that the formation of the storage company was essential to
the companies' consent to the carrying of a fixed quantitative
maximum of stocks. In the memorandum given to Messrs.
Kurusu and Yoshino, it was mentioned as a possible solution.
Mr. Macrae said that that was also his understanding.
Mr. Dickover then said that the proposition of having
private capital finance the storage soheme was perhaps not
entirely

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
entirely hopeless. He said that it might be possible
that the oil companies could pay something toward the
carrying charges of the oil. He said that he had not
discussed the idea with the oil compenies, but as the oil
was to be stored by the independent company subject to
the call of the oil companies, they might be willing to
pey something for that service, calling the payment, say,
a charge for an option on the oil, or a renting or hiring
fee. Mr. Kurusu asked if r. Dickover meant that the oil
compenies should pay storage charges on the oil, as shippers
ordinarily pay warehouse companies for storage of their
goods. Mr. ickover exnlained that he did not mean storage
charges, as the oil companies vould not in fact be the
owners of the oil stored and could not very well pay
storage charges on something which they did not possess.
The payment which they might make would be rather in the
nature of a fee for holding the oil subject to the com-
panies' call. Mr.Kurusu egreed that there might be some-
thing in the idea.
Mr. Dickover also suid that there was another angle
to the situation. The oil companies' local Japanese agents,
scattered over the country, would lose heavily should the
oil companies withdraw from Japan or have their sales
quotas cut in half. Therefore, in order to keep their
business going, they might be willing to organize the
storage company, even though it would mean a rather
small return on the money invested. They could borrow
the money from the banks to finance the scheme. Mr.
Kurusu said that there might be something in that idea
also,

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
also, and asked Mr. Macrae and r. Dickover to discuss
the two ideas with the representatives of the oil companies.
Mr. Macrae asked if there was anything which the Em-
bassies could do. .r. Kurusu saia that the case would
have to wait until he had ascertained the opinion of the
ainistry of ommerce and industry. ne said that he would
inform the mbassies as soon as he heard from Mr. Yoshino.

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
The proposal the two companies have in mind embodies:
(a) A fixed quantitative maximum for stock-holding.
(b) Guarantees and security in regard to future busi-
ness on the lines of the ive Point Memorandum
(c) An arrangement so that a dangerous precedent would
not be set for other countries.
A possible means of meeting the cost of stock-holding
to comply with a fixed quentitative maximum and over-
coming the uanger of setting a precedent for other
countries would be the establishment of a new local
Storage Company.
Such a compiny would be indenendently owned and
opereted.
It would be financed preferably by Governnent arrange-
ment througn banks, or alternatively by public money,
and would carry quitable Government guarantees. The
loans, bonds or stocks involved in such an arrange-
ment would be gradually retired by the foreign oil
companies as the excess stocks of oil were reduced,
by increasing trade, towards the eguivalent of com-
merciel needs; and the connection of the foreign
oil compenies with the Storage Company would be
limited to a contractural obligation for the re-
tirement of the loans, bonds or stocks.
In making this suggestion and asking that, until it
has been further exnlored and developed, the Government
will continue to suspend the full operation of the pre-
sent Reguletions, the Directors of the two Companies
wish to emphesize that they appreciate fully the diffi-
culties that would be involved in changing the Lew and
Regulations to meet the wishes of the foreign Oil Com-
panies: nevertheless the Companies ere faced with com-
pliance with similar legislation elsewnere if the pre-
cedent is established in Japan and therefore they cannot
depart from the views they have previously expressed on
that aspect of the problem and have put forward the above
proposal which they hope will serve as a satisfectory
solution.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apr1l 23, 1936.
Tokyo's 1757 of April 1 recounts develop-
ments in the situation created by the oil
law in Japan.
The Japanese authorities have refused
to reconsider the oil storing provisions of
the law. The foreign oil companies have
refused to comply with the storage require-
ments because of the precedent which such
compliance would create. Consequently the
foreign oil companies are preparing to
consider some kind of arrangement with
the Mitsui interests, ranging from a simple
storage company organized with Mitsui
capital, to an outright sale of the entire
importing and distributing business of
the Standard-Vacuum and Rising Sun companies.
The Japanese authorities are obviously
pleased by this development, and have offered
to take no action adverse to the interests of
the foreign companies until at least July 1.
The British Ambassador and Mr. Grew have
agreed to withhold making representations
for the time being.
You may wish to glance through the
memorandum of conversation between officials
of Mitsui and of the foreign oil companies
WT (enclosure No. 3).
WTT/DLY
