I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
268
No. 1747
dated March 20, 1936, and to previous corres-
pondence on the subject of the difficulties being ex-
perienced in Japan by the American and British oil in-
FILED
terests under the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report
the following developments in the situation.
1936
As the Department is aware, the Japanese authorities
have remained firm in their determination to adhere to
the provision in the Petroleum Industry Law and its en-
forcing regulations which requires oil importers and
refiners to carry in stock six months' supplies of oil,
while
11

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
while the Standard and Shell interests as firmly re-
fused to store this amount of oil, because of the
undesirable precedent which it would create. Conse-
quently the local representatives of the oil interests
and the Japanese authorities were exploring the possibi-
lities of organizing a separate storage company, with
Japanese governmental capital, private capital, or the
capital of the present Japanese agents of the foreign
oil companies, to carry the non-commercial stocks of
oil for the Standard and Shell interests, when the head
offices of the two oil interests in New York and London
suddenly altered their position and decided to utilize
the Mitsui interests in some as yet undefined way as
a medium through which to avoid the necessity of carry-
ing the non-commercial stocks. Instructions to approach
the authorities and the Mitsui interests with the proposi-
tion were telegraphed to the local representatives on
March 17. A copy of the cablegram received by the local
representative of the Standard-Vacuum interests on the
1/
subject is enclosed.
It will be observed that the instructions as received
by the local representatives are somewhat vague, the only
clue to the plans in the minds of the directors of the oil
interests being contained in the phrase:
"We cannot at this stage suggest nature
of deal which may be done with Mitsui but the
two companies will be prepared to go into
the whole question of oil business in the
hope of finding a solution of present impasse.
This would appear to imply that, having given up hope of
inducing

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
inducing the Japanese Government to alter the storage
regulations, the oil interests are prepared to consider
any form of arrangement with the Mitsui interests, rang-
ing from a simple storage compuny organized with Mitsui
capital, to an outright sale of the entire importing
and distributing business of the two companies. The
intentions of the head offices not being clear, the
local represent tives telegraphed for further instruc-
tions, from which it developed that the head offices did
not have in mind a complete merger of the two oil com-
panies with the Mitsui interests, but instead sone sort
of contractual rel tionship which would preserve the in-
dividual identity of the three parties.
On March 23, 1936, the local representatives of the
oil interests called on Nr. Kurusu, of the Foreign Office,
and briefly outlined the plans of their companies to him
and to Mr. Yoshino, the Vice Minister for Commerce and
Industry, who was present. A memoranaum of the conversa-
2/
tion is enclosed. The two officials were obviously pleased
with the plan of making some arrangement with Japanese
capital, and Mr. Yoshino volunteered to continue to with-
hold approval of the pending applications for new refineries
in Japan and to grant the foreign oil interests the same
quotas for the second quarter of the year as had been
given for the first quarter. He could not, however, offi-
cially state that he would suspend the regulations after
July 1, 1936 (when the foreign oil companies are supposed,
under

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
under the law, to have six months' stocks of oil in
storage) but he said that he would endeavor to meet
the wishes of the foreign oil com anies in tnis respect.
This latter roint was brought up b: the local representa-
tives of the oil comianies because, even should a satis-
factory arrangement be concluded with the itsui interests
before July 1, 1936, it would obviously be physically
impossible to erect the neces ary storage facilities by
that time.
In the meantime both this Embassy and the British
E bassy in Tokyo had received authorization to support
the proposal of the foreign oil inter sts by making re-
presentations to the Japanese Foreign Office, with a
view to obtaining suspension of the oil storage regula-
tions until the proposed negotiations with the Mitsui
interests have been concluded. As, however, the plans of
the foreign oil interests were very well received by the
Japanese authorities, who voluntarily offered to take
no action adverse to the interests of the foreign oil
companies at least until after July 1, the British Am-
bassador and I agreed that it would be best to withhold
our representations for the time being, reserving our
ammunition for use later should it become necessary or
desirable to ask for suspension of the regulations after
July 1, 1936. The local representatives of the oil in-
terests concurred in this opinion. They felt that diplo-
matic representations, if made immediately after their
interview with the Japanese officials, might imply a
lack of confidence by the oil companies in the good faith
of

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
of the officials with whom they had discussed their
plans and therefore might serve to alienate the support
of the officials, instead of enlistin such support.
On March 26, 1936, the local representatives of the
oil interests called on Mr. anjo, anacinc Director of
the Mitsui Gomei Kaisha, and r. Tajima, Vice Fresident
of the itsui Bussan Kaisha, and presented the proposal
of their head offices. A memorandum of the conversation
3/
is enclosed. The Mitsui representatives appeared to be
genuinely interested in the proposal, but expressed a
desire that the negotiations between authorized representa-
tives of both sides take place in Tokyo rather than in
New York. They stated,however, that they would discuss
the proposal with Ir. Mukai, Managing Director of the
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha (the trading department of the Mitsui
interests) and wouid later inform the local representatives
of the oil interests of their considered opinion.
At the time of writing no reply has been received by
the oil companies from thc Mitsui interests.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew.

COPY OF CABLE FROM NEW YORK,dated March 19, 1936.
Re your telegram l6th Merch after full discussion with
Friends we have come to the conclusion that any ar-
rangement whoreby an association of dealers would be
used for the rurpose of stock-keepint law would be ob-
jectionable for the following reasons:-
Firstly In all probability it would not be favored
by Government.
Secondly It would turn comparatively weak body of
men into strong united unit which under wrong
leadership could be extremely harmful to our business.
Thirdly As Standard Vacuum Oil Company in any case
have close associations with Mitsui, natural thing
would be for them to amplify this association rather
than turn to other Japanese nationals.
Therefore it seems to us that Mitsui may offer medium
through which something could be done and we agree to
develop these ideas if you and Friends' representative
concur.
In such case we suggest you have informal conversation
with Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino in conjunction with your
Imbassies (and) suggest they continue to suspend operations
of present regulation until the whole matter can be studied
in New York between an authorized representative of Mitsui
and representatives of our two Companies to determine
whether

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
whether mutual satisfactory arrangements can be made.
If you find that Government are prepared to co-operate
in such programme, you should then take up with Mitsui
in order that they would ppoint representative and
arrange date of meeting.
We quite understand Government is naturally reluctant
to alter legislation, but on the other hand Companies,
confronted as they are now almost daily with demands
from other Governments for stock-keeping, cannot possi-
bly create precedent by conforming.
We cannot at this stage suggest nature of deal which
may be done with Mitsui but the two Companies will be
prepared to go into the vhole question of oil business
in the hope of finding a solution of present impasse.
You and Friends' representative should consider whether
it would be preferable to approach Mitsui first in order
to obtain their agreement and possibly their assistance
with Government, after which you could have informal
talks with Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino in conjunction with
Embassies, suggesting that they continue to suspend opera-
tion of present regulations until the whole matter can be
studied in New York. However we give you full discretion
as to approaching Government officials first or Mitsui
first, but we wish to emphasize that from the very be-
ginning you should make clear the subsequent proposed moet-
ing should take place in New York as this offers only
practical place for early negotiations considering parties
interested.

Notes on conversation at Foreign Office
on March 23, 1936
PRESENT:
Mr. Kurusu (Chief of Bureau of Cormercial Af-
fairs, Foreign Office)
Mr. Yoshino (Vice Minister of Commerce & In-
dustry)
Messrs. Ely (R.S.P. Co.) and Meyer (S.V.O.C.)
Messrs. Meyer and Ely opened the conversation by
referring to their last meeting with Messrs. Kurusu and
Yoshino when the proposal of an independent Storage
Company had been discussed. Since then, they knew, r.
Kurusu had had a talk with Mr. Dickover of the U.S.
Embassy and Mr. Macrae of the British Embassy, in the
course of which certain suggestions had been made towards
the further development of the proposal. These suggestions
had been discussed by cable with London and New York who
had, however, for various reasons come to the conclusion
that probably the best medium through which something
might be accomrlished was a strong established concern
like Mitsuis, with whom for some time Mr. Meyer's Company
had had business connections.
Mr. Yoshino, ( speaking in Japanese) interpolated that
he also had been examining carefully the independent
Storage Company proposal which had been discussed at a
recent inter-Departmental meetin6. Moreover he had thought
of Mitsuis in that connection. The position as he saw it
was that, in regard to Heavy Oil, Mitsuis were a lready
licensed importers: and, if they assumed the stock-keeping
obligations of the two foreign companies, from the official
standpoint it  as only a question of increasing Mitsuis'
quota, the foreign Companies making some private arrange-
ment with Mitsuis as to ow that quota should be distributed.
Gasoline, however, raised quite a different problem, as he
understood that Mitsuis were not licensed to import and,
in fact, did not exist officially.
Messrs. Mayer and Ely confirmed that this was so, and
went on to say that their Principals appreciated the various
difficulties involved in the situation. In their last
cables London and New York had said that they quite under-
stood that the Government was reluctant to alter the Law
but that, on their own side, matters had reached a point
where they were being confronted almost daily with stock-
keeping demands from other countries and they could not
possibly create a precedent in that respect: they felt,
however, that a solution of this impasse might be found
in an arrangement with Mitmuis. New York and London could
not at the moment suggest the nature of the deal which
might

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
might be made, and they wished Messrs. Ely and Meyer
first to put the suggestion informally ber re Messrs.
Yoshino and Kurusu; if the latter agreed and the Govern-
ment was prepared to co-operate, London and New York were
willing to go into the whole situation with Mitsuis and
see what arrangement could be evolved.
Messrs. Yoshino and Kurusu at once signified approval,
Mr. Yoshino saying that, pending developments, he would
continue to withhold approval of the applications that had
been made for increased refining facilities, and that the
two companies' trade quotas for April/June would not be
reduced. Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino then asked if Mitsuis
had been approached, in reply to which Meesrs. Meyer and
Ely replied in the negative explaining that it vas their
Principals' idea first to obtain the understanding of the
Government and then to approach Mitsuis with a view to ar-
ranging as soon as possible a conference in New York.
After conferring together for some time Messrs. Kurusu
and Yoshino embarked upon a long discussion as to the advi-
ability of the two foreign companies coming to any arrange-
ment with any individual Japanese concern. It would, they
said, be a very big undertaking and Messrs. Ely and Meyer
must be aware of the present tendency in certain quarters
to pillory "big business". Besides Mitsuis there were
other Japanese concerns which might be interested - for
example Sumitomo, Okuras and, in particular, the Aikawa
group. The latter Mir. Yoshino knew well through Baron
Ito who was a personal friend: they controlled the Nippon
Sangyo, which had some petroleum production in the Empire
but no sales organization and consequently might be a suit-
able partner for a working arrangement with the two foreign
companies. Another possible arrangement would be for the
two companies to consider making separate deals with dif-
ferent Japanese interests. Again, Mitsuis, were big people
but they might not wish nor be able to avoid taking into
consideration some of the "smaller fellows" who also were
inter sted in the petroleum industry. There was, too,
another aspect of the situation: the two foreign companies
were working closely together in regard to an arrangement with
a Japanese concern in connection with stock-holding and
did not this point to further co-operation and rationaliza-
tion in respect to distribution and sales organization? As
Messrs. Ely and Meyer knew, Mr. Yoshino himself felt that a
great deal of economy could be effected in that direction
and in connection with the Gasoline price question the
Government was constantly hearing a lot of criticism of the
duplication and waste involved in the present system of
sales and distribution.
However, Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino did not press these
points and showed very patently their satisfaction with the
proposal that Mitsuis should be approached: they confirmed
that the Government would co-operate, Mr. Yoshino suggesting
that he might be able to assist by approaching Mitsuis, who
undoubtedly would eventually consult his Department. It
was finally agreed, however, that in view of the S.V.O.C.'s
c1ose

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
close connection with Mitsuis it would be more becoming
for the first move to be made by the two Companies direct,
and Messrs. Meyer and Ely undertook to see Mitsuis at
the first opportunity with a view to arranging a conference
in Ne: York as soon as ossible. Messrs. Yoshino and
Kurusu counselled them to be carcful in their approach
and not to work through  de artment but to go straight
to Mr. Tajima, if not to r. Ikeda himself.
In conclusion Messrs. Ely and Meyer said thut in
the circumstances they anticiiated that Mr. Yoshino would
be able to arrange to suspend the operation of the present
Regulations after July 1st. Mr. Yoshino dij not see how
he could, as the Law was in force and ther Was public
opinion to be considered, as well as the Diet: he suggested
that the fine entailed by a br ach of the Law was very small.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer pointed out that, if negotiations to-
wards a solution of the problem were in progress it would
be comparatively easy for the Government to meet the Diet
and satisfy public opinion. Mir. Yoshino agreed, but in his
official capacity he could give no undertaking in reply to
the Companies' request: unofficially, however, he was
even now considering ways and means" provided negotiations
with Mitsuis were begun quickly and progressed satisfactorily.

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3.to despatch
No. 1757 of Cwul 1,1936 from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation at Mitsui Gomei Kaisha
on March 26. 1936
PRESENT:
Mr. K. Nanjo Managing Director, Mitsui Gomei Kaisha
Mr. S. Tajima Vice President, Litsui Bussan Kaisha
Mr. Ely
(R.S.P. Co.)
Mr. Meyer
(S.v.O. Co.)
Messrs. Meyer and Ely explained that notwithstanding
the facts that Mr. Nanjo has advanced from the Presidency
of the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha and is now Managing Director of
the Mitsui Gomei Kaisha and that the matter which the S.V.O.
Co. and R.S.P. Co. have to discuss would ordinarily be
broached with Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, they had come first to
Mr. Nanjo in this instance as Mr. Parker when here last
year met Mr. Nanjo and in his latest telegram from New York
on the matter concerned had made particular mention of r.
Nanjo. Mr. Nanjo remarked that he had asked Mr. Tajima of
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha to be present and that he regretted
that Mr. Mukai, Managing Director of Mitsui Bussan Kaisha,
was prevented from being present by the funeral of his
brother-in-law Mr. Yamamoto.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely went on to say that as Messrs.
Nanjo and Tajima know the two foreign companies have been
engaged in discussions with the Government for more than
a year in an endeavor to find a solution of their oil pro-
blem: that considerable progress has been made, the Govern-
ment having modified its attitude on several important points
and the Companies also having done their share toward ar-
riving at something mutually satisfactory; that there re-
mains, nevertheless, a serious difficulty to surmount, namely
the precedent of carrying non-commercial stocks. Amplifying
the latter Messrs. Meyer and Ely explained to Messrs. Nanjo
and Tajima that companies with worldwide organizations such
as Shell and Standard must take into consideration the
danger of establishing precedents in any country which, if
generally copied, would seriously cripple the resources of
the companies and emphasized that the two companies concerned
are almost daily confronted with demands by other Governments
for the carrying of non-commercial stocks.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely further stated that in an attempt
to overcome this difficulty of "precedent" various plans had
been brought forward and considered but finally the Directors
of the two companies had arrived at the conclusion that
they should avoid anything in the nature of a purely artifi-
cial set-up and should use as a medium some strong established
Japanese company; that accordingly the two companies thought
first of Mitsuis, especially in consequence of the S.V.O. Co.'s
long and pleasant association with them.
Messrs.

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Messrs. Meyer and Ely made it clear that their
Principals have not yet arrived at a concrete plan and
that it is the idea of the Directors of the two companies
that whatever course is finally taken should be worked
out by the three interested parties together in conference.
Continuing on this point Messrs. Meyer and Ely stressed
the necessity of quick action and the consequent strong
feeling on the part of the Directors that best results
can be accomplished through Mitsuis sending a fully quali-
fied representative witn the necessary authority to New
York, where the whole oil question can be thoroughly gone
into with a view to effecting some deal that is mutually
satisfactory.
Mr. Tajima manifested doubt as to the practicability
of holding such a conference in New York, mentioning that
difficulties might arise in trying to settle matters at
such a distance from the Commerce Department. Messrs. Meyer
and Ely replied that their Directors anticipate that any
representative chosen by Mitsui will confer fully with
the Commerce Department, and any other Government Departments
that should be consulted, prior to his departure. Mr.
Tajima thought that no Mitsui director could et away on
short notice and feared that five weeks might elapse before
a representative could reach New York. Messrs. Meyer and
Ely countered that equal if not greater delay would also be
involved should Directors of the two Oil Companies come to
Japan; that moreover New York is the more central of the
cities involved and a conference there would entail a
long journey for only one party.
Mr. Nanjo suggested that the Directors determine some
plan and cable it to Japan for discussion here. Messrs.
Meyer and Ely endeavored to show that such a course would
be impractical for the reason that there are so many aspects
of the problem and possible propositions which warrant joint
study by all the Principals before anything concrete can be
undertaken.
Mr. Nanjo suggested that a joint company would serve
the purpose. Mr. Tajima at once pointed out that, what-
ever other arrangements are ma e, the stock-holding must
be performed by Japanese capital as otherwise the foreign
companies will not escape the precedent which they are
seeking means to overcome. Messrs. Meyer and Ely agreed that
Japanese capital must appear in that capacity. ir. Tajima
said that in his opinion there is a wide range of possibili-
ties open to the three parties - at one extreme is simply a
storage company and at the other a complete merger. He
wished to give the matter more thought and to talk with Mr.
Mukai. He t ought perhaps he would be able to have a talk
with Mr. Mukai tomorrow. He agreed to let Messrs. Meyer
and Ely know their considered opinion.
Early in the discussion Messrs. Meyer and Ely informed
Mossrs. Nanjo and Tajima that the idea of using Mitsui as
a medium has been discussed with Messrs. Kurusu and Yoshino
who

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
who are favorably disposed to such co-operation. Also,
in reply to the question as to whether in the course
of negotiations with the Companies, the Government had
been entirely adamant in regard to the six months' stock-
holding stipulations of the Law, Messrs. Meyer and Ely
said that, although they had been told nothing officially,
they hau gathered that the lovernment had in mind fixing
a quantitative maximum limit which would obviate the in-
creasing obligation involved in the present regulations.

The Beeretary ef State refers to the Department's

U. S. AND BRITISH OIL FIRMS MAY WITHDRAW FROM JAP M..RKET
'Oil Conservation' Law Seen Forcing the Issue
PROTESTS LODGED
By Walter Fitzmaurice
Washington, April 1, (Us).- A climax within two weeks w.s loreshadowed
today in the tense British-american diplom tic maneuvers to plug threat-
ened sapping of their  il  eserves by the Japanese wer machine.
The State Depurtment disclos the  irota overnment was
setting the stage for a s owdown ub it ..pril l) by initiatin: enforce-
ment of Japan's hi hly controversal "oil conservation" law.
Though an immense commercial m:rket is at stake, the  mer-
ican and British oil titans, Jocony-Vacu un and oyal utch shell, have
indicated they will abandon thcir Japanese investment sooner th n submit
to the meusure.
FOR FUEL RESERVES.
In effect, it would drain millions of tons of crude from
American Continental and British .siatic fields to provide fuel reserves
for the Japanese battle fleet and her mechanized troops in hina and
Manchukuo.
Ostensibly there is no discrimin tion between domestic and
forein connanies d in business in Japan.
The law reqlires e ry oii distributor to store the equiva-
lent of 50 per cent or its un ual Japanese sules of crude oil, usoline
and iubricants.
stored supp. ies are su ject to purchuse without notice by
the Japanese governnent t a "fair m rket price" to be deternined by the
government.
'The compenies themselves must ereet the storage tanks and
pay the enormous interest an insurance charges. merican storage, based
on 1935 imports, would be apnroximately ci ht million barrels, the
British about four million.
PROTEST "VIOLATION".
Upholders of the Cpen Door in China, the State Department
and the British Foreign Office lodged firm protests aginst Japanese ex-
propriation of a vital natural resource to pursue her violation of the
Open Door principle.
The term "conservation" was declared a misnomer, the Japan-
ese domestic production being negli ible. But the Japanese dmiralty
and the War Office refused to budge.
If the foreign companies withdrew, they pointed cut, a
German dehydration process for c nverting coal into gasoline could be
utilized for the air force. Lhe oil-burning fleet could be converted
back to coal.
Britain, the Japanese pointed out, thought enough of the
process to subsi ize its experimental use by British Imperial Chemicals,
Ino. The method was provi in ermany 50 per cent of her liquid fuel.
Soviet Russia is reported sharing Germany's interest in the
outcome. Moscow is said to be ready to supply any Japanese deficiency
resulting from withdrawal of the American and British companies.

OIL SHOWDOW
WITH JAPAN
DUESOON
A climax within two weeks was
foreshadowed today in the tense
British-American diplomatic ma-
neuvers to plug threatened sap-
ping of oil reserves by the Jap-
anese.
The State Department disclosed
the Hirots government was setting
the stage for a showdown about
April 15 by initiating enforcement
of Japan's highly controversial
"oll conservation" law.
American and British oil titans,
Socony-Vacuum and Royal Dutch
Shell, have indicated they will
abandon their Japanese invest-
ment sooner than submit to the
measure, which would drain mil-
lions of tons of crude from Amer-
ican Continental and British Asi-
atic fields to provide fuel reserves
for the Japanese battle fleet and
her mechanized troops in China
and Manchukuo.
Ostensibly there is no discrim-
ination between domestic and for
eign companies doing business in
Japan.
The law requires every oil dis-
tributor to store the equivalent of
50 per cent of its annual Japanese
sales of crude oil, gasoline and
lubricants.
Stored supplies are subject to
purohase without notice by the
Japanese government at a "fair
market price" to be determined
by the government.

PAR RASTERE SURVEY,
March 12, 1936.
POREION OIL COMPANIES IN JAPAN PACE
IMPASSE
The stalemate between the Japanese Government and the
fereign eil eompanies im Japan continues, with no indieation
yet as to how the difrieulty will be resolved. The poimt of
eemfliet is the provision of the Oil Lew of l9s4 which, in
addition to regulating the import and refining of oil by
liesnse, required all companies operating in Japen to in-
erease their usual storage of petrolem products from a two
er three months' supply to one adequate for six months. The
Japanese companies complied, built the necessary additional
tankage, and inoreased their imports to the desired point.
The attitude of the foreign companies, however, has been
that they do not need this additional storage space to carry
on their business. When they had not met the provisions by
the date originally set, Cotober 1, 1935, the time l1mit was
finally extended to the end of June, l936. Negotiations are
still going on between Japanese officials and foreign companies,
most important of which are the Standard Oil and the Rising
Sun. As yet no move has been reported that would indioate the
wishes of the government are being met.
The situation would seem to be approaching an impasse,
for Japan is vitally in need of the assurance of contimuing
foreign supplies of the mineral whioh is so essential to her
industrial development and national defense, for she can
supply only a small percentage from domestic sources. (See
our Memorandum on Japan's Cil Problem, Deo. 21, 1934) At the
same time, the British and American companies, which have
built up a lucrative business cver a long perlod of years,
will hardly want to relinquish their 50% share of the Japanese
market, even though they are not propared to go to the expense
and risk of providing extra tankage which they feel they do
not need.
Now, as from the first of 1936, the Department of Com-
meroe and Industry has fixed marketing quotas for the various
companies on the basis of their actual sales in 1935, these
quotas to be effective until the end of Yaroh. On the basis
of an estimated 13 1norease in demand, all of the potential
increase in gasoline sales, some 140,C00 kls., hns been
allotted to Japanese companies. It is understood that if the
foreign companies meet the requirements by the end of Larch,
20,00 kls. estimated increase will bo transferred to their
quotas.

stwious ose of assuring a ready sapply in ease of emergeney

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
Many thanks indeed for your letter
894.6363/273
of April 6th, not to mention your previous note on
this question which I genuinely appreciated.
I heartily endorse the views ex-
pressed on pages 3 and 4 of your letter of April 6th
with respect to the policy on publicity in connection
with the oil companies' present difficulties in Japan.
This policy is one which we have also pursued and one
which we deem to be essential so long as the question
is under negotiation. In fact, when Mr. Walden and I
left Japan a year ago at the completion of our negotia-
tions there, at the request of the Japanese Government
both parties agreed that publicity was undesirable and
that we would refrain from issuing any statements to
the press. I am naturally pleased to see that our
views are in such close harmony with yours.

CONFIDENTIAL
TOKYO, April 10th, 1936.
Dear Mr. Meyer,
I am instructed by Mr. Nanjo to inform you that
we regret we cannot entertain the proposal regarding
oil storago made to him by you and Mr. Ely.
We must apologize for the delay in answering and
hope to be able to see you in near future to explain the
main reason how we came to above conclusion.

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No.1800 of May 1, 1936
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation at offioes of Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, Tokyo,
on Apr11 14th. 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. S. Tajima
Vice President, M.B.K.
Mr. T. Mukai
Managing Director
Mr. Ely
R.S.P. Co.
Mr. Meyer
s-v. O.co.
Mr. Mukai arrived alone and opened the conversation by
stating that he regretted that he was unavoidably absent when
Messrs. Ely and Meyer had their talk with Messrs Nanjo and
Tajima and he went on to say that he understood that the two
oil companies had proposed that M.B.K. perform their stock-
holding.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer explained that the problem of
stock-holding had resulted in their Principals approaching
Mitsuis, and in this connection reiterated the statements
made to Messrs/Nanjo and Tajima, but made it clear that
London and New York are prepared to go into the whole question
of the oil business in the hope of finding a solution of the
present impasse.
During the course of the above conversation Mr. Tajime
joined the group and entered into the conversation with the
statement that if the oil companies have in mind utilizing
Mitsui only in a storage or warehousing capacity, Mitsuis
are definitely not interested as they are organized for
trading purposes.
This feature both Mr. Tajima and Mr. Mukai elaborated
upon at some length and gave Messrs. Ely and Meyer an opening
to enquire directly if Mitsuis would be interested in a wider
arrangement involving trading on their part. Both Mr. Tajima
and Mr. Mukai replied in the affirmative adding however that
they must make it clear at the outset that any negotiations
in connection with an arrangement must take place in Tokyo.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer repeated the reasons given to Messrs.
Nanjo and Tajima that make New York the most appropriate place
for holding discussions. Messrs. Tajima and Mukai countered
with the statement that in their opinion Tokyo is the obvious
place for the reason that it would be essential for them
to constantly keep in very close touch with the Government
(not only Commeroe Department but also Army) and moreover the
complexity of Mitsui's organization necessitates a great deal
of inter-departmental discussion. Mr. Mukai emphatically
stated that he would not go and Mr. Tajima quickly added
"neither will I under any ciroumstances".
Messrs.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Mossrs. Ely and Meyer stated that their Principals would
be very much disappointed to learn that such was their atti-
tude, whereupon Mr. Mukai remarked to the effect that even
if it means foregoing a deal with the oil companies Mitsui
cannot agree to negotiating in New York. Mr. Tajima then
stated that in their opinion any deal should be negotiated
in Japanese atmosphere. Messrs. Ely and Meyer reminded him
of the fact that the Principals of both companies with whom
Mitsui would deal in New York are persons familiar with Japa-
nese conditions and customs.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer sounded Messrs. Tajima and Mukai
as to their ideas of an arrangement. Messrs. Tajima and Mukai
asked that the companies refrain from "turning the tables"
on them and stated that since the companies are the experts
in the oil business it is fitting that they should advance
any proposals. Neither Mr. Tajima or Mr. Mukai would consent
to giving consideration to any arrangement until the com-
panies give a clearer indication of the daal which they would
favor.
Although Messrs. Tajima and Mukai steadfastly refused
to suggest any basis for an arrangement, they gave a clue to
their line of thought by pointing out that
(1) if stocks are held by a joint concern the companies
would not be getting rid of the precedent of holding
non-commercial stocks
(2) companies could not retain even reduced quotas in
their own name unless they themselves undertook
corresponding stock-holding obligations.
Messrs. Tajima and Mukai in the course of the conversation
professed inability to understand the attitude of the companies
regarding the danger of establishing a precedent of carrying
non-commercial stocks. They expressed the opinion that the com-
panies have not dealt with the problem in a practical manner
and consider that it would have been business like, if com-
pensation was considered inadequate, to request more and to
deal with each problem in various countries on its own merits.
This line of discussion occasioned Mr. Tajima to expound at
considerable length on what he termed the "philosophical
side" of the pil question, namely the scope for liberality on
the part of those possessing raw materials toward those who are
lacking in this respect. He predicted an increasing tendency
toward redistribution among the "haves" and "have nots" and
inferred that the oil industry clings to an obsolete attitude.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer listened politely and attentively but
reminded Mr. Tajima that the companies have no disposition
to deny oil supplies to Japan, a fact which is efidenced by their
presence in Mitsui's office to endeavor to find ways and means
of continuing to supply the country.
The discussion terminated with Messrs. Ely and Meyer
undertaking to advise their Principals of Mitsui's position as
expressed by Messrs. Tajima and Mukai.

to

of May 1, 1936 from the

Notes on Meeting at Shokosho on April 24th. 1936.
PRESENT1
Mr. Yoshino
Vice Minister of Commerce and Industry
Mr. Ely
R.S.P. Co.
Mr. Meyer
S-V. O.Co.
Mr. Yoshino began by saying that he personally was in an
uncomfortable position in regard to the applications which had
been made by the Japanese refiners for ertension of their faci-
lities. As Messrs. Ely and Meyer knew, he had postponed con-
sideration of those applications until the foreign companies'
negotiations had progressed further but he was being con-
tinually pressed and only last week had been epproached again.
Speaking in confidence, he said also that although he had not
sent in his resignation - the reports appearing in the news-
papers were wrong - he had now had nearly five years as Vice
Minister and had in mind resigning but, especially since Mr.
Kurusu's appointment to Brussels, it had devolved upon him to
clear up the situation that had arisen under the Petroleum Law -
a situation that was very complex because it involved not only
disoussions with foreign companies but also inter-Departmental
negotiations. He hoped therefore that the foreign companies'
problem would be solved quickly. In that connection, contrary
to his own expectation his Department had not been approached
by Mitsuis and he had therefore himself spoken to Mr. Mukai.
Apparently there has been some misunderstanding in regard to
the proposal put forward by the foreign companies.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer replied that, as Mr. Yoshino himself
knew when they approached Mitsuis, they did not propose any
specifio plan but after a rather long delay - which might have
been due in part to the changes which were reported to have been
taking place in Mitsuis internal organization - Mitsuis had re-
plied apparently on the understanding that it had been proposed
that they should act merely as stook holders, which they were
not willing to do: they had said, however, that they were
quite willing to consider some broader arrangement. Messrs.
Ely and Meyer had cabled to their principals in that sense and
were now awaiting replies from London and New York on receipt
of which they would be resuming their discussions with Mitsuis.
Mr. Yoshino said that he rather sensed that Mitsuis were
approaohing the Foreign companies'suggestion very cautiously,
not only from the standpoint of business pure and simple but
also because of the more general features to which Mr. Kurusu
and he had referred in their last conversation with Messrs. Ely
and Meyer. Mitsuis were liable to a good deal of public criticism:
he

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
he felt, for instance, that if Mitsuis made a deal with the
foreign companies and market prices went up the movement might
be associated with Mitsui's entry into the business and Mitsui
blamed. Messrs. Meyer and Ely suggested that, as the result
of the request that had been put forward already by the Japa-
nese Companies, prices surely would be raised substantially
to the proper level in the immediate future and long before
Mitsuis could be blamed? Mr. Yoshimo refused to be drawn,how-
ever, and went on to repeat that Mitsuis undoubtedly were
being very cautious: they might, however, modify their atti-
tude if they were told by the Gover ment that it wished the
arrangement to be made. He hoped that the two companies would
push on with the negotiations.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely assured Mr. Yoshino that they
would be seeing Mitsuis again immediately they received
replies, which they were expecting at any moment, to the
cables they had sent to London and New York as the result
of their last talk with Mitsuis.
The interview terminated with general conversation,
Mr. Yoshino reverting to his pet subject of the Companies'
attitude which he considered anachronistic, towards "con-
trolled economy" and also asking questions concerning the
trend of Crude Oil prices in U.S.A. Messrs. Meyer and Ely had no
special information on that subjeot, but were of the opinion
that, with the more correct balance between the prices of crude
and refined oils which had been brought about by the recent
advance in crude prices, the industry generally was in better
order and the tone of the market distinctly strong.

applied to high compression and aviation gasoline.

low pressure or high pressure is sometimes said to
be the only factor remaining to hinder its wider
commercial use.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
washington.
Sir:
Referring to the Embassy's despatch No. 1800, dated
May 1, 1936, and to previous correspondence in regard to
the difficulties being experienced in Japan by the Ameri-
can and other foreign oil interests under the Petroleum
Industry Law, I have the honor to report the following
FILED
developments in the sttuation.
Negotiations mith the Mitsui Interests.
As was reported in despatch No. 1800, both the Mitsui
interests and the Ministry of Commerce and Industry of the
Japanese Government had asked the Standard and Shell oil
interests to evolve some concrete plan, which could be
F/FG
used as & basis for negotiations, for presentation to
itaui

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Mitsui. On May 14, 1936, the local office of the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company received a cablegram from the New York
1/
headquarters, a copy of which is enclosed, containing the
outline of the plan evolved between New York and London
in regard to co-operation with Mitsui in the Japan market.
This plan contemplated the carrying by the Mitsui interests
of all the non-commercial stocks required by the Government,
for hich the Mitsui interests would receive the entire
compensation paid by the Gov rnment for stock-keeping
as well as some share of the foreign oil companies' business
in Japan. The oil companies would also make a permanent
supply contract with Mitsui, but in return asked that the
present volume quotas granted by the Japanese Government
be changed to percentage quotas. The cablegram proceeded
to point out the advantages of some such arrangement both
to the Japanese Government and to the oil companies, and
implied that unless same arrangement of the sort could
be reached, the companies mould be compelled sooner or
later to withdraw from Japan. An identic telegram mas
received by the Rising Sun Petroleum Company in Japan
from the London headquarters.
The local representatives of the foreign oil interests
decided to present this plan first to Mr. Yoshino, the
Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, in order to as-
sist him should he be embarrassed mith interpellations in
the Diet, and later to discuss the matter with the Mitsui
representatives. On May 16, therefore, the representa-
tives of the oil companies called upon Mr. Yoshino and
gave him a copy of the telegram containing the plan.
In

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
In the conversation which followed, Mr. Yoshino asserted
that the Japanese Government wished the foreign oil
companies to remain in Japan. There was some discussion
of the plan offered by the oil interests, and Mr. Yoshi-
no expressed the opinion that Mitsui's seeming reluctance
to enter into negotiations with the oil interests was
partly policy, in order to obtain the most satisfactory
concessions from the oil interests, and partly real,
due to Mitsui's interest in the Fischer procees for the
liquefaction of coal. Mr. Yoshino also stated that he
wished that the principles, at least, of any arrangement
With Mitsui could be settled before July 1, l936, and
that he would endeavor to induce Mitsui to expedite
2/
matters. A memorandum of the conversation is enclosed.
Later on the same day the local representatives of
the oil interests interviewed Mr. Mukai, a Managing
Director of the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, and handed him a
note containing the substance of the message received
from their principals. Mr. Mukai received the proposal
of the oil interests favorably but did not commit his
company further than to say that he would reply after
he had studied the matter. A memorandum of the con-
3/
versation mith Mr. Mukai is enclosed herewith. At the
time of writing no reply has been received by the local
representatives of the oil interests from the Mitsui
interests.
In a conversation with the First Secretary of the
Imbassy on May 26, 1936, Mr. Tajima, a Director of Mitmui
Bussan Kaisha, said that the oil negotiations with the
foreign

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
foreign oil companies constituted the "hardest nut he
had had to crack" in his many years of experience i th
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha. He said that the prinoipal ob-
stacle lay in the fact that the foreign oil interests
wanted the negotiations to be held in New York. At the
same time, it mas absolutely necessary that the Japanese
Ministries of Commerce and Industry, War and Navy be
consulted in regard to every step of the negotiations.
The First Secretary asked if it were not possible for
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha to formulate a concrete plan which
would fulfill the wishes of the Ministries eoncerned and
to take that to New York as a basis of negotiations. Mr.
Tajima said that that was impossible, because the various
Ministries did not see eye to eye in regard to the mat-
ter and could not agree as to what they wanted - in fact,
they did not know exactly what they wanted. Mr. Tajima
said, however, that, although the problem was a most
difficult one, it was absolutely necessary, both for the
foreign oil companies and for Japan, that it be solved
in a satisfactory manner, and that Mitsui mas working
very hard in an endeavor to find the solution. He added
that he did not think that a solution would be found by
July 1, 1936 (by which time the oil companies are supposed
to possess six months' stocks in Japan), but that he did
not think that the authorities would be hard on the
foreign companies for that reason.
Alleged Plan to Form a Semi-Official Storage Compeny
The ASAHI SHIMBUN of May 22, 1936, stated that a
plan

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
plan is being proposed by the Japanese oil industrialists,
for the establishment of a semi-official oil storage com-
pany for the purpose of carrying the non-commercial stocks
and thus taking the burden of carrying such stocks from
the shoulders of the Japanese oil traders.
The local representative of the Standard interests,
upon being questioned in regard to the above rumor, stated
that he had known nothing of the matter before seeing it
in the newspapers. He presumed, however, that, having
found that the foreign oil interests could neither be per-
suaded nor coerced into carrying six months' stocks of oil,
the Japanese Government was endeavoring to find a way out
of the dilemma by essaying a proposal, allegedly emanating
from the Japanese oil interests, that the excess storage
be carried by a subsidized company rather than by the oil
companies themselves, in order to relieve the domestic com-
panies from the heavy burden. The hesitation of the Mitsui
interests in entering upon negotiations with the foreign
oil interests might possibly be ascribed to the fact that,
with some such plan gradually taking form,it mould not be
necessary for the foreign oil interests to employ Japanese
capital to carry the stocks.
The Search for Oil.
The same article in the ASAHI SHIMBUN states that the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry is planning to increase
the amount of the subsidy given to domestic companies which
undertake test borings for oil. The subsidy allotted for
such

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
such borings at present amounts only to some Yen 200,000
or Yen 300,000 per annum, which is insufricient to en-
courage test wells in many promising fields owmed by
private oil companies in Japan. The Ministry therefore
plans to increase the subsidy for test borings from next
year, and also to subsidize wells which, hile productive,
cannot produce profitably in the present state of the
market.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No. 1860of May 7,1936 from the

Notes on interview at the Department of
Commerce & Industry on May 16, 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. Yoshino - Vice Minister of Commerce & Industry
Mr. Ely
- R.S.P.Co.
Mr. Meyer - S-V.O.Co.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer opened the conversation by
stating that London's and New York's reply had now been
received and in order that Mr. Yoshino might understand
it fully they had brought a copy of the message for his
perusal, but asked that in doing so he should bear in
mind that the telegram was addressed to the representa-
tives of the Companies.
Mr. Yoshino read the message and commented:
First - that of course the Government wants the
Foreign Companies to stay in Japan.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer replied that they appreciated
that Mr. Yoshino has repeatedly given the Companies such
assurances and that it was with respect to this feature
in particular that they had asked Mr. Yoshino to bear in
mind that the telegram was addressed to the representa-
tives of the Companies.
Second - that a very long time has elapsed for the
development of a message which is chiefly repetition of the
views of the foreign companies.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer politely challenged the assor-
tion that the message is largely repetition and emphasized
that two ideas in particular are now advanced in a con-
crete form, namely (1) willingness on the part of the
Foreign Companies that Mitsui share in the trade (2) security
of supply for that portion of the trade to be done by Mi-
tsui and the prospect of an amicable attitude on the part
of the Foreign Companies toward the supply and other pro-
blems of the Domestic Companies. Messrs. Ely and Meyer
also grasped the opportunity to impress Mr. Yoshino with
the fact that in their opinion the solution of the problem
would have progressed much further had Mitsui seen fit to
send representatives to New York as soon as possible fol-
lowing New York and London's original suggestion.
Mr. Yoshino then dwelt at some length on Mitsui's
attitude of reluctance both towards the Foreign Companies
and towards the Government. In Mr. Yoshino's opinion such
a tendency on the part of Mitsui is not only characteristic
of a firm which occupies the position that it does in the
field of commerce, but is also no doubt designed to accomp-
lish the maximum results from its own point of view and
perhaps

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
perhaps still further a reflection of Mitsui's interest
in hydrogenation. (In this connection Mr. Yoshino
casually enquired ir Messrs. Ely and Meyer had not obtained
the impression that Mitsui's interest in oil has waned
somewhat since they became interested in the Fischer process.
Messrs. Ely and Meyer replied that they have drawn no such
conclusion. To Messrs. Ely and Meyer's inquiry if Mitsui
has definitely bought the Fischer process Mr. Yoshino re-
plied that he has no positive confirmation).
Mr. Yoshino suggested that Messrs. Ely and Meyer see
Mitsui immediately and upon being informed that they had
an appointment with Mr. Mukai promptly after seeing him,
he stated that he would telephone Mitsui on Monday or
Tuesday in order to expedite matters. He went on to say
that he himself is in a rather embarrassing situation when
questioned concerning the solution of the problem of
Foreign Companies' stock-holding and that he wants to get
something accomplished without further delay and that the
principles, at any rate, of the Foreign Companies' working
arrangement must be settled before July lst. Mr. Yoshino
expressed the hope that Mitsui will not now cause further
delay. Messrs. kly and Meyer suggested that, in the event
that the Government endorses the proposals made by the
Foreign Companies and will advise Mitsui to proceed to New
York for discussions, it is probable that Mitsui will heed
their advice. Mr. Yoshino agreed that such a procedure
might be effeotive.

Messrs. Ely and Meyer informed Mr. Mukai that London

of that sort at the interview that morning.

Mr. Dooman;
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
894.6363
Mr. Mackay.
UN 1 2 1936
Subject:
The oil situation in Japan and in
connection therewith the visit to
Washington of Ambassador Kurusu.
Mr. Mackay called Mr. Parker at New York by telephone.
Mr. Mackay referred to Mr. Parker's letter of June 8,
in regard to the above-mentioned subject, and said that due
894.6363/279
to Mr. Parker's absence from New York yesterday afternoon
an endeavor to reach him by 'phone at the time indicated
had proven unsuccessful. Mr. Mackay said that Ambassador
Kurusu had called at the Department on June 10; that there
had followed a discussion of commercial relations between
the United States and Japan and of problems in connection
therewith, including that resulting from the Japanese
Petroleum Control Law; and that as Mr. Dooman was present
at the discussion he (Mr. Dooman) would endeavor to acquaint
Mr. Parker with the substance of that portion of the con-
FILHD
versation which related to the oil situation in Japan.
Mr. Dooman then stated to Mr. Parker that, in the
course of an extended conversation between Mr. Kurusu and
an officer of the Department when various commercial
problems lying between the United States and Japan were
F
discussed,
F
G

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.98)
7F
- 2 -
disoussed, opportunity was had to explain to Mr. Kurusu
in clear language the dissatisfaction of the American
Government over the manner in which American oil interests
in Japan were being dealt with by the Japanese Government.
Mr. Kurusu had replied that unfortunately the issue had
been brought about by the enactment of a law, with regard
to which law he would reserve his opinion. Mr. Kurusu
had expressed confidence that difficulties would be
amicably adjusted. He had pointed out that the American
oil interests in Japan had put forward three desiderata:
first, a guarantee that they would be permitted to operate
in Japan for a stipulated period of time; second, a
guarantee that they be permitted to sell each year a
definite quantity of petroleum products; and third, the
raising of the price in Japan of gasoline. Mr. Kurusu
had stated that the Japanese Government had met the foreign
oil companies at once on the first two points, and that
although there had been certain difficulties in increasing
the price of gasoline he had heard shortly before leaving
Japan that decision had been made to increase the price
of gasoline.
Mr. Parker stated that apparently Mr. Kurusu had not
been frank with regard to the second point; that although
it is true that the Japanese Government has agreed to
guarantee that the foreign oil companies be permitted
to market each year a definite quantity of petroleum
produots,

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.98)
7
- 3 •
products, the foreign companies had in fact insisted
that they be permitted to share in meeting the growing
demand in Japan for petroleum produots; and that this
demand had not benmet by the Japanese Government.
Mr. Dooman stated that the general principle of
American interests being permitted to expand their
business in Japan as there is increasing demand for the
products handled by these American interests had in fact
been emphasized.to Mr. Kurusu.
Mr. Dooman added that Mr. Kurusu had expressed
optimism in regard to certain negotiations which were
taking place between the foreign oil concerns and Japanese
interests, and that he (Mr. Kurusu) had given the impression
that as soon as the various parties concerned had agreed
upon a place for negotiation the negotiations would be
brought rapidly to a successful conclusion.
In response to Mr. Dooman's question whether Mr. Parker
desired that any further points be discussed by officers of
the Department with Mr. Kurusu, Mr. Parker stated that he
would appreciate the hope being expressed to Mr. Kurusu
that he would urge his Government to persuade Mitsui to
send their representatives to New York to enter into
negotiations with representatives of the foreign oil
interests. Mr. Parker stated that the company's repre-
sentative in Japan had recently cabled that oil quotas for
the second half of the year would be allotted by the
Japanese

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.96)
F
Japanese Government on July 1, but that the terms of
the petroloum law with regard to stocks would have to
be complied with on or before June 30. It was Mr. Parker's
thought that if the Japanese Government could be assured
that the negotiations with Mitsui were progressing
satisfactorily, no penalties would be exacted for
non-fulfillment of the stock keeping requirements. It
1s therefore very urgently desired that Mr. Kurusu do
what he can to induce Mitsui to despatch representatives
to New York as soon as possible. Mr. Dooman said that
he would see Mr. Kurusu this morning, and that he would
be glad to discuss the matter with him.
Mr. Parker expressed appreciation of the information
which had been made available to him and stated that he
did not expect that any further acknowledgment would be
made of his recent letter to Mr. Hornbeck on the subject
of Mr. Kurusu's visit.

Sir:
Referring to the Embassy's despatch No. 1860,
FILED
dated May 27, 1936, and to previous correspondence on
the subject of the difficulties being experienced by
the American and other foreign oil interests in Japan
under the Petroleum Industry Law, I have the honor to
report the following developments in the situation.
Negotiations with the Mitaui Interesta.
In despatoh No. l860, referred to above, the Embassy
roported that the foreign oil interests had proposed to
F/FG
the

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the Mitsui interests that the Mitsui interests carry
for the oil companies all non-commercial stocks re-
quired by the Japanese Gover ment, in return for which
the Mitsui interests would receive all compensation to
be paid by the Gover ment for the storage of oil, as well
as some undetermined share of the foreign oil companies'
Japan business and a long-term contraot for a supply of
oil to cover Litsui's requirements. The representatives
of the foreign oil companies conferred on several oc-
casions with the representatives of the Mitsui interests
and by June 1 the Mitsui interests had evolved the
plan described below.
The Mitsui interests woula organize a new company
to carry the excess stocks of oil, with a capitalization
of Yen 20,000,000, on which the Mitsui interests would
expect to receive a net profit of eight per cent per
annum. In order to obtain this return, they would expeot,
in addition to the Government compensation, income in the
form of a storage fee to be paid by the foreign companies
for the carrying of the oil and further income in the
form of profit on & certain volume of trade in petro-
leum products to be allotted to the Mitsui interests out
of the foreign companies' annual quotas. The Mitsui in-
terests would not build further distribution facilities
to compete with the facilities of the foreign oil com-
panies, but would want to share the present facilities.
The Mitsui interests emphasized that they have no desire
gradually to crowd the foreign oil companies out of the
Japan

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
Japan field, but wish to render a service by finding
a harmonious and permanent solution of the whole
pr blem of oil in Japan. Owing to the present feel-
ing in Japan against the large financial and industrial
interests, however, the Mitsui interests refer that
Japanese capital other than itsui be included in the
new comnany (referring probably to the Mippon Sangyo
interests, who desire participetion in the oil trade),
and that the storage fee to be paid by the foreign
oil companies be kept as low as possible, in order to
avoid criticism to the effect that Japanese middle-
men are causing any future increase in the price of
gasoline. The Mitsui representatives also stated posi-
tively that they could not consent to hold the nego-
tiations in New York, and that if conferences in New
York were a condition of the proposal they would be
compelled to refuse to consider the proposal.
The local representatives of the foreign oil
interests telegraphed the Mitsui proposal to their
respective head offices on June 1, l936, adding their
own views in regard to the proposal. The views of the
local representatives of the oil companies do not differ
from those of the Mitsui interests on points of prin-
ciple but only on points of fact. For example, they
believe that seven per cent per annum on the capital
involved should be sufficient, and that the storage
fee should be fairly large, in order to avoid the turning
over to Mitsui of too large a proportion of their trade.
It appears, therefore, that the negotiations in the future
w111

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
will no longer be based on the principles of the arrange-
ment but will consist simply of business details. There
is reason to believe, therefore, that the negotiations
will eventually evolve a satisfactory arrangement, un-
less it appears later that there is too great a spread
between the business ideas of the foreign oil interests
and those of the lAitsui interests.
Attitude of the Authorities toward the Legotiations.
The Vice Liinister for Commerce and Industry (Mr.
Yoshino) successfully avoided interpellations in the
Diet on the subject of the refusal of the foreign oil
companies to carry six months' stocks of oil, but he
is very anxious that the negotiations with the Mitsui
interests reach some definite arrangement before June
30, 1936, at which time the quotas for the nert six
months must be allotted. On June 9, 1936, therefore,
the local representatives of the foreign oil companies
were called to the Mining Bureau of the Department of
Commerce and Industry and there requested to give some
assurance that the negotiations with the Japanese in-
terests were progressing toward a satisfactory solution
and that the basis of the arrangement and the outline of
the scheme had been agreed upon. An answer was requested
by the end of the week, it being necessary to proceed
with the official business in connection with the July-
December quotas. The local representatives of the foreign
oil companies telegraphed this request to their principals,
with the suggestion that some suoh answer as the following
be sent to the Mining Bureau :
An

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
"An arrangement for the satisfactory solution of

Legislation.

dated December 12, 1935, it was proposed to increase
the import tariff on asoline, crude oil and lubricating
oils and greases, in order to provide additional revenue
with which to meet the cost of payment of compensation for

Department at a later date, with a di. cussion of the efiect
of the revised rates upon the merican petroleum trade with
Japan.
The Diet also approved an item in the budget of
Yen 2,640,000 , being compensation for the holding of stocks
of oil under the Petroleum Industry Law. Details of the
contemplated allocation of this sum to the various Japa-
nese and foreign oil companies have not yet been disclosed,

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American Oil Interests.
In reply to the proposal made in March by the
American and British oil interests that a contractual
arrangement of sorts be made between the foreign oil
companies and the Mitsui interests so as to avoid the
necessity of storage of non-commercial stocks by the
foreign firms, the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha (Mitsui Products
Company) told representatives of the foreign firms that
if the oil companies intended to utilize Mitsui simply
as a storage or warehousine medium the Japanese company
was definitely not interested in the proposal for the
reason that Mitsui Bussan Kaisha was organized for trade
purposes. However, the Mitsui representatives expressed
a willingness to consider a broader arrangement which
would involve trading on their part and requested that
the oil companies make a definite proposal. Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, implied in
a conversation with representatives of the foreign oil
interests on April 24 that Mitsui might be more willing
to negotiate if the Japanese Government expressed a wish
that some agreement be reached but added that it would
first be necessary for the oil companies to draw up a
definite proposal for the approval of the Japanese Govern-
ment.*

(a).
American Oil Interests.
On May 16 the representatives of the American and
British oil companies presented the following plan to Mr.
Yoshino, Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, concern-
ing cooperation between the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company on the one hand and
the Mitsui interests on the other: Mitsui would carry
all the non-conmercial oil stocks required by the Japanese
Government; in return Mitsui would receive the entire
compensation paid by the Government for stock-keeping,
as well as some share of the foreign oil companies' business
in Japan; and the oil companies would make a permanent
supply contract with Mitsui, in return for which they would
ask that the present volume quotas granted by the Japanese
Government be changed to percentage quotas. In their
conversation with Mr. Yoshino the oil company representatives
implied that unless some arrangement of the sort could be
reached the companies would be compelled sooner or later
to withdraw from Japan, to which Mr. Yoshino replied that
the Japanese Government wished the foreign oil companies
to remain in Japan, promising that he would endeavor to
induce Mitsui to expedite matters so that some arrange-
ment could be reached before July 1, l936 (the date on
which the oil companies are required to possess six
months' stocks in Japan). On the same day the American
and British oil company representatives interviewed one
of the managing directors of the Mitsui interests along
similar lines. The Mitsui representative received the
proposals favorably.* Although negotiations were

Referring to the Embassy's despatch No. 1895, dated
June 12, 1936, and to previous correspondence on the
subject of the difficulties being experienced by the
American oil interests in Japan under the Petroleum In-
dustry Law, I have the honor to report the following
developments in the situation.
Relations with the Japanese Authorities.
In despatoh No. l895, referred to above, the Embassy
reported that the local representatives of the Standard

and

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and Shell interests had proposed to their principals
a statement which, it was believed, would serve to

tentions of the oil interests in connection with the
stockholding obligation of the law. The statement,
as revised by the head orrices of the oil interests
and presented to the Department of Commerce and In-
dustry, read as follows:
"An arrangement for solution of stock-
holdings question is now under discussion
between our Companies and Jepanese interests.
The mutually acceptable basis on which arrange-

and also establishment of trade relations with

This statement was presented to I.r. Sakai of the

A memorandum of the conversation of the local represen-
tatives with the representative of the Depertment of Com-
1/ merce and Industry on that day is enclosed herewith.
It was thought that the statement given above would
be sufficient to satisfy the authorities, but on June 22,

terests were called to the Department of Commerce and

The local representatives of the oil interests were
certain that their principals would not agree to the
first part of the statement, as it obligated them defi-
nitely to fulrill the stockholding obligation under the
law. They therefore discussed the uestion for some time
with the represe.tative of the Depurtment of Conmerce and
Industry, endeavorin to use some such  hrase as 'we are
endeavorin to arra.ge to fulfill" or "we ure in process
of negotiatin arra..gements to fulfill", but the repre-
sentative of the Depart..ent of Conmnerce and Industry re-
fused to consider any such mouifications. A memorandum
2/
of the conversation is enclosed.
The local representatives of the oil interests uis-
cussed this development vith their respective Embassies
and on the following day Sir George Sansom, the Comaercial
Counsellor of the British mbassy and r. Dickover of this
Embassy, happening to meet r. Yoshino, Vice Minister of
Commerce and Industry, at the Tokyo Club, brou ht up the
subject in the course of the e suing conversation. Ir.
Yoshino stated that it was imperctive that his Department
receive some definite assurance within the next two or
three days that the oil compenies would complete an arrange-
ment to carry six months' stock of oil, as otherwise it
would be impossible for him to grant to the foreign oil
companies for the period from July to December of this
year the seme quotas as they had received during the
first half of the year, and, moreover, he would be un-
able to postpone further the granting of permits for
additional

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
additional refining facilities to the domestic oil com-
panies which had applied for such permits. Mr. Yoshino
then stated that he could accept as sufficient some such
statement as the following:
"The companies are now negotiating a
special errangement to enable them to
fulfil their obligation under the Pe-
troleum Law and have reached an agree-
ment in principle."
Sir George Sansom and ir. Dickover obtained the distinct
impression from the conversation that a statement such as
the above was the minimum which ir. Yoshino could accept
in order to give unreduced import and sales quotas to the
foreign oil companies and to continue to postpone the
granting of additional refining permits to the domestic
companies. They therefore translated the above statement
into English and imnediately communicated it to the local
representatives of the oil companies, who telegraphed to
their principals for authority to present it to the Depart-
ment of Conmerce and Industry. Authorization has now been
received and the statement was presented today.
In the course of the conversation with Mr. Yoshino,
he stated definitely that it would be impossible for the
authorities further to postpone the enforcement of the
stockholding provision of the law, as the enforcement had
already been postponed for nine months and the authori-
ties (which authorities were meant was not stated but the
implication was that r. Yoshino was speaking of the mili-
tary authorities) would not permit a further postponement.
In view of the fact, therefore, that any representations made
by the Embassies to endeavor to obtain a further postponement
of the stockholding obligation would probably meet with a rebuff,
it

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
it appeared advisable to this Embassy and to the British
Embassy that no official representations be made on the
subjeot unless end until the authorities of the Depert-
ment of Commerce and Industry refused to accept the re-
vised statement quoted above. At the time of writing it
appears thut the revised stutenent will be accepted by
the authorities.
Negotiations with the Mitsui Interests.
As was stated in despatch .o. 1895, the Mitsui in-

the itsui proposition was acceptable in principle, al-
though there was some disagreement on details. The local
representatives have not yet communicated the reply to
the Mitsui interests and are discussing certain points
with their principals by telegraph, but it is believed
that another conversation on the subject will be held
with the Mitsui interests before the end of this week.

Notes of interview at Department of Commerce
& Industry on June 13th, 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. Sakai (Chief of Fuel Section) acting on
behalf of Mr. Kojima, Head of Mining
Bureau
Messr.Meyer (S.V.O.C.) and Ely and Ezoye (R.S.P.C.)
Messrs. Ely and Meyer reminded Mir. Sakai that he had
asked them by that day to make a statement concerning the
stock-holding position.
They wished therefore to confirm that an arrangement
for a solution of the stock-holding question was now
under discussion between their Companies and Japanese
interests.
They were able also to inform him that the mutually
acceptable basis on which the arrangement was being de-
veloped was that the Japanese interests would provide the
non-commercial stocks and storage required by the Petro-
leum Law in return for suitable compensation: This would
include, besides the pecuniary compensation to be paid by
the Government in respect of such stocks and storage,
other considerations to be mutually agreed upon and also
the establishment of trade relations with the Japanese
interests concerned and supply contracts in respect of the
petroleum business done by them in Japan.
Mr. Sakai listened attentively and noted down Capt.
Ezoye's translation of what Messrs. Ely and Meyer had
said. He asked if they could tell him anything more
specific concerning the stage which the negotiations had
reached: for instance, with regard to the various considera-
tions to be agreed upon, could it be said that some were
nearer settlement than others?
Messrs. Meyer and Ely said that it was rather diffi-
cult to answer that question specifically and to differenti-
ate in regard to the progress being made with the various
phases of the basic arrangement they had outlined. In re-
gard to the present position of the negotiations, they
could tell Mr. Sakai that they were now in communication
with their Principals by cable concerning certain specific
points - what Mr. Sakai might call "details" - which had
developed from the basis they had described.
In reply to another question from Mr. Sakai as to
whether they themselves felt that the negotiations would
be brought to a successful conclusion Messrs. Meyer and
Ely replied in the affirmative subject to no unforeseen
circumstances arising.
M.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Mr. Sakai then went on to say that, going on what
Messrs. Meyer and Ely had reported officially, he could
not yet consider himself convinced that the matter would
eventually be successfully settled. He would report to
Mr. Yoshino and enquiries might have to be made from the
Japanese interests concerned. In these circumstances
with lst July gettin: near, he had to warn Messrs. Ely
and Meyer that there was the risk of the Department having
to take some drastic or unpleasant action towards their
Companies - although the authorities did not wish to have
to do so. He would therefore ask them to keep the Bureau
advised of further progress in the negotiations.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely promised to do so and asked
Mr. Sakai's advice as to whether, in order to regularise
their position in Chosen and Taiwan, they should give
similar information to the two Governments-General. They
also wished to request the good offices of the Ministry
of Commerce & Industry to make known the situation to the
Overseas Ministry.
Mr. Sakai said he could not reply without consulting
Mr. Yoshino, which he would do and communicate later with
Messrs. Ely and Meyer. The position was that in the
Commerce Ministry knowledge of the Foreign Companies'
negotiations with Japanese interests was confined strictly
to Mr. Yoshino, Mr. Kojima and himself, while Mr. Yoshino
was personally in touch with one official in the Overseas
Ministry. If the Companies were to advise the Governments-
General of Chosen and Taiwan it was rather a delicate
question as to what particular officials should be approached.
He could, however, tell Messrs. Meyer and Ely that in
Chosen and Taiwan their Companies would receive similar
treatment in regard to this situation as in Japan Proper.

Enclosure No. 2 to despatoh
No.1914 of June 26, 1936 from the

Notes of Interview at Department of Commerce and
Industry on June 22nd, 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. Kojima (Head of Mining Bureau)
Messrs LMeyer (S.V.O.C.) und Ely & Ezoye(R.S.P.Co.)
Mr. Kojima said he wished to discuss the question which
had been the subject of essrs Ely and Meyer's recent inter-
views with r. Sakai and the first part of the ensuing con-
versation followed very clo ely the lines of the interview
on June 13th, Messrs. ly end eyer outlining the state-
ment they had made to ir. Sakai nd confirming their pre-
viously expressed opinion that, subject to no unlooked for
circumstances, there were good prospects that the arrange-
ment under discussion with Japanese interests for a solution
of the stock-holding question would be hrou ht to a success-
ful conclusion.
l.r. Kojima then itimated that in order to regularize
its position under the Law, for the administration of which
it was responsible to the Gover: ment and therefore subject
to criticism fro... other Departments, the Commerce Ministry
must have something more uefinite from the two Companies.
To preserve their status as Importers and Traders and to
receive quotas, it was necessary for the Companies to com-
ply with the Law and to lold the stipulated amount of stocks,
and the Ministry required a specific decleration of the
Companies' intention to do so. r. Kojima then dictated
in Japanese a draft of the notification which he desired
the Companies to send him in writin, the translation being:-
(1) We (the two Companies) will fulfill the tock-
holding obligation.
(2) For that purpose we are arranging special means.
Messrs. Meyer and ly pointed out that this particu-
lar wording did not guite meet the situation in that it
dissociated from the Companies' fulfilment of the stock-
holding obligation the special means, which, as Ir. Kojima
knew, were essential to it because the Companies them-
selves could not undertake to assume the burden of carrying
non-commercial stocks. They suggested many alternative
formulae to overcome this point, but in the course of a
long discussion Mr. Kojima rejected everything which asso-
ciated the means with the fulfilment of the obligation or
in any way qualified (l). He politely but firmly insisted
that he must have a separate and straightforward declara-
tion that the Companies would fulfil the stock-holding re-
quirements of the Law and he closed the interview with a
request that Messrs. Ely and Meyer would further consider
the matter and let him have a reply as soon as possible.

JAP OIL LAW
H
U.S.UnitAccepts
TOKIO. June 27. -- Standard
Vacuum Oil Co. and Rising Sun
Petroleum, the latter a Royal
Dutch-Shell affiliate, today agreed
to accept the oil law provisions
requiring the storage of an amount
equivalent to six months' imports.
Actual terms of the compromise
were not revealed, but it is under-
stood the government is overlook-
ing the fact that tankage will not
be available on July 1, when the
provision becomes legally effective.
The decision closes a two-year
controversy, in which foreigners'
defiance once seemed likely to
force their withdrawal, resulting
in their boycotting Japan.
The Diet recently voted to de-
fray part of the expenses of build-
ing tanks for storing oil, which
simplified the solution.

received quotas on all products equal in volume to those al-
located for each of the previous quarters.
The assurance we gave the Government in regard
to meeting the storage provision, is predicated on a joint pro-
posal of our British friends and ourselves, that a Japanese
storage company comprising Japanese capital, be formed in Japan
for the purpose of holding for our account, the non-commercial
stock requirements, and on terms which are now being negotieted.
It will be seen from this that we are continuing the policy of
presenting a united front with the British.
We have also, in conjunction with London, of-
fered to make available to the Japanese, certain hydrogenation
processes controlled by our associates. While the Japanese have
undoubtedly made considerable progress themselves in hydrogena-
tion, nevertheless it seems that the Government is interested
in acquiring our processes. We are hoping that our offer will
be of material value in the negotiations regarding the storage
company.
