Sir:
Referring to the Embassy's despatch No. 1914, dated
June 26, 1936, and to previous correspondence on the subject
of the difficulties being experienced by the American oil
interests in Japan under the Petroleum Industry Law, I have
the honor to report the following developments in the sit-
uation.
Negotiations with the Japanese Authorities.
As was reported in despatch No. 1914, the local
representatives of the Standard and Shell interests re-
ceived permission from their principals to submit to the
Department

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Department of Commerce and Industry a statement to the
effeot that the Companies are now negotiating a special
arrangement to enable them to fulfill their obligations
under the Petroleum Law and have reached an agreement in
principle. This statement was to be submitted for the
purpose of permitting the Department of Commerce and In-
dustry to grant them quotas similar to those held before
July 1, and to avoid the imposition of the penalties pro-
vided by the Law for the failure to hold the stooks re-
quired. The statement was presented to an official of
the Department of Commerce and Industry on June 26, 1936,
but while the official accepted the statement, he stated
that his Ministry was still somewhat embarrassed, as no
material progress had been made toward the storage of the
oil. However, he said, if the foreign oil companies in-
dicated on the form provided therefor their plans for carry-
ing six months' stocks during the July-September period,
that statement would be accepted as evidence of their in-
tention to carry the stocks required and the Companies would
not be considered as law-breakers. This was done and the
Companies were granted quotas for the July-September period
equivalent to those granted for the first two quarters of
this year.
On June 27 and 28, certain Japanese newspapers pub-
lished statements to the effect that the foreign oil com-
panies' attitude in regard to stock-holding was softening
after protracted nogotiations with the Government, that they
had agreed to fulfill the stock holding obligation and that,
therefore, no penalties would be inflicted upon them. The
special agreement by which the stocks were to be held was
not mentioned by the newspapers.
on

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
On July 4, at their own request, Mr. Meyer of the
Standard-Vaouum Oil Company and Mr. Ely of the Rising
Sun Petroleum Company had an interview with Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice-Minister for Commerce and Industry. Mr. Yoshino
stated that the Minister for Commerce and Industry, in
view of the imminence of the time when all oil companies
were supposed to have six months' stooks in storage, was
compelled to make some announcement of the foreign com-
panies' position. He had, therefore, made a brief announce-
ment to the press but he had mentioned the special arrange-
mont in his announcement. The newspapers, however, had
neglected to include the special arrangement in their
aocount of the interview, and had thus implied that the
foreign oil companies had reversed their position and had
agreed to carry six months' stooks.
Mr. Meyer and Mr. Ely then took up with Mr. Yoshino
the question of the allocation of quotas in the future,
(their companies had been given no share of the natural
inorease in the quotas for the July-September quarter)
as such quotas had an important bearing on their nego-
tiations with the Mitsui interests. Mr. Yoshino stated
that, as the question of stock-holding was still not en-
tirely settled, the question of sharing the natural in-
crease in demand must also be held in abeyance for the
presènt. Mr. Yoshino affirmed, however, that all guar-
antees and benefits embodied in the Five Point Memorandum
of April, l935, would automatically come into force for the
foreign oil companies as soon as the stook-holding obli-
gation was fulfilled, and that he might even consider
putting quotas on a permanent percentage basis.
This

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
This conversation is important in that it olearly
indicates the present attitude of the Japanese authori-
ties toward the question of the position of the foreign
oil companies, and therefore a memorandum of the con-
1/
versation is enclosed.
Negotiations with the Mitsui Interests.
On June 26, 1936, Mr. Meyer and Mr. Ely called on
Mr. Mukai of the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha and informed him
that their principals had agreed in general to the plan
previously put forward by Mr. Mukai, namely, that (1)
the foreign oil companies' non-oommercial stock-holding
obligations under the new law be performed by Japanese
interests and (2) a new company to be organized to per-
form this function will derive its income from Govern-
ment compensation and profits on distribution of a share
of the foreign oil companies' trade. Nothing more do-
finite evolved from this meeting.
On July 6, Mr. Meyer and Mr. Ely discussed with Mr.
Mukai of the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha some further details
of the problem. As a result of the conversation, Mitsui's
agreement was obtained to the following points:
1. The storage company to be organized by Mitsui
will construct tankage on or adjacent to the eristing
installations of the foreign oil companies.
2. The foreign oil companies will take over stocks
and tanks when they become commercial stocks and tanks.
3. The Japanese storage company will loan the
stocks in bond to the foreign oil companies in order that
the latter as licensed importers may pass them through
the Customs.
on

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
On the question of sending a representative to New
York to disouss further details, however, Mr. Mukai
again refused any agreement.
The local representatives of the Standard and Shell
interests cabled the results of their conversations of
July 4 with Mr. Yoshino and of July 6 with Mr. Mukai to
their principals, adding their opinion that the time
had arrived for more specific discussions with the Mitsui
interests, such as giving the Mitsui interests a rough
estimate of the cost of laying down non-commercial stocks
if on or adjacent to the foreign oil companies' properties.
They also advised an early offer to sell the I.G. pro-
cess for hydrogenation of coal to Japanese interests,
without reference to any question of oil storage, in view
of the fact that the Japanese are proceeding resolutely
with the development of the Japanese Navy's process for
hydrogenation and that a long delay might be disadvan-
tagoous to the holders of I.G. patents.
The head offices of the Standard and Shell interests
have agreed to the proposition, and it is now expected that
negotiations will proceed more rapidly.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatoh
No.1938 of July 10, 1936 from
the Embassy at Tokyo.
Notes of Conversation at Ministry of Commerce and
Industry on July 4th. 1936.
PRESENT:
Mr. Yoshino (Vice Minister of Commerce and Industry)
Messrs Meyer(S-V.O.C.) and Ely (R.S.P.Co.)
The first part of the conversation was occupied with the
subject of the reports which appeared in the leading Japanese
newspapers of 27th and 28th June concerning the attitude of
the foreign Oil Companies towards the stock-holding obli-
gations. Mr. Yoshino said that he realized that Messrs.
Meyer and Ely might be placed in an awkward position vis-a-vis
their Prinoipals and he wished therefore to explain that
the newspaper reports were a highly colored version of
a very guarded announcement made by the Minister himself.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely would realize that the imminence of
July lst had necessitated some announcement - in the in-
terests of all concerned - but that it would have been pre-
mature and inadvisable to have made any detailed reference
to the special arrangement which the foreign companies were
discussing but which so far had been agreed upon only in
principle. The Minister had therefore merely mentioned
the special arrangement.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely confirmed that the press reports
might lead to a very dangerous misunderstanding abroad, as
all of them had stated that the foreign companies had swallowed
the precedent to which they had been objecting and which,
as Mr. Yoshino knew, they could not accept; and not one
of them seemed to have made any reference whatever to the
existence of a special arrangement. Mr. Yoshino emphasized
that the Minister himself had mentioned it, quoting the
Japanese expression which had been used, and suggested
that Messrs. Meyer and Ely should advise London and New
York of what had actually taken place, which they promised
to do.
By saying next that he hoped that the negotiations with
Mitsuis would proceed and be brought to a conclusion as quickly
as possible Mr. Yoshino gave Messrs. Meyer and Ely the op-
portunity of bringing up the subjeot of the future treatment
of their Companies in regard to trade quotas. They pointed
out that, as Mr. Yoshino knew, the proposed arrangement
involved a consideratien of trade and that this was a question
which might have a very important bearing on the success
of the negotiations. They assumed that their Companies
could look forward to receiving their full share of the
increase in consumption - but on the other hand they had
just been given for July/September only the same quotas as
for the previousrquarters of the year.

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. Yoshino immediately confirmed that as soon as
the stock-keeping obligations of the Law were fulrilled
the two Companies would participate fairly in the in-
crease in consumption (mentioning later, parentheti-
cally, that the amount available for distribution might
be affected by the manufacture of substitutes) and re-
minded Messrs. Meyer and Ely that this was one of the
points of the Five Point Memorandum. At present the
special arrangement planned by the Companies was still
under consideration and consequently the question of
their share of the natural increase must also be kept
"on suspense". He went on to say, however, that this
matter of the division of the natural inorease be-
tween importers and refiners was one which he was an-
rious to get settled as soon as possible, especially
as he had held up and was continuing to hold up appli-
cations from Refiners for extensions of their facilities.
His own idea of the division was 50/50. There was also
the question of guarantees for the foreign companies.
In reply to Messrs. Meyer and Ely's question Mr.
Yoshino also confirmed that all the principles covered
by the Five Point Memorandum would hold good and, on
being reminded of the suggestion made by London and
New York in a recent cable that the Companies' position
and their share in the natural increase should be con-
solidated by their being given a percentage share of the
trade, he again emphasized that he wae very anxious to get
this question fired and the future clarified, as he felt
that he had some personal responsibility in the matter.
Messrs. Meyer and Ely thanked Mr. Yoshino for these
personal assurances which gave them a great deal of con-
fidence and which, with his concurrence, they proposed
to convey to their Principals. They understood that what
Mr. Yoshino had said applied also to Chosen and Taiwan -
and they mentioned the former in particular, because there
seemed to be signs that the Government General was inolined
to act independently: they were relying on Mr. Yoshino's
good offices, promised at the time the Directors were here,
to make arrangements with the Overseas Department. Mr.
Yoshino avoided a specific reply, saying that the Chosen
Government-General was "difficult" in other matters, e.g.,
Ammonium Sulphate, with which he had to deal, but inti-
mated that he would continue to work with the Overseas
Department and that the same principles should apply
in Chosen as in Japan Proper.
In conoluding the conversation Mr. Yoshino made one
or two very unofficial remarks. He was sorry that so many
difrioulties had arisen in connection with the Petroleum
Law and felt that much trouble would have been avoided if
there had been more consultation beforehand with the oil
Companies -, with which view Messrs. Meyer and Ely
emphatically conourred. Mr. Yoshino went on to say that he
had to take some responsibility personally, but actually
a great deal of the work in conneotion with framing the Law
had

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
had been done by subordinates, in particular by Mr.
Fukuda, ex Head of the Mining Bureau. Even so, why
had the foreign companies not come to him (Mr. Yoshino)?
Mr. Ely pointed out that the foreign companies would have
been only too glad to do so but that, from the first,
they had been confronted by a fait accompli.

AUG25 1936
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
FULED
Referring to the Embassy's despatch No. 1938, dated
July 10, 1936, and to previous correspondence on the
subject of the difficulties being experienced in Japan
by the American and other foreign oil companies under
the recently inaugurated Petroleum Industry Law, I have
the honor to report the following developments in the
situation.
Negotiations with the Mitsui Interests.
The negotiations between the Mitsui interests and
the Standard and Shell interests have been progressing
slowly, but apparently steadily, toward a common agreement.
丑
A8

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
As was reported in previous despatches, an agreement on
principle has been reached, but details have yet to be
arranged. These details must be referred back to the
headquarters of the Standard and Shell interests. At
present the principal desiderata of the foreign oil
interests are that the Jananese Government shall indicate
the maximum amount of storage to be required (to place a
"ceiling" on the storage, as kir. Kurusu expressed it),
and to indicate clearly the amount of governmental com-
pensation which may be expected for the carrying of excess
stocks in the future. These items are necessary to the
foreign oil interests in order that they may reach an
agreement with the Mitsui interests. As the foreign oil
interests have not been able to obtain a definite statement
on these points from the Japanese Government, they have
asked the Mitsui interests to make the necessary inquiries.
The Mitsui interests have also indicated clearly that they
will place no relience upon Japanese governmental com-
pensation for interest and profit on and amortization of
their investment in carrying the foreign oil interests'
non-commercial stocks, but will expect a definite annual
percentage of the foreign oil interests' sales quotas
as their primary compensation.
Relations with the Japanese Authorities.
In the meantime the authorities of the Japanese Ministry
of Commerce and Industry have been pressing the foreign oil
interests for some definite statement indicating when the
storage facilities, which it is implied will be built, will
be constructed and when the oil required by law to be stored
w111

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
will be placed in storage. The oil interests have there-
fore, with the approval of their head offices, stated
that the construction of tanks will be commenced by the
end of September next and that the oil will be placed in
storage about April, 1937, "subject to alteration depending
upon special erranement now under discussion".
Hydrogenation of Coal.
As was reported in despatch No. 1938, the oil in-
terests have decided to sell the I.G. process for the
hydrogenation of coal to Jananese interests without
reference to the question of oil storage. Accordingly,
on July 15, 1936, the local represe tatives of the foreign
oil interests called upon Lir. Yoshino, the Vice Minister
for Commerce and Industry, and stated that the I.G. pro-
cess was offered for sale, entirely outside any negotiations
on the oil question. They then requested information as to
the Japanese interests which they should approach in recard
to the matter. ir. Yoshino advised them confidentially
that the Government had a plan of forming a pool, which
would acquire the various hydrogenation processes and which
would probably be owned partly by the South Lianchuria Rail-
way and partly by the large financial interests, such as
Mitsui, Mitsubishi and Sumitomo, and agreed that, pending
a decision upon the pool, it would be better for the
foreign oil interests not to negotiate with private
Japanese interests. Lir. Yoshino, however, exhibited,
considerable interest in the proposal of the oil interests
to sell their patent rights to Japanese interests.
He later brought up the question of the economio
value of any of the coal hydrogenation processes. He
thought

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
thought that gasoline made from coal might be commercially
feasible if the price of petroleum gasoline could be raised
to 55 sen per gallon (the present price is about 50 sen
per gallon). It was pointed out to him by the representa-
tives of the oil interegts, however, that distribution
costs alone amounted to about 15 sen per gallon, and that
in reat Britain, where hydrogenation of coal had been
placed on a commercial basis, the selling price of gasoline
made from coal was about double the selling price of gaso-
line in Japan .
The newspapers, in commenting upon the determined efforts
of the Government to encourage the production of gasoline
by the hydrogenation of coal, state that the selling price of
gasoline must rise to 70 or 80 sen per gallon before the
scheme will be commercially feasible.
The Search for Oil.
The Embassy has been confidentially informed that the
Toa Sekiyu Kabushiki Kaisha (Far astern Petroleum Company)
of Fusan, Chosen, has secured an oil concession in the
Tehuantepec valley of Mexico amounting to 280,000 chobu
(one chobu is equivalent to 2.l54 acres). The Company,
which was formed in ay, 1936, has a capital of Yen 1,000,000,
fully paid up, and plans have been made to increase the
capital to Yen 10,c00,000 and 1ater to Yen 30,000,000.
The Company is now erecting tanks to receive the crude oil
at Fusan, Chosen , and is planning to erect tanks at Wakayama,
Japan. It is also stated confidentially that Mr. Ikeda, the
representative

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
representative director of the Company, has the support
of the Japanese Army, and that an army officer accom-
panied him to Mexico to investigate the properties which
the Company now controls.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph . Grew.

between the foreign oil companies and Mitsui,
with regard to the storage of the non-
commercial stocks required by law, are pro-
gressing very slowly -- in fact so slowly
that the Government departments concerned are
showing signs of impatience. The Vice

future.

develop Japanese sources of fuel supply. It
is reported that a company is to be set up

1s further reported that consideration is

requiring dealers in gasoline to mix a certain
proportion

proportion of alcohol in all gasoline sold.

aloohol produced in Japan, and in view
further of the high cost of alcohol, it
seems unlikely that this project will be put
into effect.
Attention is drawn tc the last nara-
graph of the latter despatch, in which it
is stated that although it would seem that
the efforts of the Jananese Government tc
make Japan independent of foreign sources
of liquid fuel "would be doomed to failure",
the success which the Japanese have rad in
other directlons, notably in regard to iron
and steel, and chemicals, the Embassy appears
to believe that the Japanese Goverrment will
eventually get a considerable measure of suc-
cess.

46
Referring to the Embassy's despatch No. 1977, dated
July 10, 1936, and to previous correspondence on the subject
of the difficulties being experienced in Japan by the American
and other foreign oil companies under the Petroleum Industry
Law, I have the honor to report the following developments
in the situation.
FILED
Negotiations with the Mitsui Interests.
The negotiations of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
the Rising Sun Petroleum Company with the Mitsui interests, in
regard to the storage by the Mitsui interests of the non-commer-
cial stocks required by law, are progressing very slowly,
F/FG
largely due to the fact that each point of the negotiations
must

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
must be referred by the local representatives of the two
oil companies to their home offices and by the fact that
the Mitsui representatives also must refer each important
point to their directors. It is the opinion of the local
representatives of the oil companies that the negotiations
would have been concluded by now if the discussions had been
held in New York as was originally proposed by the Standard
and Shell interests. However, there appear to be no serious
points of difference between the foreign and Japanese in-
terests and, therefore, there is no reason to believe that
the two parties will not eventually reach an agreement.
In the meantime, however, the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry is pressing the foreign oil companies for some con-
crete evidence of their intention to arrange for the carrying
of the stocks required by the law. On August 11, 1936, Mr.
Yoshino, the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, invited
the local representatives of the foreign oil interests to his
office and informed them that other Departments of the Govern-
ment were dissatisfied with the apparent lack of progress
made in the negotiations between the foreign oil companies
and the Mitsui interests, rendering most difficult the posi-
tion of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry vis-a-vis other
governmental Departments. Mr. Yoshino also stated that the
Government would have to inject its influence into the nego-
tiations if those negotiations became too protracted. More-
over, if too much time should be taken for the negotiations
it might be necessary to have other governmental representa-
tives, probably of the Army and the Navy, bring pressure
on

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
on the Mitsui interests to start the purchase of tank materials.
The local representatives of the oil interests conveyed the
gist of this conversation to the Mitsui interests, who pro-
mimed to make some concrete proposal to the foreign oil in-
terests within a short time.
National Fuel Policy.
In connection with the present examination by the Cabi-
net of proposed State policies in Japan it has been reported
in the newspapers that the interested members in the Cabinet
have agreed that a national fuel policy shall be adopted.
Details of the proposed policy have not been disclosed but
the newspapers assume that the policy will include the ex-
ploitation of oil and coal resources in Japan and Manchuria;
a search for oil resources abroad; and the development of oil
substitutes within Japan.
As a first step toward the implementation of this policy,
according to the ASAHI of August 10, the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry desires to establish a fuel bureau independent
of the Ministry itself. The projected bureau will have as a
board of advisers the Chief of the Cabinet Investigation Bu-
reau, the Chief of the Bureau of Resources, Vice Ministers
of the Ministries concerned, some Chiefs of Bureaux, and
scholars and experts on fuel matters. The Bureau will have
a General Affairs Division and First and Second Divisions.
The General Affairs Division will handle general affairs, in-
vestigation and planning. The First Division will handle
resources, oil, and fuel utilization. The Second Division
will handle substitute fuels and alcohol.
In

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
In addition the OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI NICHI
of August 5, l936, reports that the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry will include in its budget for the next fiscal
year an unspecified sum for research work in connection with
the liquefaction of coal. The article states that the sum
will be used for the purpose of investigating the kinds and
qualities of coal available for liquefaction and the possible
amount of production. The fund will be used for investiga-
tions both in Japan and Manchuria.
The plans for the liquefaction of coal in Japan appear
to be progressing steadily. The newspapers report that a
company, which has been projected for some time past, to be
named the Manchuria Coal Liquefaction Industry Company, will
be established in Ssupingkai, Manchuria, on August 27, the
negotiations between the promoters and "Manchukuo" having
been concluded. The company will have a capital of Yen2,500,000
and intends to produce 10,000 tons of gasoline per annum. The
company will use coal from the Hsian Mine of the Manchuria
Colliery Company and will employ a hydrogenation process de-
veloped by an expert of the Japan Iron Manufacturing Company.
The gasoline produced by the company will be sold to the "Man-
chukuo" Petroleum Monopoly at 30 sen per gallon. The shares
of the company will be taken up by Japanese life insurance
companies, the Manchuria Colliery Company, the Manchuria Oil
Company, the Nippon Oil Company and the promoters. The com-
pany expects to start actual production some time in l937.
The authorities realize that the commercial production
of gasoline from coal will be unprofitable at the present
prioo

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5-
price of gasoline in Japan (about 50 sen per gallon) and,
therefore, are prepared to raise the price of gasoline to a
point where the production from coal will be made a commercial
PrOpOSition. The OSAKA MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI NICHI of
August 8, 1936, reports that the Finance Office has decided
to raise the price of gasoline in Japan either by imposing
a new consumption tax (rumored to be 5 sen per gallon) or
by raising the tariff on imported gasoline. The newspaper
states that the opinion of the authorities of the Finance
Office is that the projected national fuel policy should be
regarded as very important from a broad view of national de-
fense and that in order to develop the coal liquefaction in-
dustry it will be necessary to raise the price of gasoline.
It has also been reported in the newspapers that the
authorities are considering the introduction of a bill into
the nert session of the Diet compelling all dealers in gaso-
line to mix a certain proportion (reported to be 30%) of al-
cohol with all gasoline sold. In his conversation with the
local representatives of the foreign oil companies on August
11, however, Mr. Yoshino, the Vice Minister of Commerce and
Industry, stated that he did not believe, from the experience
of European countries, that the proposition was hopeful. He
thought that it would take five years for the alcohol pro-
ducers of Japan to reach a state of production where even 1o%
of alcohol could be mixed with the gasoline consumed. He also
thought that, assuming the cost of alcohol to be 90 sen per
gallon and the selling price of gasoline to be 60 sen per
gallon, the Government would have to subsidize alcohol to the
amount

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
amount of about 30 son per gallon. He also said that the
movement for the use of alcohol as fuel is political in
nature and that it is being advocated by the Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry which is anxious to promote the
welfare of the farmers.
In view of the fact that Japan possesses at present
very slender oil resources, it would seem that the efforts
of the authorities (more especially the authorities of the
defense forces), to make Japan independent of foreign sources
of liquid fuel, would be doomed to failure. In the light
of past experience with Japanese initiative and ability,
particularly in regard to iron and steel, synthetic fertilizers
and synthetic dyestuffs, however, the Embassy is not sure
that they will not succeed in considerable measure in the
course of time.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Grew

American Oil Interests in Japan.

THE FOREIGN SERVICE

AMERICAN EMBASSY
Tokyo, October 14, 1936.
SUBJECT: AMERICAN OIL INTERESTS IN JAPAN.
894.6363
894.636/294
EgL
sli
and natuction to
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
NOV3-1936
The Honorable
Department of State
The Secretary of State,
ner
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
FILED
290
No. 1989, dated August 20, 1936, and to previous corres-
1936
pondence on the subject of the difficulties being ex-
perienced in Japan by the American and other foreign oil
interests under the Petroleum Industry Law, and to report
the following developments in the situation.
The negotiations with the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, by
which the Standard and Shell interests hoped to arrange
the carrying of the non-commercial stocks required by the
law

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
92
- 2 -
law of their companies, progressed in a desultory manner
throughout the summer. The problem of the arrangements
under which the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha would carry the non-
commercial stocks was not easy of solution because of a
large number of details which it was necessary to work
out before a final arrangement could be concluded. These
details included such problems as the proportion of the
trade of the Standard and Shell interests to be turned
over to the Mitsui interests as partial compensation for
the carrying of the non-commercial stocks; whether such
trade should be determined as a percentage or as a fixed
annual amount; whether the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha should
conduct this trade on a commission basis or on a c.i.f.
basis (in the latter case assuming the business risks
incident to the trade); whether the share of the trade
turned over to Mitsui should be istributed through the
Standard and Shell dealers or through new dealers to be ap-
pointed by Mitsui; whether or not the present Japanese
agents of the Standard and Shell interests should parti-
cipate in the capitalization of the new stock carrying
company; and the period for which the foreign oil in-
terests should conclude a supply contract with the Mitsui
Bussan Kaisha. The largest problem, however, arose
from the question of whether the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha
should participate in the Standard and Shell trade as
a trading concern, taking the risk of all the variable
factors, such as the increase or decrease of consumption,
the increase or decrease of Government compensation for
stook-holding, the profitableness or unprofitableness of
the trade, etc., or whether Mitsui should act simply as

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
13
- 3 -
a money lending concern requiring a fixed rate of com-
pensation for its services in connection with the stock-
holding problem.
In the meentime both the foreign oil interests and
the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha were being pressed by the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry to hasten the negotiations, as
the Ministry of Commerce and Industry was in a difficult
position vis-a-vis other interested ministries and the press.
The foreign oil interests were approached on August 17,
August 24, and September 9, by the Ministry of Commeroe and
Industry, urging greater speed in the negotiations. Finally,
on September ll, the foreign oil companies were in-
formed that they must reach a definite settlement of the
question by September 20, and a hint was given by the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry to the effect that that
Ministry would be compelled to change its policy if a
settlement was not reached in the very near future, in-
timating that the present applications for additional
domestic refining licenses would be granted and that the
official attitude toward the foreign companies would be
changed to the disadvantage of the foreign companies.
The foreign oil interests were requested to show some
concrete progress towards the fulfillment of the stook-
holding requirement of the law in order to ease the
situation.
The Mitsui Bussan Kaisha had by this time taken the
stand that it did not wish to conclude an arrangement
which would put that concern in the position of a trading
concern, subject to the variable factors involved in
the trade. The company stated that it was willing to
invest the amount of Yen 17,000,000 to carry the three
months'

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.98)
14
- 4 -
months' non-commercial stocks for the foreign com-
panies and would accept as its remuneration the Govern-
ment compensation given for stock-holding plus an amount
of guaranteed profit on the trade to be turned over to
it by the Shell and Standard interests which would com-
pensate the company to the amount of 8 per cent per
annum on its investment. The LWitsui Bussan Kaisha, in
other words, took the position that it desired to be
simply a money lender, desiring guaranteed interest
at the rate of 8 per cent per annum on the investment
made. The foreign oil interests took the position that
they would turn over to the  itsui Bussan Kaisha as
partial compensation for the carrying of the non-com-
mercial stocks a fixed percentage of their trade in
Japan. With the expectation that the amount to be
carried in stock would be a fixed amount, of which
the proportion in relation to the amount of trade
would decrease annually as the trade naturally in-
creased, the foreign oil interests estimated that
the non-commercial stocks carried by Mitsui would
gradually diminish, having been taken over by the oil
companies as commercial stocks, and that eventually
the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha would no longer bé carrying
non-commeroial stocks and would have as a free gift
the fixed proportion of the trade turned over to that
concern by the foreign oil interests. According to
their caloulations, therefore, the foreign oil in-
terests considered that the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha
shoula

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.97)
9s
- 5 -
should acoept a lower rate on their investment in the
beginning with the expectation of a much higher rate
at a later time. The Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, however, re-
fused to consider this proposal and insisted upon thefr
proposition that the foreign oil companies guarantee
it a fired rate upon its capital investment, without
consideration of the variable factors involved in any
trading proposition, but agreed to reduce the interest
expected upon its investment to 7 1/2 per cent.
At this point a difference of opinion arose between
the Standard and Shell interests, the latter wishing
to adhere to the position that Mitsui should accept
the variables involved in a trading status, in order to
defend the principle that the foreign oil interests can-
not consent to pay for the carrying of non-commercial
stocks, even under some camouflaged arrangement, while
the Standard interests desired to safeguard their trading
position in Japan, paying the price therefor, rather than
to set up a third party in permanent trade at a sacrifice
in profits to the foreign companies. The Standard view
eventually was accepted by the Shell interests.
The Standard and Shell interests, however, did not feel
in a position to guarantee a fixed rate to the Mitsui Bussan
Kaisha while so many variable factors existed in their
Japanese trade. Consequently, before agreeing to the pro-
posal of the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha they requested that some
assurances be obtained from the Government in regard to
(1) the quantitative maximum which would be set my the
Government, in order that the foreign companies might
visualize

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.97)
7
- 6-
visualize what their share of the 7 1/2 per cent de-
manded by Mitsui would amount to; (2) some assurance
from the Government as to continuance of trade with a
share of the natural increase, as otherwise the foreign
oil interests could not be sure that they would have funds
at their disposal with which to pay the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha;
and (3) an assurance from the Government that prices would
be maintained by the Government, as it would obviously
be impossible for the foreign oil interests to pay com-
pensation to Mitsui when they were taking losses instead
of profits on their Japanese trade.
On September 19, therefore, representatives of the
Standard, Shell and Mitsui interests jointly called upon
the Vice Minister of Commerce and Industry who gave them
the following assurances:
1. The Government will give written assurance that the
rate of compensation to be paid by the Government for stock-
holding will not be altered unfavorably to the stock-holders.
2. No definite assurance can be given at the moment
in regard to the quantitative maximum of stock-holding
as such assurance cannot be given while the foreign com-
panies are in a position of law breakers. The Vice-
Minister stated, however, that it was his personal opinion
that the Government would fix a maximum to stook-holding
when all the companies had complied with the law, as the
Government realized the practical disabilities involved
in the increasing stook-holding obligations.
3. The Government will abide by the Five-Point
Memorandum of April, 1935, in regard to allocation of
a share of the natural increase in consumption to the
foroin

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.97)
22
- 7 -
forwign oil companies when the original idle refining
capacity in Japan will have been absorbed, unless in the
meantime the produotion of gasolene substitutes in Japan
will have equalled the amount of natural increase.
4. Reasonable prices will be maintained and the
importers will not be compelled to sell at unremunerative
prices.
The head offices of the foreign oil interests then
agreed to the arrangement with Mitsui Bussan Kaisha on
the basis of these assurances given by the Ministry of
Commeroe and Industry. No definite contract has yet
been signed between the foreign oil interests and the
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha but the verbal agreement so far
reached is along the following lines:
The Mitsui Bussan Kaisha will invest approximately
Yen 17,000,000 to erect tanks, partly on the oi1
companies' property, partly on the Mitsui Bussan kaisha
property and partly on property to be acquired by itsui,
and will purchase the oil required to be carried by the
companies from the Standard and shell interests. For
this service Litsui will require compensation at the rate
of 7 1/2 per cent per annum on the investment. A part of
this compensation will be derived from the compensation
paid by the Government for stock-holding, and the re-
mainder will be obtained from the foreign oil interests
in the form of commissions to be paid to mitsui for the
marketing by mitsui of a variable quantity of gasolene
and heavy oil each year, the gasolene and heavy oil to
be distributed primarily through the present agents or the
foreign oil companies. This arrangement is to continue
for

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.98)
28
- 8 -
for twenty years, during which period the mitsui Bussan
Kaisha will amortize the investment for tankage, unless
in the meantime the non-commercial stocks are taken over
as commercial stocks by the oil interests. If for any
business reason it should become necessary to terminete
the contraot before twenty years, a settlement will be
reached by negotiation and mutual agreement, but the foreign
oil companies will not be responsible for any loss incurred
by the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha if the foreign oil companies
are compelled to withdraw from Japan because of Governmental
action. Although a few minor points, such as the allowance
to be made to Mitsui Bussan Kaisha for leakage and for
earthquake insurance, are still being negotiated, the main
agreement has been reached as inaicated above.
If and when a final signed contract is concluded, the
Embassy will endeavor to obtain oopies for the Department's
files in order that it may have on record the details of
the conclusion of this protracted and troublesome dispute.
On October 9, 1936, the local representatives of the
Shell and Standard interests addressed a letter to the
Minister for Commerce and Industry, enumerating the oral
assurances which had been given them by the Vice Minister
on September 19, and asking the Minister to confirm these
assurances. The confirmation has not yet been received by
the oil interests, although there is little doubt that
the confirmation will be given in due course. The Standard
and Shell interests, however, wish a more formal con-
firmation to be obtained by the Embassies and it is expected
that they will request the American and British Embassies
to obtain some further confirmation through the Foreign
Offiee. The Embassy believes that this might be done in
tho

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.92)
7
the same manner as the confirmation of the Five Point
Momorandum of April, 1935, was obtained, namely, by pre-
senting a memorandum of the assurances to the Chief
of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign
Office, and asking for oral or written confirmation of the
memorandum. If the Department has any objection to this
procedure, telegraphic instructions are requested.
Respectfully yours,
UiDeikn
E.R. Dickover
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

Conversation:

Mr. Mackay.
Subject: Oil situation in Japan.
Mr. Coe described at some length the developments
leading up to the existing impasse in the negotiations
between the foreign oil interests and the Japanese Gov-
ernment (see attached memorandum of December l6 prepared
by Mr. Coe), with particular reference to the statements
contained in the attached copy of a telegram under date
December 15 roceived by the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
from Mr. P. W. Parker, who is now in London. Mr. Coe in
effect said that the refusal of the Japanese Government
FILED
to confirm in acceptable form the oral assurances repeat-
edly made to the foreign oil interests had, together
with certain other disturbing developments in the recent
past, raised grave doubts as to the good faith of the
Japanese Department of Commerce; that it was the unani-
mous opinion of the concerned American and British oil
F/FG
interests

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
interests and the American and British Ambassadors in
Tokyo that it would be useless to attempt to deal fur-
ther with the Japanese Department of Commerce and that
in consequence representations should be made to the
Japanese Ambassador in Washington by the Department of
State and to the Japanese Ambassador in London by the
British Foreign Office.
Following a frank and lengthy discussion of the
situation, with particular reference to the statements
made in Mr. Parker's telegram under reference and in
275
Ambassador Grew's telegrams No. 259 of December 14 and No.
296
261 of December 17, the opinion was reached by those
present, including Mr. Coe, that although it would seem
advisable that diplomatic representations be made to the
Japanese Government at this time it would be more expedi-
ent to have the conversations take place in Tokyo between
the American and British Ambassadors respectively and the
Japanese Prime Minister rather than, as suggested, in
Washington and London respectively. It was also the
opinion of those present that, as, among other reasons,
the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company is not at this time pre-
pared to state whether it will or will not withdraw from
Japan if its desires are not met by the Japanese Govern-
ment, the proposed representations should not be of too
strong a character and that the approach to the Japanese
Government

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
Government should take the form of an expression of anxi-
oty in regard to the future in Japan of the foreign oil
interests concerned and a request for (a) a clear indica-
tion that it is not the desire of the Japanese Government
to force the foreign oil companies to discontinue opera-
tions in Japan and (b) written confirmation of the oral
assurances already given to the foreign oil interests.
A telegram addressed to the American Embassy at
Tokyo in conformity with the decisions indicated above
was then drafted in which Ambassador Grew was authorized,
if after consultation he and his British colleague should
agree that the proposed line of approach is practicable
and if after referring the matter to his Government the
British Ambassador should receive authorization to pro-
ceed as indicated, to make with the British Ambassador
approximately simultaneous and substantially idontical
representations to the Japanese Prime Minister.
Mr. Coe, upon taking his departure, said in effect
that his report to Mr. Parker would state merely the fact
that the Department had requested Ambassador Grew to con-
fer with his British colleague along certain lines which
were deemed appropriate under existing circumstances.

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
December 16th, 1936.
Following the enactment of the Japan Petroleum
Industry Control Law, Mr. G.S. Walden, Chairman of the Board of
Directors, and Mr. P.W. Parker, President, of Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company, proceeded to Japan the early part of 1935 to discuss with
the responsible officials of the Japanese Government, the application
of this Law in respect t the operations of the Company they represent.
They were joined by Mr. Godber of the Shell interests.
311199
Discussions ensued with officials of the Department of
Commerce and the Foreign Office, with the result that there was drawn
up and submitted to the Japanese Foreign Office, that hich all parties
concerned now call the Five Point Memorandum. A copy of this is at-
tached, from which it will be observed that the representetives of
Standard-Vacuun il Company received definite assurances in respect to
future operations by that Company concerning the points involved. In
addition, it should be noted that assurance was extended that any ex-
penditures incurred by Stancard-Vacuum Oil Company would not be ren-
dered unprofitable. This point was in relation to expenditures neces-
sary in order to comply with the stockholding provisions of the Law.
Subsequently, and for reasons well known to the Japanese
Government, an arrangement was made by Standard-Vacuun Oil Company with
Mitsui & Company, for the creation by them of a Compeny for the purpose
of holding the former's non-commercial stock, obligutory as a result
of the stockholuing provision in this Law. This agreement met with the
approval of the Japanese Government, and Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
were prepared to undertake all the oblig&tions under it on the previous
assumption of the assurances applyin to the Five Point Memorandum.
At this juncture Standard-Vacuum Oil Company applied for
written assurances of the Five Point Memorandum. On November 24th, the
reply from the Japanese Government was:
"In regard to four items proposed in your letter, we will
consider them carefully in our future enforcement of the
Petroleum Law."
In view of previous negotiations, the recognition of the
Five Point Memorandum and other principles subsequently enumerated, it
is the opinion of Standard-Vacuum Oil Company that this reply from the
Japanese Government is not satisfactory and that they are entitled to
unqualified affirmation of the Five Point Memorandum and other prin-
ciples as evolved during the course of these negotiations which have
been proceeding for more than two years.
The principle of non-discrimination was accepted by the
authorities and is one of the points in the Five Point Memorandum. This
being so, the proposed tariff revision, which it is understood will be
presented at the next session of the Diet, involves heavy duty increases
on finished petroleum products, which in effect, will most seriously

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Dec. 16/36.
penalize the business of Standard-Vacuun Oil Company, and in its
opinion, is discriminatory against them inasmuch as it will place
the local Japanese refiners on a far more favorable cost basis than
the importers of finished products, such as Standard-Vacuun Oil Company.
Standard-Vacuun Oil Company protest in respect to the alloca-
tion of Kerosene Oil Quotas. At the beginning of i936, the Mining
Bureau of the Department of Commerce, after first depriving Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company of that volume of their Kerosene trade represented
by their quota for Light Oils for 1935, and of which a considerable
portion was done in imported oils, then estimated that the 1936 con-
sumption would be about 10% less then l935 end applied this cut pro
rata to both importers and Japanese refiners, but vitin the result
that in comparison vith the actual sales of i9F5, the Jupenese refin-
ers received small increases and Standard-Vacuum Oil Compeny suffered
a decrease. Recently when the Mining Bureau reelized that actual
consumption would exceed quotas eranted, they issued additional quotas
to the Japanese refiners snd did not permit Standard-Vacuum Oil Com-
pany to participate in the estimated increased volume of trude. As a
result Stondard-Vacuum Oil Company has been deprived of a lerge amount
of trade as compared with its 1935 actual sales, which is contrary to
No. 1 of the Five Point Memorandun and i: also discriminatory in action.
ATTACH.

Department's 160, DecemberA7, 2 0.m
636
One. I have discussed with my British colleague
the question of representations concerning the oil si tuation
22
along the lines suggested by the Department.
298
Two. In view of the attitude of the Department,
Clive will recommend to his Government that a further
diplomatic approach approximately simultaneous and sub-
stantially identical in character be made by us in
Tokyo. lie, however, feels very strongly that a further
approach of this nature would be ineffective and insuf-
ficient to secure results. During the past two yearswe
have repeatedly supported our oil companies by diplonit-
ic representations here with almost negative resuits. The
FLED
present Minister and Vice . inister for Foreign affairs
have probably never familiarized themselves with the
details of the situation and would merely pass on to the
Ministry of Commerce copies of the aides memoires which we
would present. In the Ministry of Commerce the matter
F/G
is handled by an official who is known to be anti-foreign
in

=== Page 321 === (confidence 1.00)
U -2- #264, Dec. 19, 6 p.m. from Tokyo
in his attitude while the new Vice Minister is less
helpfully inclined than was his predecessor Yoshino.
The polite written inquiry (sent after oral assurances
had been given) from the oil companies in regard to assur-
ances for future trade evoked a reply of which the Japanese
text was offensive in tone. Clive, therefore, feels that
the time has come for a new method of procedure and be-
lieves that our representations here would be given em-
phasis if a day or two prior to our representations, the
anxiety of our two Governments regarding the future in
Japan of the foreign oil interests and their desire that
assurances as to the intentions of the Japanese Government
be given in writing were appropriately presented to the
Japanese Ambassadors in Washington and London.
Three. fter thenost careful consideration I incline
to the opinion that representations from both ends of the
line would serve to emphasize the unsatisfactory nature
of the situation.
Four. Our recommendations for "strong representa-

Six. As we are now on the threshold of the holidays

Sir:
ASSISIANT SFCRETARY
DEP
With reference to this Embassy's despatch No. 1989,
detd August 20, 1936, and to previous correspondence
OF ST
Japanese Government to find sources of liquid fuel
outside the control of the United States and Great Bri-
FH
tain, I now have the honor to report the following de-
19371
velopments in the situation.
Hydrogenation of Coal.
Several projects have been announced during recent
months for the establishment of plants in Japan and
Manchuria for the hydrogenation of coal and the production
thereby of synthetic gasoline and fuel oil. The Japanese
Navy
11

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Navy Ministry, which has been experimenting for years
on processes for the hydrogenation of coal, has now,
according to the press, developed a process which is
believed to be commercially feasible and has entrusted
the industrial development of this process to the South
Manchuria Railway Company. A plant to produce gasoline
and fuel oil on a commercial scale from coal is now un-
der construction by the Railway Company at Tokuyama,
Yamaguchi Prefecture, within the precincts of the Naval
Fuel Depot. The annual production is not stated, but
the press reports that the plan is to expand the plant
gradually until the Japanese deficiency in oil has been
overcome.
The Mitsui Bussan Kaisha has purchased the Japan
rights to the German Fischer process for the liquefac-
tion of coal and is reported to be planning to erect two
plants, one near its coal mines in the Hokkaido and one
in Kyushu. The machinery for one of these plants has al-
ready been ordered, mostly from Germany, although some
parts are to be built in Japan. The cost of the plant has
not been published, but the rumored capacity of the plant
already ordered is 30,000 tons of gasoline per annum.
It has also been reported in the press that the Nip-
pon Sangyo Kabushiki Kaisha (the Kuhara-Aikawa interests)
have formed a combination of coal mining interests for the
purpose of establishing a company, capitalized at Yen
20,000,000, for the liquefaction of coal and the produotion
of gasoline. This combination is understood to be negotia-
ting for the I. G. process, controlled by the International
Hydrogenation

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
Hydrogenation Patents Company of Holland, part ownership
of which is held by the Standard Oil Company of New Jersey.
Two plants for the liquefaction of coal by hydrogena-
tion are under construction or planned in Manchuria. The
South Manchuria Railway Company, it is re orted, has de-
cided to build a plant at a cost of Yen 14,000,000 at
Fushun (where the great open-cut coal mines are located),
presumably using the Navy process. Work was supposed to
have been started on the plant this year and will be com-
pleted in June, 1938. The output of the plant will consist
of about 20,O00 tons of gasoline and other oils per annum.
The Manchuria Coal Liquefaction Industry Company (A Manchu-
rian legal entity with Japanese capital), with a capital
of Yen 5,000,000, is reported to have started work on a
coal liquefaction plant at Ssupingkai, in Manchuria. This
company will use a coal liquefaction process developed by
Mr. C. Kuroda, of the Yawata Steel Works, and plans to pro-
duce about ll,ooo tons of gasoline and other petroleum sub-
stitutes per annum.
Under the plans for self-sufficiency in liquid fuel
evolved by the National Fuel Commission, the Department
of Commerce and Industry will introduce into the coming
session of the Diet a bill providing for the formation of
a semi-official company with a nominal capital of Yen
100,000,000, half of which is to be subscribed by the
Government. The company will also be authorized to issue
debentures up to Yen 200,000,000 or Yen 300,000,000, and
provision will be made for the company to increase its
capital sufficiently to permit total expenditures during
seven

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
seven years of seven or eight hundred million yen. This
company will take over and control all the processes used
in Japan for the liquefaction of coal and will license and
finance private companies to operate witn those processes.
In other words, the semi-official com any is to be a hold-
ing company to control and finance all coal liquefaction
operations in Japan. The objective of the scheme is to
produce 1,000,000 tons of gasoline and 1,000,000 tons of
heavy oil from coal per annum, under a seven-year develop-
ment plan. The Government is to guarantee the company di-
vidends at the rate of five per cent per annum. In view
of the existing shortage of funds in Japan for the financing
of military expansion, however, it is problematical if this
ambitious scheme for securing a supply of liquid fuel for
Japan will succeed in obtaining the approval of the Diet.
It will be observed that the combined total of the out-
puts of the various projects for the liquefaction of coal,
including those building and those merely planned, but ex-
cluding the enormous Government scheme, is less than 100,000
tons per annum. As Japan uses something over 2,000,000 tons
of petroleum and petroleum products per annum, the output
of the coal liquefaction plants cannot be considered as of-
fering any great menace to the American trade with Japan in
petroleum products. Moreover, it is believed in oil circles
that the Japanese Government is unduly optimistic in its
scheme to produce 1,000,000 tons of gasoline and 1,000,000
tons of heavy oil from coal within seven years.
A1coho1.
As another step in the Government's plan (which will be
described later) for securing self-sufficiency in liquid
fuel, the Finance Ministry has announced that it will

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
introduce a bill in the coming session of the Diet for
the formation of an alcohol manufacture and sales mono-
poly. The project will be developed under a seven-year
plan and the total capital outlay is expected to amount
to Yen 490,000,000. The Government will set up seven
fuel alcohol manufacturing plants during the first year,
to produce about 23,000 tons of alcohol. The number of
plants will be increased gradually through the seven-year
period, reaching 80 in the last year, with an estimated
production of 267,000 tons of fuel alcohol. From the
fiscal year 1938-39 the Government plans to compel all
users of gasoline to mix a certain percentage of fuel al-
cohol with the gasoline. Efforts will be made to lower
the cost of fuel alcohol, so that the mixture can be sold
at about 55 sen (15.6 cents) per gallon. The Government
will monopolize manufacture and sales of straight alcohol,
and will monopolize sales of "shochu" (a sort of Japanese
brandy), although existing manufacturers of "shochu" will
be permitted to continue operations under license. Profits
from the monopoly are expected to amount to Yen 31,000,000
per annum after the first year. This revenue will be used
to cover probable losses on the manufacture of fuel alcohol.
This scheme, like that for the Government control of
coal liquefaction, presupposes the ability of the Government
to obtain large sums for capital expenditure - a doubtful
factor in the situation.
National Fuel Policy.
In August last it was reported that the National Fuel
Commission (composed of officials of six Ministries
interested

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
interested in the problem) had adopted a general plan
for the realization of a basic national fuel policy, the
plan having received the approval of the Cabinet. Funda-
mentalsof the plan were reported to be as follows:
1. Companies engaged in the liquefaction of coal or
the conversion into gasoline of tar obtained from the low-
temperature distillation of coal to be exempted from in-
come, business profits ana business taxes and to receive
subsidies.
2. A fuel alcohol monopoly system to be established,
in order to supply uel alcohol at low prices.
3. A law to be promulgated compelling users of gaso-
line to mix fuel alcohol with gasoline, in the proportion
of not over 20 per cent of alcohol.
4. The Government to establish a model plant for the
liquefaction of coal at a cost of Yen 10,000,000, using
the processes evolved by the Fuel Research Institute of
the Mi istry of Comnerce and Industry.
5. The Government to amalgamate and unify the existing
laboratories and institutes for research into the problem
of the liquefaction of coal.
6. The Government to raise the import duty on gasoline
and adopt a gasoline consumption tax, to assist the establish-
ment of industries for the manufacture of oil substitutes.
7. The Government to set up a special bureau for all
administrative work in regard to fuel.
The recent enthusiaam for the establishment of plants
for the liquefaction of coal appears to have been caused
by the first item of this plan, while the proposed bill
for the creation of an alcohol monopoly undoubtedly arises
from

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
from the second and third items of the plan.
The Japanese Government is certainly proceeding
steadily with its plans to acquire independent domestic
sources of liquid fuel, in order to be self-sufficient in
this regard in time of war. Progress will necessarily be
slow and expensive, but Japenese success in developing
other key industries under adverse conditions inclines the
Embassy to believe that eventually a certain degree of in-
dependence from foreign supplies of liquid fuel will be
attained.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

patah No. 2076, dated October 14, 1936, and to previous
M
porrespondence on the subject of the difficulties being
experienced under the Japanese Petroleum Industry Law
by the American and other foreign oil interests, and
to report the following developments in the situation:
Assurances

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Assuranoes.
As was reported in despatch No. 2076 the local rep-
resentatives of the Standard and Shell interests on Ootober 9,
l936, addressed a letter to the Minister for Comnerce and
Industry, requesting confirmation of the assurances given
orally by Mr. S. Yoshino, Vice Minister for Commerce and
Industry. (Mr. Yoshino in the meantime had resigned to
accept another post.) The officials of the Ministry of
Commerce and Industry refused to accept the letter, in-
sisting that the assurances requested were too detailed
and specific; that they could give no assurances in re-
gard to the companies' trade in Chosen and Taiwan, as those
colonies were administered by a different Department of
the Government; and that they could not give & definite
assurance in regard to the allocation of any decrease in
consumption, as that point had not been discussed at the
conferences which resulted in the formulation of the five-
point memorandum of April 13, 1935. Lengthy discussions
then ensued between the local representatives of the for-
eign oil interests and the officials of the Ministry of
Commeroe and Industry, as a result of which it was finally
agreed, in regard to the first objeotion, that the foreign
oil companies should submit a letter asking only for ab-
stract assurances, this letter to be confirmed in writing
by the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, and that the
companies should incorporate their understanding of the
detailed administration of the assurances in a later
letter, which would not be confirmed by the Ministry of
Commeroe and Industry. Much the same procedure was to
be followed in regard to the assurances conoerning the
companies'

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
companies' trade in Chosen and Taiwan; a letter was to
be addressed to the Ministry of Commerce and Industry
requesting the good offices of that Ministry in obtaining
assurances concerning Chosen and Taiwan similar to those
given for Jepan Proper. The point in regard to the allo-
cation of any possible future decrease in consumption was
more difficult of settlement but a compromise was finally
reached, the foreign oil companies agreeing to accept the
wording "consideration will be given so that the decrease
is apportioned fairly in the light of actual conditions at
the time".
The difficulties having been ironed out, on Novem-
ber ll the local representatives of the Standard and
Shell interests submitted the revised version of their
original letter in regard to assurances and a supple-
mentary letter requesting the good offices of the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry in regard to their trade in Chosen
and Taiwan, dating both letters October 9, l936, the date
of the original letter. Copies of the English versions
1/2
of these letters are enclosed. It will be observed that
the letters are most courteously and respectfully worded
and contain a simple request that confirmation be given
of the four assurances, which briefly are as follows:
1. The foreign oil companies will be granted
their l935 allocation as a minimum and the
natural increase will be allocated fairly.
Should there be a decrease in consumption,
such decrease will be apportioned fairly in
the light of actual conditions at the time.
2. Market prices will be based on costs and con-
sidered fairly in order that the foreign oil
companies may sell at remunerative prices.
3.

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
3. The foreign oil companies will be accorded
the same treatment as other petroleum in-
dustrialists in Japan.
4.
The present rates and extent of compensation
for stock-holding will not be altered unfavor-
ably to stockholders.
In reply to this letter the local representatives of
the Standard and Shell interests received letters dated
November 21, 1936, signed by the direotor of the Mining
Bureau of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry. An
English translation of the letter received by the local
3/
representative of the Standard interests is enclosed here-
with. The Japanese text of the letter is couched in dic-
tatorial and almost offensive language, it having been
described by a student of Japanese as "the language a
Japanese policeman would use in speaking to an offender".
The letter scolds the foreign oil interests for their de-
lay in performing their stock-holding duty and curtly
orders them to speed up operations for stock-holding. In
regard to the assurances the letter simply states "we
will consider them carefully in our future enforcement
of the Petroleum Industry Law".
The representatives of the oil companies, immediate-
ly upon receipt of this letter, conferred with their
respective Embassies, which agreed that the confirmation
given was inacceptable as a basis for future trading
in Japan. At the advice of the Embassies, the local
representatives of the oil companies promptly took the
matter to Mr. Yoshino, the former Vice Minister for Com-
meree and Industry, wno had given the assurances orally,
and

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
and had stated that they would be confirmed in writing.
Mr. Yoshino readily agreed to endeavor to obtain suoh
assurances as the companies would consider satisfactory.
It is known that he discussed the matter with officials of
the Ministry of Commerce and Industry but his efforts did
not avail to alter the stand taken by that Ministry. The
difficulties apparently arise largely from the fact that
there have been several changes in the personnel of the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry within the past two
months and that some of the new officials, especially Mr.
Sakai, the Chief of the Fuel Section of the Bureau of
Mining Industry, are not well disposed toward foreign
business interests.
While these discussions and exchange of letters
were proceeding, certain events transpired which caused
the local representatives of the foreign oil companies
to doubt the substantiality, under the present adminis-
tration of the Petroleum Industry Law, of the oral as-
surances given in good faith by Mr. Yoshino, then Vice
Minister for Commerce and Industry. The first circum-
stance concerned the kerosene quotas for the latter part
of 1936 (erroneously given as 1937 in the Embassy's tele-
295
gram No. 259, December 14, 1 p.m.). When the kerosene
quotas were first allotted for the year the Japanese
authorities estimated that there would be a considerable
decrease in consumption and consequently the foreign oil
companies' quotas were reduced below their l935 quotas by
13,180 kilolitres, or 8,854 kilolitres less than the
actual trade done by the two companies in l935. Later,
the

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
the authorities found that their estimates had been
too low and that instead of a decrease there would be
an increase in consumption of kerosene. They therefore
decided to make a further allotment of 10,700 kilolitres,
but this allotment was given entirely to the Japanese
refiners.
 As a result, the Japanese refiners have
quotas of 3,200 kilolitres in excess of thcir 1935 quotas
while the foreign companies have quotas of 13,180 kilo-
litres below their 1935 quotas. As can be seen, this is
in direct contradiction of the first assurance quoted
above, both as regards the guaranteed minimum gallonage
and the allocation of the naturel increase.
The second circumstance concerns the proposed re-
vision of the import tariff on petroleum and petroleum
products which, according to the newspapers, was approved
by the Tariff Investigation Commission on December 22.
The revised tariff increases the duty on gasoline by
approximately five sen per gallon and elso increases
the duty on the gasoline content of crude oil imported
for refining purposes by about the same amount. Os-
tensibly, therefore, the revised tariff should give no
advantage to the refiners over the importers of gaso-
line. In actual praotice, however, there is a certain
amount of disorimination due to the fact that the duties
are also increased on certain by-products of the refining
processes, including kerosene, neutral oil, Diesel oil,
fuel oil, lubricating oil, and asphalt, imported in their
pure state. An equivalent increase was not made in the
content of these by-products in the orude oil imported
for refining purposes. The domestic refiners, therefore,
w111

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
will have an advantage equivalent to the increased duty
on these by-products. Under the present tariff the re-
finers have an advantage over the importers of approximate-
ly 3.61 per 100 gallons of crude oil refined, while un-
der the proposed tariff they will have an advantage of
¥5.16 per l00 gallons (these calculations are based on
Kettleman crude oil, the principal oil used for refining
purposes in Japan.) The giving of this increased ad-
vantage to the refiners is in contradiction of assur-
ance No. 3 and also in contradiction of an oral assur-
ance given by the former Vice Minister for Commerce and
Industry on September 19, l936, to the effect that the
foreign oil companies would not be discriminated against
in any future increase in the tariff rates on petroleum
and petroleum products.
Moreover, on December 17, 1936, the ASAHI SHIMBUN
reported that the Japanese oil refining companies are
conducting an agitation against the granting of further
increases in sales quotas by the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry to the foreign oil companies. This agi-
tation is based on the allegation that the prinoipal
purpose of the petroleum industry law is to protect
the Japanese oil industry and that therefore the sales
quotas granted to the foreign companies should not be
inoreased, and on the ground that the foreign oil compan-
ies have as yet failed to fulfill their duty in regard
to oil storage, while the Japanese refiners have fulfilled
their obligations in this regard. The newspaper also
stated that the question may become an issue in the
forthcoming session of the Diet.
Faoed

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
Faced with this apparent disorimination and oppo-
sition, the Standard and Shell interests decided to
request their respective Embassies to take the matter
up with the Japanese Government through diplomatic
ohannels.
Representations.
As a result of conferences between the local
representatives of the Standerd and Shell interests
and the American and British Embassies, it was de-
cided on December 14 to ask the Department of State
and the British Foreign Office for instructions in
regard to representations. The British Ambassador
felt very strongly that the time had come for strong
representations and even suggested going to the length
of accusing the Japanese Government of bad faith. He
proposed that we cable to Washington and London res-
pectively to recommend that our officials at home call
in the Japanese Ambassadors in Washington and London to
express the anxiety of our Governments in regard to the
future in Japan of our respective oil interests; to ask
for a categorical statement of the intentions of the
Japanese Government, as the suspicion cannot be avoided
that it is the wish of the Japanese Government to force
the foreign oil interests out of Japan; and to submit
a request that if the Japanese Government does not wish
to force the foreign oil interests out of Japan the
written assurances required by the oil interests in
order to assure them of a reasonable volume of trade
and

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
and a reasonable profit be given them by the Japanese
Government.
This Embassy concurred in the British Ambassador's
judgment to the extent that it believed that the time
had come for more effective representations than had
been made in the past. The Embassy, however, did not
believe in preferring charges of bad faith as such
representations might place the Japanese Government in
a position where it could grant no concessions without
being accused by the Japanese public of submitting to
foreign pressure, to the detriment of the interests of
the foreign oil companies. I believed, however, that
we should present the situation to the Japanese Govern-
ment with marked emphasis in order to avoid the prob-
ability that our representations would, as formerly, be
treated in a routine way and merely passed on to the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry for such comment as
might be made by the junior official handling oil matters
in that Ministry. Moreover, as the Minister for Foreign
Affairs was preoccupied with the troublous domestic and
foreign political complications, it seemed to us that
the emphasis which we felt should be placed on our rep-
resentations could better be secured by presenting the
case to the Japanese Ambassadors at Washington and London
by the Department of State and the British Foreign Of-
fice respectively.
296
Upon receipt of the Department's telegram No. 160,
December 17, 2 p.m., I immediataly discussed the Depart-
ment's

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
ment's suggested procedure with Sir Robert Clive and
asked him to seek from his Government authorization to
make the representations in Tokyo desired by the Depart-
ment. The Ambassador agreed to recommend to his Govern-
ment that a further diplomatic approach be made in Tokyo
substantially identical in character and approximately
simultaneous with that to be made by us. At the same
time, however, he continued of the opinion that our rep-
resentations in Tokyo should be supported by representa-
tions to the Japanese Ambassadors in Washington and London.
In the meantime the foreign oil companies approached
their respective embassies with a request that action on
the representations be expedited, as they feared that the
foreign oil companies would be penalized by having their
l937 sales quotas reduoed because they had not fulfilled
their oil storage obligation and they therefore desired
that diplomatic steps be taken before Christmas to fore-
stall such penalties. They also held the hope that dip-
lomatio representations might forestall the anticipated
approval by the Tariff Investigation Commission of the
proposed revision of the petroleum import tariff men-
tioned above. This explains the element of haste in
the matter.
On December 23 the British Ambassador received his
instructions to concert with me and to proceed to joint
representations to the Japanese Government at the earliest
possible moment. The telegram also stated that Mr. Eden,
the British Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs, agreed
with the views of the Department of State in regard to
the representations. The telegram added that Mr. Eden
had

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
had taken the opportunity of an interview with the
Japanese Ambassador at London to draw his attention
to the difficult position in which the British oil in-
terests find themselves as a result of the failure on
the part of the Japanese Government to fulfill its
promises in regard to the future trading position in
Japan of the foreign oil companies.
The Department's instructions arrived on Decem-
ber 24. Members of the staffs of the American and
British Embassies immediately conferred and agreed
upon substantially identical (not joint) representa-
tions containing the points brought out in the Depart-
1296
ment's telegram No. 160,
that is, to express the anxiety
of our Governments in regard to the future in Japan of
our respective oil interests; to request a clear indi-
cation from the Japanese Government that it is not the
desire of that Government to force our respective oil
interests out of Japan; and to make an express request
that the Japanese Government give in writing the assur-
anoes which the oil interests seek in order to continue
operations in Japan.
The British Embassy embodied its representations in
the form of a Note Verbale, including therein a brief
history of the situation as well as a reference to the
charges of discrimination preferred by the oil interests
against the Japanese Government and terminating the note
with an expression of anriety and requests for assurances
for the future. This Embassy decided to communicate
orally to the Minister for Foreign Affairs the history
of

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
of the case and to embody in an Aide Memoire the
specific desiderata. In my interview with the Min-
ister at four o'clock that afternoon, very shortly
after the Department's telegram had been decoded and
with little time to prepare the final document, I
sketched to him orally the background of the position
in which the Standard Oil interests find themselves
in Japan, presenting the case as a purely business prob-
lem. I then read to him and left with him our Aide
41
Memoire (copy enclosed) containing the points outlined
270
in the Department's telegram No. 160.
In dealing with this situation, the Department's
wish to avoid at this juncture what might be consid-
ered as "strong representations" was carefully kept
in mind. My talk with the Minister for Foreign Affairs
was conducted in all friendliness and no tone or ex-
pression of recrimination entered into my presentation.
The difficulties encountered by our oil interests were
explained simply as a business problem and the reason-
ableness of their requests was brought out as a matter
of simple logic. The fact that the friendly and cordial
attitude and manner of Mr. Arita throughout the inter-
view underwent no change after the oral representations
and the reading of our Aide Memoire, which was enunciated
slowly and clearly so that he could not fail to grasp
its tone and purport, indicated that he took no exception
to either the one or the other. He merely observed
that he himself was not familiar with the matter, which
he thought had been settled some time ago, and that he
woula

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
would study it and would give the Embassy a reply as
soon as possible.
In our Aide Memoire the Department's points were
clearly presented. It would have been idle and futile
to have rendered them less concise by endeavoring to
soften the phraseology of the document which was in
effect merely a written enumeration and confirmation of
the desiderata orally presented in the interview.
My British colleague made his representations on
the same day shortly before my own and he left with
the Minister his Note Verbale of which a copy of en-
5/
olosed. It will be observed that the points brought
out in the concluding paragraph of his note are sub-
stantially identical with those expressed in our own
Aide Memoire.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.
