Your Excellency:
With reference to our letter addressed to the Mining
Bureau of Your Excellency's Department on 26th June, we
beg to confirm that our Companies were able on 22nd Septem-
ber to come to an agreement with Messrs. The Mitsui Bussan
Kaisha on the basic points of a special arrangement whereby
the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha will co-operate with us for the
performance of the stock-holding obligations of the Fetro-
leum Industry Law. Steps to this end were begun immediately
and are being pursued actively and, in assuring Your Excellency
that it will be achieved as soon as possible, we would res-
pectfully request Your Excellency to accept our apologies
and to favour us with special consideration in regard to this
delay in the fulfillment of the obligation.
At the same time we wish to express to Your Excellency
our appreciation and thanks for the consideration which has
already been given to our Companies, and also respectfully
request Your Excellency to confirm our understanding that
we may have the following assurances in regard to our busi-
ness during such time as the petroleum industry is under
legislative control:-
1. So long as there is no decrease in consumption,
sales quotas will be allocated as follows, subject
to

- 2 -
to preference being iven to increase in indigenous
production (that is, domestic production of petroleum
and manufacture of petroleum substitutes):-
(a) Cur Companies will be granted as a minimum their
1935 gallonage
(b) Any natural increase will be allocated fairly,
due consideration bein tiven tc traders' established
position and acquired interests, and until the idle
refining capacity as at the time of the enforcement
of the Fetroleum Industr: Law is taken up the al-
location to Importers of the said increase will be
not less favorable than the (said) allocation in
1935.
In the event of the anount of trade available for allo-
cation being reduced by reason of a decrease in consump-

will be given so that the decrease is apportioned fairly
in the light of actual conditions at the time.
II.
That market prices will be based upon costs and considered
fairly by the Government so that our Companies can sell
at remunerative prices.
III.
That our Companies will be accorded the same treatment
as other petroleum industrialists in Japan.
IV.
That the present rates and extent of compensation for
the cost of stock-holding will not be altered unfavorably
to stockholders.

Haruhiko Ogai, Director of the Mine

and Industry Ministry.
Mr. C.E. Moyer, Representative of
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company.
You have reported on Oot. 9th jointly signed with T.
G. Ely, Representative Director of Rising Sun Sekiyu Kabu-
shiki Kaisha regarding the performance of petroleum stock-
holding duty that you require a considerable time more for
the perfect performance of your six months' supply holding
as a matter of fact, but as your delay in the performance
of the said duty is a violation of the Petroleum Industry
Law, whioh can not be tolorated, we would like you to
speed up your preparations for the stock-holding and
perfectly perform the holding of six months' supply as soon
as possible. We also request you to let us know promptly
when you expect to complete the stock-holding and what pre-
parations and plan you are making for the stock-holding.
We request you the above by order.
In addition to the above, as for the four item desires
you expressed in your letter, we will consider them care-
fully in our future enforcement of the Petroleum Industry
Law as well as the Regulations for the Grant of Petroleum
Stock-Holding Aids.

from the Embassy at Tokyo.
AIDE MEMOIRE
The American Government feels impelled to express
to the Japanese Government its serious anxiety in regard
to the future of the American oil interests in Japan, as
represented by the Standard Vacuum Oil Company, and to ask
for a clear indication from the Japanese Goverment of its
intentions in regard to those interests. In view of the
present procedure of the Japanese Government in administer-
ing its petroleum policy, which hes led to doubts as to
those intentions, the American Goverrment would welcome a
substantial indication that it is not the intention or
desire of the Japanese Government to drive the American
oil interests out of Japan. The American Government elso
requests that the American oil interests be given such
written assurances in regard to their future trading
position in Japan as they may seek in order to continue
their business in this country upon e reasonably secure
and profitable basis.
December 24, 1936.

to despatch No
of December 28 , 1936, from the Bmbassy
at Tokyo.
Note Verbale.
His Britannic Majesty's Ambassador presents
his compliments to His Imperial Japanese Majesty's
Minister for Foreign Affairs and under instructions
from his Government has the honour to request His
Excellency's good offices in respect of certain
difficulties which have lately arisen out of the
application of the Petroleum Industry Law to the
undertakings of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company in
Japan.
This Company in 1934 appealed to the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry for the aileviation of certain
hardships imposed upon them by the Law, and in January
1935 despatched a Director to Japan to put their case
before the competent authorities.
As the result of protracted discussions, an
understanding was reached as to certain points upon
which they desired to be assured before making further
capital investment in Japan. This understanding was
recorded in a document which, though it may not have
constituted a formal undertaking by the Japanese
Government, was stated by the Director of the Commercial
Bureau in a conversation with the Commercial Counsellor
of this Embassy on April 25th, 1935, of the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs to represent substantially the intentions
of the competent authorities.
In

=== Page 367 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
In subsequent discussions with the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry, the Company earnestly
endeavoured to devise a scheme which, while relieving
them of certain direct capital investment that they
were not in a position to undertake, enabled them to
fulfil their obligations as to stockholding under the
Petroleum Law. Such a soheme was at length completed,
and the Company were ready to put it into operation as
soon as they should be satisfied that their trading
prospects in Japan were of a nature to justify the
increased expenditure which it involved. They had
already received from the Vice-Minister of Commerce
oral assurances on five cardinal points in this connec-
tion, and it only remained ror them to obtain some
agreed documentary record of those points, in order to
guard against future misunderstanding which might arise
whether from changes of personnel or from other causes.
At an interview on September 19th, 1936, the Vice-
Minister expressed his satisfaction at the outcome of
the discussions and his readiness to confirm the above-
mentioned assurances in riting. The Company thereupon
addressed to the Minister of Commerce and Industry a
letter reporting that they had made a special arrangement
for the performance of their obligations under the
Petroleum Industry Law, and begging the Minister to
confirm their understanding of their future trading
position in Japan.
To

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
To this request they received a reply in
which they were strongly reprimanded for delay in
fulfilling their obligations and, as to the points
on which they desired assurance, were merely told
by way of postscript that these would be taken into
consideration. In other words, after negotiations
lasting over two years, the Company found themselves
in greater doubt than before, seeing that they were
denied the very reasonable degree of assuranco which
they had conridently anticipated in view of oral
statements re eatedly made to them in the course of
their discussions with officials of the Ministry of
Comerce.
Their doubts have been further increased
by the announcement of new trading quotas (for
kerosene) and new import tariff rates which, they
state, are incompatible ith those assurances.
In view of the circumstances recited above,
His Majesty's Government cannot but feel grave anxiety
as to the future of British oil interests in Japan and
are constrained to ask the Japanese Government for a
clear indication of their intentions in regard to
those interests. It is difficult for His Majesty's
Government to avoid the suspicion that the Ja anese
Government's petroleum policy, as at present adminis-
tered, involves discrimination against British oil
interests and will result in driving them out of Japan.
H1s

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
His Majesty's Government would be gratified
to learn that this is not the desire or the intention
of the Japanese Government; and, if it is not their
intention, His Majesty's Government request that the
British oil interests may be given suchh written
assurances in re~ard to their future trading osition
as they may reasonably require, in order to continue
their operations in Japan upon a secure and profitable
basis.
December 24th, 1936.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 16, 1937.
Reference Tokyo's telegram No. 6 of
January 15, 6 p. m., in regard to the oil
situation in Japan,--
For sometime past it has been my
understanding that, except for the ironing
out of minor details, all that was necessary
to clear the way for a mutually satisfactory
settlement of the difficulties encountered in
Japan by the foreign oil interests was the
receipt from the Japanese Government of a
written confirmation of oral assurances
previously given.
Tokyo's telegram under reference would
seem to indicate that satisfactory confirma-
tion has been iven and it is hoped that
this long-drawn-out controversy will soon
be removed from the realm of diplomatic
negotiation.
No action required.

Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's despatch No. 2194,
dated December 28, 1936, and to previous correspondence,
written and telegraphic, in regard to the difficultigs
FILED
G
being experienced in Japan by the American and othert
1
foreign oil interests under the Petroleum Industry
1939
Law, I have the honor to report the following develop-
ments.
Korean Quotas.
As was briefly explained to the Department in the
Embassy's telegram No. 3, January 8, 7 p.m., the sales
quotas
FEG

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.99)

The quotas of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company were cut to
an equal degree, but the quotas of the Chosen Oil Company
(which has refineries in Korea) were increased by an aver-
age of 69.5% for the same period. In conversations which
were held later between representatives of the foreign
oil companies and the officials in Korea, it developed that
the quotas were allocated as above because the officials
estimated that a coal liquefaction plant in Korea would
come into production in 1937 and would produce some 22,600
kilolitres of gasoline. Room had to be made for this pro-
duction, and consequently the quotas of all importing oil
companies were cut accordingly. At the same time, however.
the natural increase in consumption (which is estimated as
larger than the production of the coal liquefaction plant)
was allotted entirely to the Chosen Oil Company on the ground
that that firm is an enterprise confined to Korea and hence
unable to expand elsewhere. As a result of this process of
reasoning

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
reasoning the foreign firms had to bear the bulk of
the reduction in quotas caused by the necessity of making
room for the synthetic gasoline, while the Japanese refineries
in Korea received greatly increased quotas.
The local representatives of the American and British
oil interests thereupon suggested tc their respective
Embassies that these reductions in the Korean quotas might
well be used as illustrations to strengthen the formal
representations made by the American and British Ambassadors
on December 24, 1936, on the general subject of our re-
spective oil interests in Japan. On January 9, 1937,
accordingly, Mr. Dickover, First Secretary of the Embassy,
called on Mr. Matsushima, Chief of the Bureau of Com-
mercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, recalled to his
attention the representations made on December 24, re-
cited briefly the facts of the Korean situation, and
stated that these developments were most disheartening
to the American oil interests, who were finding it im-
possible either to invest sums of money in Japan or to
assure an adequate return to such Japanese capital as
they might utilize when their future was as uncertain as
was indicated by this latest occurrence. Mr. Matsushima
agreed heartily and proceeded to discuss the general
situation in which the foreign oil interests found them-
selves in Japan. A memorandum of the conversation is
1/
enclosed. Mr. Matsushima later stated to Mr. Dickover
that he was looking into the matter of the Korean quotas
(although this Embassy had made no direct representations on
that

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
that subjeot, treating the matter orally and informally
and simply as an illustration of the treatment to which
the foreign oil companies were subjected in the Japenese
Empire)but as yet he has not transmitted any information
on the subject to this or the British Embassy.
Sir George Sansom, the Commercial Counsellor of the
British Embassy, also approached Mr. Matsushima informally
on the matter of the Korean quotas at about the same time
as the approach made by Mr. Dickover.
Under date of January 14, 1937, the foreign oil
companies sent a formal protest to the Governor General
of Korea in regard to the cut in their quotas. It will
not be necessary further to burden the Department's files
with copies of this letter. At the same time the local
representatives of the oil interests suggested to their
respective Embassies that the Embassies file with Mr.
Matsushima of the Foreign Office copies of this protest.
The British Ambassador agreed with me, however, that it
might be inadvisable for the Embassies further to press
the Foreign Office and the local representatives were
therefore advised to take up the matter with Mr. Matsu-
shima directly. This they did on January 20, at which
time Mr. Matsushima discussed with them the whole question
of their position in Japan. A copy of a memorandum of
2/
their conversation with Mr. Matsushima is enclosed.
In this connection, however, it will be remembered
that the foreign oil interests have at no time received
a definite assurance from the Japanese Government in
regard

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
regard to their trade in Korea. In the five-point
memorandum of April 13, 1935, in duscussing the per-
centage of the natural increase to be allotted to
the foreign companies, the phrase "Chosen possibly
better" was inserted. While not a definite assurance,
the inclusion of this clause indicated that it was
intended that at least the same and possibly better
treatment, than that accorded in Japan Proper, would
be accorded in Korea. hen this Embassy took the
five-point memorandum to the Foreign Office for con-
firmation, however, Mr. Kurusu, then Chief of the
Bureau of Commercial Affairs, stated that he was un-
able to confirm the point in regard to Korea, as he
had not participated in the disoussions on this point.
The assurance contained in the five-point memorandum,
therefore, cannot be considered to be of value, es-
pecially in view of the fact that the oil quotas in
Korea and Formosa are not administered by the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry of the central Government
but are administered by the Governments General, under
the supervision of the Ministry of Overseas Affairs.
The foreign oil companies, however, are of the opinion
that the treatment accorded them in Korea may be con-
sidered as an indication of the treatment which they
may in the future receive in Japan Proper, and there-
fore they are inclined to consider the matter as of
considerable importance.
Reply to Representations.
As the Department was informed in the Embassy's
telegram

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
telegram No. 6, January 15, 6 p.m., on January 15,
1937, Mr. Matsushima of the Foreign Office handed
to Mr. Dickover the reply to the formal representa-
tions made on December 24, 1936. The reply to the
aide memoire was in the form of a note verbale, a
3/
copy of which is enclosed, while in another document,
4/
marked "Unofficial" (copy enclosed) were the replies
to the two points regarding the kerosene quotas
and the proposed revised tariff which had been brought
out in the oral representations. The British Embassy
at approximately the same time received the reply to
their representations, in the form of a note verbale
5/
(copy enclosed) containing the same information, in
almost the same words, as that in the two documents
received by this Embassy. The British Embassy had
not divided its representations into oral and formal,
as had this Embassy, and consequently the form of the
reply was somewhat different. The notes were de-
livered in both the Japenese and English languages,
in order that, as Mr. Matsushima said to Mr. Dick-
over, "there might be no misunderstanding". This
procedure is unusual, as the Foreign Office almost in-
variably uses only Japanese in its correspondence
with the foreign diplomatic missions in Tokyo.
Both this Embassy and the British Embassy con-
sider the phrase, "On the contrary, the reference was
intended to be a oonfirmation in writing of the four
points" as a satisfactory assurance and as a gratifying
conolusion

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
conolusion to our respective representations. The
reply to the oral representations was not as satis-
factory, as it indicated that the foreign oil com-
panies in the future will be made to bear the bulk
of reductions made necessary in the quotas because of
increased domestic production, on the ground that such
cases are "exceptional", and, moreover, that the Government
intends to proceed with the proposed tariff revision,
thus indicating that little reliance can be placed on the
previous verbal assurance that there will be no further
discrimination between importers and refiners in fixing
tariffs.
Attitude of the Companies.
The local representatives of the American and
British oil interests express themselves as greatly
pleased with the broad assurances obtained through
the Foreign Office in regard to their trade in Japan
Proper, and feel that all has been obtained that could
be expeoted from the Government. The tendenoy shown
gradually to whittle away the trade of the foreign oil
companies in Korea they regard as regrettable, and they
consider that the replies given to the oral representa-
tions indicate that advantage will be taken of oppor-
tunities for "exceptions" to turn over all the natural
incfease in the trade in Japan Proper to the domestic
refineries. Taking these facts into consideration,
therefore, they believe that their future trading posi-
tion in the Japanese Empire will be approximately as
follows:

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
follows: In Japan Proper they will be able to retain
their 1935 volume of trade, probably with small in-
creases in quotas if the manufacture of substitutes
(oil from the hydrogenation of coal and alcohol) in-
creases in lesser ratio than the natural increase in
consumption of oils, but with decreases in quotas if
the manufacture of substitutes increases in greater
ratio. The Formosa position will be much the same,
with perhaps the l936 volume of trade as the basis.
In Korea no more than the 1937 volume of trade can be
expected (it will be remembered that the 1937 quotas
were cut considerably below those for l936), with the
possibility that quotas will be decreased still further
because of the increasing local production of oil from
coal. As the price for retaining this volume of busi-
ness, the foreign oil companies are asked to invest
more capital in order to supply tankage for three
months' stocks of oil and to assure the Mitsui in-
terests of an adequate return on their investment
in arranging for the storage, on behalf of the foreign
oil companies, of an additional three months' stocks
of o11.
This, very briefly, is the position in which the oil
interests now find themselves. The directors of the com-
panies have been fully acquainted with the facts and are
now considering them. In view of the political crisis
in Japan at the moment, however, it is believed that the
directors will delay a decision until the complexion of
the

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.97)
the nert Cabinet is ascertained, as the character of
the government under which the companies will have to
operate has an important bearing on the case.
Respectfully yours,
2072
Joseph C. Grew.

January 9, 1937.
Mr. Matsushina, Chief of the Bureau
of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, and
Mr. Dickover.
Subject: Petroleum.
I called on Mr. Matsushima at the Foreign Office at
11:30 by appointment.
I recalled to Mr. Matsushima's attention the fact
that the American Ambassador had made certain representa-
tions to the Minister for Foreign Affairs on December 24,
in regard to the assurances which the American oil inter-
ests required in order to continue business in Japan on a
secure basis. I then stated that the gasoline quotas which
were given the Standard Vacuum Oil Company in Korea for the
first half of 1937 were 37.7% less than one-half of the
1936 quotas and that smaller reductions, of about l0%, had
been made in the quotas for kerosene and lubricating oil.
I pointed out that these reductions in the Korean quotas,
added to the previous developments which had been men-
tioned in our representations, were most disheartening to
the American oil interests. I said that the oil companies
were being asked either to invest further large sums of
money in Japan or to guarantee an adequate return on the
sums advanced by the Mitsui interest to store the non-
commercial stocks of oil for the Standard interests, and
that it was impossible for the American in terests either
to invest further sums of money in Japan or to guarantee
an adequate return to Mitsui when their future here was
as uncertain as was indicated by this latest development
in the situation. Mr. Matsushima agreed heartily, and
said that Mr. Arita had asked him to take up the whole
problem

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
problem of the foreign oil interests in Japan with the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry and was pressing him
almost every day to urge the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry for an eerly adjustment of the situation. He
proceeded to state that he did not believe thet the
English version of the reply of the Ministry of Comnerce
and Industry to the Standard Vacuum Oil Company's request
for assurences had been properly translated. He said
that, instead of the translation "careful consideration"
he thought that "due consideration" or "full consideration"
would have been better. He said that in Japan the latter
interpretations would have been considered as adequate
for assurances. He added, however, that he understood
the forein companies' position and feelings in the
matter. He said that imaediately after receiving the
roply of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry they had
received a cut in their kerosene quotas and a threat
of discriminatory tariffs and that those factors caused
the companies to doubt the value of the assurances which
had been given by the Ministry of Comnerce and Industry.
He said that he was discussing this phase of the question
with that Ministry and was endeavoring to persuade them
to restore the quotas to their previous quantities and
to adjust the situation in other ways in order to meet
the requirements of the foreign oil companies.

Enclosure No.2 to despateh No.

the Embassy at Tokyo.
January 20, 1937.
PRESENT:
Mr. Matsushima (Chief of Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Office)
Messrs. Meyer (S-V.O.C.) and Kly ( R.S.P.Co.)
After opening in a very pleasant manner this first
interview since he had succeeded to Mr. Kurusu's position,
Mr. Matsushima said that he understood that the Com anies' rep-
resentatives wished to see him in regard to the Chosen quota
matter concerning whioh Sir George Sansom and Mr. Dickover had
also paid him a visit previously. Messrs. Meyer and Ely
confirmed that this was so: Mr. Matsushima would appreciate,
they said, the importance of this matter to them, especially
now that the contents of the Japanese Government's notes
to the British and U.S. Embassies had been made known to
their Prinoipals, as the treatment they received in Chosen
must materiaily influence their interpretation of the as-
surances which had been given by the Government.
Mr. Matsushima said that he had already addressed an
official enquiry to the Overseas Ministry but had not yet
received a reply, so that he was not in a position to make
any comments at the moment. He asked if the Companies were
represented in Keijo and if the Government General had given
any explanation of the reduction in quotas. He was told
that the very brief explanation to the Companies' Keijo
Managers was to the effect that the reduction was due to
the factor of production by hydrogenation, but the manner
in whieh this factor had been taken into account was, to
put it mildly, extraordinary. Messrs. Meyer and Ely had
brought for Mr. Matsushima a copy of the protest they had
filed in the form of a letter addressed to the Governor
General and this, they thought, would make the position
clear to him: generally the treatment they had received
in regard to Chosen quotas seemed to be entirely at variance
with the assurances given by the Government.
Mr. Matsushima pointed out that the question of the
Chosen quotas had not been raised when the Embassies made their
representations to the Foreign Office. It had not come up
until later - actually when the Government's reply had
already been drafted - and therefore, while the reply made
reference to two partioular points, the Tariff and the
Japan Kerosene Quotas, which had been mentioned originally
by the Embassies it could not deal with the Chosen matter.
He had, however, made enquiries and would let the Companies
know as 'soon as possible.
Messrs.

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Messrs. Meyer and Ely thanked Mr. Matsushima and
hoped that they would hear something in the very near
future. In regard to the matter of the Japan Kerosene
quotas, whioh Mr. Matsushima had just mentioned, they
wished to say that they had been informed by their
Embassies of the reference made to it in the Japanese
Government's notes, but they themselves were unable to
understand it. Their Companies had not complained that
no increase in quotas had been given to them in 1936 but
that their trade had been decreased in that year as com-
pared with l935, while other companies had been given
increases. After the facts of the case had been briefly
outlined, a copy of the Companies' joint letter to the
Minister of Commeree and Industry was handed to Mr.
Matsushima who promised to study it.
Mr. Matsushima said that he hoped that this whole
oil question would be settled soon, but it seemed to him
rather as though things were going in a vicious circle -
the Commerce Department's attitude being that the Com-
panies could not be given increased trade until they had
conformed with the Law, and the Companies' that they
were not willing to conform unless they were given an
increase. Messrs. eyer and Ely said that this was not
so: the position was that the arrangements whereby stocks
would be laid down to conform with the Law involved them
in certain obligations with Mitsuis and they could not
undertake these obligations unless and until they knew
where they stood - it was, as their Directors had
put it on one oocasion, almost a matter of straight
mathematios. The sooner their Principals could inter-
pret more precisely the assurances given by the Govern-
ment, the quicker could stock-keeping arrangements be
made.
In concluding the interview Mr. Matsushima re-
iterated his willingness to see Messrs. meyer and Ely
at any time and to do his utmost to bring about a satis-
factory solution of the Companies' problems.

Note Verbale.
The Department of Foreign Affairs, having consulted
the competent Department on the subject of the Aide
Memoire of the United States Embassy, dated December
24th, 1936, have the honour to state in reply as follows:
The Japanese Government have no intention to make
any discrimination between the business operations of the
Standard Vacuum Oil Company, which represents the American
oil interests in Japan, and those of eny other oil indus-
trialists. Nor have they any thought whatever of driving
out of Japan the American oil interests represented by
that company. To be more precise, the phrase, "they will
be taken into full consideration", in the reply addressed
by the Director of the Mines Bureau in the Department of
Commerce and Industry to the General Manager of the Standard
Vacuum Oi1 Company, under date of November 2lst, 1936, did
not mean that consideration would be given to the question
as to whether or not the four points enumerated in the
letter addressed by the General Lanager of the said company
to the Minister for Commerce and Industry on October 9th,
1936, would be carried into execution. On the contrary,
the reference was intended to be a confirmation in writing
of the four points. The company, therefore, need entertain
no misgiving, and it is hoped that this information will be
conveyed

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
conveyed to it so that there shall be no misunder-
standings in this connection.
The Japanese Government hereby declare that, so
long as the above-mentioned company faithfully observes
the Japanese laws and regulations concerned, they will
always be prepared to give favourable consideration to
its business operations. At the same time, they desire
to point out that the company should speedily fulfil
the obligations required by the Japanese laws and re-
gulations.
January 14th, 1937.

1. That an increase was allowed to home oil in-
dustrialists but not to the Standard Vacuum Oil

quotas for the latter half of 1936, is because
the increase in the home output of oil, and this
alone, rendered it possible to increase allot-

ment was required, due note being taken of the
first of the four points set forth in the letter
addressed to the Minister for Commerce and In-
dustry by the General Manager of the Standard
Vacuum Oil Company on October 9th, 1936.
2.
The proposed tariff revision imposes no increase
on the kerosene, light oil and lubricating oil

The Japanese Government have no intention to make any

Rising Sun Petroleum Co.,Ltd., which represents the British
oil interests in Japan, and those of any other oil indus-
trialists. Nor have they any thought whatever of driving
out of Japan the British oil interests represented by that
company. To be more precise, the phrase, they will be
taken into full consideration", in the reply addressed by
the Director of the Mines Bureau in the Department of
Commerce and Industry to the Managing Director of the
Rising Sun Petroleum Co., under date of November 2lst,
1936, did not mean that consideration would be given to
the question as to whether or not the four points enu-
merated in the letter addressed by the Managing Director
of the said company to the Minister for Commerce and In-
dustry on October 9th, 1936, would be carried into execu-
tion. On the contrary, the reference was intended to be
a confirmation in writing of the four points. The company,
therefore, need entertain no misgiving, and it is hoped
that this information will be conveyed to it so that there
shall

=== Page 404 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
shall be no misunderstandings in this connection.
The British Note Verbale refers to the fact that
no increase was granted to the Rising Sun Petroleum
Co. in the new trading quotas for kerosene for the
latter half of l936. The fact is that the increase
then made in the quotas for home oil industrialists was
due to the increase in the home output of oil. Accord-
ingly, under the first of the four points above referred
to, the matter was dealt with as an exceptional case.
Reference is also made to the proposed import tariff
rates, but this Customs treatment is applicable to any
person or concern without any distinction, and i not,
therefore, of a nature to present any question.

long as the above-mentioned company faithfully observes
the Japanese laws and regulations concerned, they will
always be prepared to give favourable consideration to
its business operations. At the same time, they desire
to point out that the company should speedily fulfil the
obligations required by the Japanese laws and regulations.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) American and British Oil Interests in Japan.
No satisfactory written confirmation was received
from the Japanese Government of the oral assurances
894.6363
given to the representatives of the American and British
oil interests on September 19, 1936, by Mr. Yoshino, then
Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry.* Furthermore,
the Ministry of Commerce and Industry having adopted an
antagonistic attitude in November, certain other indi-
cations of opposition to and discrimination against the
foreign oil companies became manifest in the first half
of December. Therefore the representatives of the Ameri-
can and British companies came to the conclusion that
little or nothing could be expected from the oral assur-
ances which had been given in good faith by Mr. Yoshino
before his resignation as Vice Minister for Commerce and
Industry.**
Consequently, after consultation between the Ameri-
can and British oil representatives and their respective
Embassies and under instructions received from the Gov-
ernments of the United States and reat Britain, the Ameri-
can and British Embassies on December 24 made substantial-
ly identical (not joint) representations in writing to the
Japanese Foreign Office in which each Government expressed
its anxiety with regard to the future of its oil interests
in Japan, requested a definite indication from the Japan-
ese Government that the latter does not desire to force
those oil interests out of Japan, and expressly requested
that the Japanese Government give the written assurances
sought
* Embassy's despatch No. 2076 of October 14, 1936.
**Embassy's despatch No. 2194 of December 28, 1936.

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
sought by its oil interests for the ccnt nuation of op-
erations in Japan.*
On December 25, the day after the making of repre-
sentations, a report was published by Domei to the effect
that the American Ambassador had notified the Minister
for Foreign Affairs of the intention of the United States
Government to retain 19,O00 tons of destroyers in excess
of the London Treaty limits because of Great Britain's
retention of five. "B" class cruisers under the authority
of Article 17 of the London Naval Treaty. Since the
American Government's notification of the retention of
destroyers had been made on November 6, there is a
possibility that the Japanese Foreign Office gave out
this version of the Ambassador's conversation with Mr.
Arita in an effort to conceal the fact that representa-
tions had been made by the American and British Govern-
ments on the oil question.

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apri1 22, 1937
Tokyo's No. 2337 of April 1 disausses
Japan's efforts to free herself of dependence
on foreign countries for liquid fuel.
The Minister for Commerce and Industry
described in the Diet the bill which would
create an Imperial Fuel Company which would
serve as the central administrative organ
for all synthetic petroleum enterprises in
Japan. It would be capitalized at about
Yen 750 million and shared in by both
Government and public.
It is hoped that by the operation of
this Company Japan would become largely
self-sufficient in liquid fuel within seven
years. By 1943, it is expected that the
entire Japanese demand will be supplied
by gasoline substitutes and by gasoline re-
fined in Japan from imported crude oil.
It is the opinion of foreign experts
that gasoline from coal can be produced
commercially in Japan. While subsidies may
be required at first, eventually a self-
supporting gasoline industry can be
developed which, with a degree of rationing,
will be able to supply the nation in time
of war.

Godo, made a statement explanatory of the Imperial Fuel
Company bill and the bill for the encouragement of the
artificial petroleum industry. The two measures constitute
a seven-year fuel plan under which the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry hopes to render Japan about 60 per cent self-
sufficient in gasoline and 45 per cent self-sufficient
in heavy oil by 1943 (the term "heavy oil" refers to
oils such as fuel oil and diesel oil).
According to the statement of the Minister for Com-
merce and Industry, it is planned to invest a total of
Yen 700,000,000, partly in the form of subscriptions
to stock and partly in the form of debentures, in the
projected Imperial Fuel Company, which will act as the
central administrative organ for all synthetic petro-
leum enterprises in Japan. In addition, the existing
synthetic petroleum plants, capitalized at approxi-
mately Yen 50,000,000, will be taken over by the Imperial
Fuel Company, making the total funds invested in the in-
dustry about Yen 750,000,000. It is planned that the
Government and the public will share equally in the sub-
scriptions to the stock of the Company.
The Ministry of Commerce and Industry estimates that,
taking the annual increase in demand as 160,000 kilo-
litres, by 1943 the domestic demand for gasoline will be
2,470,000 kilolitres. During the same period the domes-

kilolitres. Therefore, whereas in l936 there was a short-
age in supply as compared with demand of 1,271,800 kilo-
litres, by 1943 the shortage will be reduced to 949,500
kilolitres. Detailed figures of this supply and demand

The plan contemplates the erection of several plants,
of the annual capacity of 100,000 kilolitres each, for the
hydrogenation of coal. The plants are to be located in
towns near the source of the raw material, coal, rather
than in urban centers, and are to be designed to provido
easy access to railway and water transport.
According to the statement of the Minister for Com-
merce and Industry, the cost of production of gasoline
by either the hydrogenation process or the gasoline syn-
thesis process will, in 1937, be about 73 sen (20.8 cents)
per gallon, as compared with the present retail price of
natural petroleum gasoline in Tokyo of 51 sen (14.5 cents)
per gallon. (It is expeoted, however, that the price of
petroleum

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
petroleum gasoline will be raised to 6l sen (17.3 cents)
per gallon in the near future, owing to the proposed in-
orease of duty of 5 sen per gallon and the proposed con-
sumption tax of 5 sen per gallon.) The Ministry of Com-
merce and Industry hopes, however, that the cost of gaso-
line from coal can be lowered to 57 sen (16.2 cents) by
1943. It is hoped, moreover, that the cost of gasoline
from the low temperature carbonization of coal can be
brought down to the level of the price of natural petro-
leum gasoline by 194l. It will, therefore, be necessary
to provide a governmental subsidy for the artificial oil
plants for the first few years of operation, and it is under-
stood that a subsidy of Yen 57.00 ($16.24) per kilolitre
of gasoline will be granted for the first year. No figures
are given for the subsidy on heavy oil, if it is planned to
grant any. The total cost of the subsidies for the seven-
year scheme is estimated at Yen 95,000,000 (about $27,075,000).
In addition the Government will guarantee a minimum annual
dividend on the shares of the Imperial Fuel Company for ten
years and will guarantee the interest payments on the
debentures issued by the Company. It is expected that
these guarantees will cost the Government Yen 19,000,000
($5,415,000). The estimated cost to the Government, there-
fore, exclusive of its investment in the Imperial Fuel Com-
pany, wi11 be Yen 114,000,000 (32,490,000).
Comment.
The opinion of foreign experts (Dutoh and German) who
are or have been in Japan in conneotion with the sale to
Japanese interests of patents for the hydrogenation of coal,
is that the Japanese will be able to produce artificial
gasoline

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
gasoline and fuel oils from coal on a commeroial basis
(although somewhat more expensive than gasoline and fuel
oils from petroleum), because of the low cost of labor and
coal in Japan, Korea and Manchuria. Even at a cost of 73
sen (20.8 cents) per gallon, gasoline will not be any more
expensive in Japan than in many States of the United States,
and therefore the price should prove no great impediment
to the development of motor transport. The opinion of the
experts, however, is that the scheme cannot be realized in
less than ten years, and that it will cost about Yen 1,200,000,000
instead of Yen 750,000,000.
It will undoubtedly be necessary to subsidize the pro-
duction of gasoline from coal for a time, as the suddon
increasing of the price of natural gasoline to 73 sen per
gallon by means of customs and consumption taxes would create
a social problem in Japan, where taxi, bus and truck owners are
accustomed to operate on 5l sen gasoline. However, it is ex-
pected that the Government will pay subsidies to the pro-
ducers of gasoline from coal for a time, and will gradually
raise the price of petroleum gasoline, thereby acoustoming
the populace to increased cost of motor transport. Even-
tually the Government will be able to dispense with the sub-
sidies and will have a self-supporting artificial gasoline
industry, which, with a degree of rationing, will be able to
supply the nation in time of war.
The development of this substitute liquid fuel industry
in Japan has a very important bearing upon the future in
this country of the American and other foreign oil interests.
Th1s

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.93)
- 8-
This problem will be disoussed in the next of the series
of despatches on the subjeot of "American Oil Interests
in Japan".
Respectfully yours,
Po. 
Joseph C. Grew.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 2233, dated January 29, 1937, on the subject of the
difficulties being experienced in Japan by the American
and other foreign oil interests under the Petroleum In-
dustry Law of l934, and to submit the following report
AN
on the latest developments in the situation.
JG1
FILED
Korean Quotas.
1
19
In dospatch No. 2233, the Embassy reported that the Stan-
dard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company
had protested to the Government General of Korea against
the reduotion in their sales quotas for the first half
of 1937 and had called upon Mr. Matsushima, Chief of the
Bureau
F/G

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office,
leaving with him copies of their protests to the Govern-
ment General. On February 4 the local represontatives
of the two companies left with Mr. Matsushima copies of
a telegram on the subject from their head offices (copy
1/
enclosed). Having received no repiy from either the
Government General or Mr. Matsushima, the local representa-
tives of the two companies called on Mr. Matsushima on
March 16 to inquire regarding the reply. Mr. Matsushima
said that he had referred the matter to the Government
General through the Department of Overseas Affairs and
had received a reply, which he had sent back as being
"too simple". (He probably meant that the reply was not
logical.) In the meantime, as a matter of policy, the two
companies have been selling gasoline in Korea at the same
rate as during last year, i.e., as if there had been no cut
in their quotas. Consequently, they will have sold out
their quotas for the first half of the year by the end of
April, after which their dealers will have to close down
their selling stations until July l, unless some ameliora-
tion of their position can be obtained. The dealers in
Korea of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company realize the plight
in which they will be placed, and have sent a delegation
to Japan to press the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company to adopt
some plan which will assure them of a continuance of their
trade. The dealers at first proposed that they be permitted
to handle petroleum products other than those of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, in order to replace the deficit
caused by the cut in the quota of the Company. They later
proposed

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
proposed that they be permitted to establish in Korea a
refinery to supply them with gasoline equivalent to the
former Standard-Vacuum quota, the Company to guarantee
them a steady supply of crude oil for refining. They
also informed the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company that the
Government General of Korea intended to establish a
socond refinery in the peninsula, the first being the
refinery of the recently-established Chosen Oil Company,
and expected that the two oil refineries, with the coal
hydrogenation plants to be established under the Imperial
Fuel Company, would be able to supply the entire demand of
Korea for gasoline within a few years, at which time the
Government General would institute a petroleum monopoly
similar to that already established in "Manchukuo". The
Standard-Vacuum dealers hoped to obtain the license for
this second refinery and thus to be able to continue in
the petroleum business even should a monopoly be established
and the foreign oil companies be completely eliminated from
the Korean market. The dealers (all of whom are Japanese)
claimed that they had had no previous understanding with
the Government General of Korea, but the local representa-
tives of the Standard-Vacuum Oil Comrany are not inclined
to credit this statement. For some unknown reason, the
dealers of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company have not joined
the Standard-Vacuum dealers in the movement to finance a
refinery in Korea.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company informed the dealers
that the Company would under no circumstances give considera-
tion to the refinery proposal or to any alternative proposal
unt11

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
until the Japanese Government had replied to its petition
in regard to restoration of its Korean quotas, and that
while the Company expected that the Government's reply
would be favorable and would permit it to remain in busi-
ness in Korea under its present status, nevertheless should
the reply be unfavorable, it was doubtful that the Company
would agree to the refinery proposal if it were conditional
upon a commitment by the Company to supply crude oil. The
dealers are not satisfied with this reply and propose to
remain in Yokohama until the matter is settled. The local
representatives of the Company have referred the matter to
the head office of the Company in New York but as yet no
reply has been received except as indicated in the next
section of this report.
Representations.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company is therefore being faced
with a situation in which it is unable to obtain a satis-
factory reply from the Korean Government General or the
Japanese Government to its petition to have its Korean quotas
restored, while its dealers are pressing for some solution
of the difficulty. Moreover, the cut in the Korean quotas
was made on the excuse that room had to be made for the
products of the hydrogenation of coal (although as a matter
of fact such products will not come on the market in Korea
until the latter part of the year), and the Company is in-
clined to believe that the treatment which they have received
in Korea in this connection will constitute an inder of the
tremtment which they will receive in the much larger
Japanese market when the coal hydrogenation plants now pro-
posed (see neit section) come into production. The assurances
given

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
given the oil companies through their respective Embassies
(Embassy's despatch No. 2194, dated December 28, 1936, and
despatch No. 2233, dated January 29, 1937), it will be
remembered, provides for the allocation of quotas, "subject
to preference being given to increase in indigenous produc-
tion (that is, domestic production of petroleum and manu-
facture of petroleum substitutes"). Later in the same as-
surance it is provided that should there be a decrease in
consumption or increase in indigenous production, "consi-
deration will be given so that the decrease is apportioned
fairly in the light of actual conditions at the time". The
companies place great importance upon the interpretation of
the word "fairly" and hold that they were not treated fair-
ly in the allocation of the Korean quotas, and that the in-
terpretation of the word in the allocation of the Korean quotas
may create a precedent which will be followed in Japan Proper
in the future.
The circumstances bein& as indicated above, the head of-
fices of the companies cabled to their local representatives
on March 24, 1937, suggesting that in their opinion it would
appear to be appropriate at the present juncture for the
Embassies to express concern to the Japanese Foreign Office,
at least unofficially, on the ground that the assurances are
not being implemented, especially in Korea, and to ask for
an early statement of the companies' position. The local
representatives of the oil companies discussed the question
with their respective Embassies, with the result that it
was decided not to make inquiries of the Foreign Office, even
informally and orally, at the present moment, because (1)
tho

=== Page 427 === (confidence 0.95)
27-1
- 6 -
the local representatives of the oil companies have not
yet pressed the Foreign Office sufficiently to be consi-
dered to have exhausted all resources at their command,
(2) an unofficial or informal inquiry of the Foreign of-
fice would only elicit an unofficial reply, which would
be of little or no value, and (3) the Embassies prefer
not to waste their influence with the Foreign Office on
心
minor questions, such as the Korean quota question, but
to reserve their efforts for the larger issues which un-
3
doubtedly will arise in the future, as is indicated in the
0
(1)
next section of this report. The Embassies are not inclined
3
to believe that the outcome of the Korean issue will to
any considerable extent influence the vastly more important
question of the companies' position in Japan under the
Government's apparent determination to attain a reasonable
degree of self-sufficiency in liquid fuel.
The local representatives of the oil companies have
reported the attitude of the Embassies to their head offices
by cable, and it is possible that a representative of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company will discuss the question with
the Department.
Future Position of the Foreign Oil Companies in Japan.
As was reported in despatch No. 2337, dated April 1,
1937, the Japanese Government is apparently determined to
proceed with its grandiose scheme to render Japan fairly
self-sufficient in liquid fuel through the hydrogenation
of coal, oil synthesis by the Fischer method, low temperature
carbonization

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.98)
29-2
- 7 -
cabornization of coal, and the mixing of absolute alcohol
with gasoline. The project, which contemplates the in-
vestment of about Yen 750,000,000, will establish the
Imperial Fuel Company, which will act as the financing
and administrative organ of all the synthetic gasoline
plants to be established in Japan, Korea and presumably
Manchuria. The plan contemplates the use of about
9,000,000 metric tons of coal per annum and, it is esti-
mated, will render Japan about 60 per cent self-sufficient
in gasoline and about 45 per cent self-sufficient in heavy
oil (fuel and diesel oil) at the end of seven years. Govern-
ment subsidies amounting to about Yen 1l4,000,000 will be
necessary to attain this end, in addition to the Government's
investment in the Imperial Fuel Company.
In view of the fact that coal hydrogenation industries
in other countries have not been commercially feasible be-
cause of the high cost, as compared with the cost of petro-
loum gasoline, of the gasoline produced by such hydrogena-
tion industries, it might be thought that the Japanese
scheme is impractical. Foreign experts who have studied
the question in Japan, however, are of the opinion that
the project is feasible, largely because of the low cost of
labor and coal in Japan, although it is improbable that the
object can be attained in seven years (ten years being a more
likely period) or that the scheme will cost only Yen 750,000,000.
It is considered more probable that the total cost of the
project will be about Yen 1,200,000,000. However, if the
military authorities are determined to proceed with the
plan and render Japan reasonably self-sufficent in liquid
fue1

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.99)
27-3
- 8 -
fuel, there is every reason to believe that they will
eventually accomplish their object, regardless of the
cost to the nation. The plans have been delayed for a
time by the dissolution of the Lower House of the Diet
before the Imperial Fuel Company bill and the bill pro-
viding for subsidies for the hydrogenation industries
could be approved, but the bills will undoubtedly be re-
introduced into the next session of the Diet and there is
no reason to believe that they will not be approved.
This scheme of the Government is of great importance
to the foreign oil interests in Japan, principally because
it will, if carried out, deprive them of their market here
for gasoline and kerosene, although it is probable that they
could continue to do some business ir other petroleum products.
As was stated before, the plan contemplates the production
of 60 per cent of Japan's requirements of gasoline by the
1943-44 fiscal year. This production does not include the
gasoline refined in Japan from imported crude oil. The
existing Japanese oil refineries, plus those already sanc-
tioned, will be able to produce the remaining 40 per cent
of Japan's requirements. In regard to kerosene, the authori-
ties are already in a position to dispense with imports
from foreign sources at any time. The position therefore
is that, by l944, or a year or two later, there will be no
room in the Japanese trade for imports of so-called "white
products".
The assurances recently received from the Japanese
Government concerning the future position of the foreign oil
companies in Japan do not protect the companies against
reduotion

=== Page 430 === (confidence 0.99)
22-4
- 9 -
reduction of quotas caused by increase of indigenous pro-
duction of gasoline substitutes. The assurances only
specify that "consideration will be given that the decrease
is apportioned fairly in the light of actual conditions at
the time". It may therefore reasonably be assumed that the
decrease in the demand for petroleum gasoline caused by the
increase in production of gasoline from coal will fall upon
the foreign gasoline importing companies, and that the domes-
tic refineries will be authorized to care for the entire
amount of gasoline which cannot be supplied by the hydroge-
nation plants. In regard to fuel and diesel oils, the
foreign oil companies will be in a somewhat better position,
as the hydrogenation plants, according to the estimate, will
be able to supply only about 45 per cent of the demand, but
the foreign companies estimate that most of the demand will
be given to Japanese importers, unless and until the foreign
companies lay in the six months' stocks required by law. As
the foreign companies cannot store such stocks under present
conditions in Japan, it appears that all or almost all of
the fuel and diesel oil trade will go to Japanese importers.
The same conditions apply to the lubricating oil and grease
trade.
Recent circumstances tend to indicate that the Japanese
authorities have in mind the forcing out of Japan of the
two foreign oil importing companies by the gradual reduction
of their quotas as products from the hydrogenation of coal
come into the market. This is, of course, implied in the
whole scheme of obtaining oil from coal, but recently a
new circumstanoe lends strength to this opinion. For months
previous to last November the Government had been pressing
both

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.99)
27-5
- 10 -
both the foreign oil companies and the Mitsui interests,
with whom the oil companies then were trying to reach an
agreement in regard to the storage of non-commercial
stocks of oil, to proceed with the laying in of six months'
stocks as required by law. Since November, 1936, however,
the foreign oil companies have heard nothing further, either
from the Government or from the Mitsui interests, in regard
to the matter, and the supposition is that the Government
prefers that the oil companies remain in the position of
law-breakers (not having stored six months' stocks as re-
quired by law), in order that they will have no claim to
rights or privileges and that their quotas may be cit at
any time to make room for the products of the projected hydro-
genation plants.
The above forecast of the situation is predicated upon
the supposition that (1) the Japanese will be able to accom-
plish their program for the hydrogenation of coal and (2)
that they will be able to obtain supplies of crude oil for
their oil refineries. There is nothing now to indicate that
they will not succeed in fulfilling both of these conditions.
of course, many things may occur between now and the year
1944 which will completely change the aspect of the case,
such as a volte face in regard to Japan's policy of nationalis-
tic economy, or trade agreements which would require the
maintenance of the status guo as regards foreign investments
in Japan.At the present time, however, it can only be said
that conditionsin Japan do not appear at all hopeful for
the future of the foreign oil companies.
The foreign oil interests, therefore, are confronted
with the problem of deciding whether to sell out at once to
Japanese

=== Page 432 === (confidence 0.99)
27-6
-11-
Japanese interests (who undoubtedly would be protected by
the Government in their trade) or to wait for two or three
years longer in order to ascertain the probability of suc-
cess of the Japanese venture in the production of artifi-
cial gasoline. The only other alternative, if the Japanese
venture is successful, will be for the com anies to stay
in the market, selling what they are permitted to, and
eventually withdrawing and selling their properties at such
prices as may be obtainable under the conditions then pre-
vailing.
Under these circumstances, the Embassy believes that
it would be most useful if the headquarters of the Standard
and Shell interests could formulate a definitive policy in
regard to their trade in Japan, based on the latest develop-
ments and trends, as well as upon their past experiences in
this market. The time appears to have passed for the oil
companies to be concerning themselves with minor details
of their trade in the Japanese Empire, and it would appear
to be the part of wisdom for them to consider with the ut-
most seriousness the question of their future in Japan in
the light of the Japanese Government's apparent determination
to acquire a degree of self-sufficiency in liquid fuels. >If
and when the oil interests reach a decision in regard to this
matter, their respective diplomatic agencies will be able to
adopt an intelligent policy of assistance to them.
Respectfully yours
Joseph C. Gew

NOTE OF JOINT CABLED MESSAGE FROM LONDON & NEW YORK
Jan. 29:37
TO JAPAN REPRESENTATIVES OF R.S.P.CO. & S-V.O.C.
The Directors of the two Companies have carefully considered
the replies given by the Japanese Government to the British
and American Ambassadors and the Netherlands Minister; and,
having reference in particular to the Notes Verbales dated
15th January 1937, they find these replies in themselves
very reassuring.
The Directors are most grateful for these written assurances
from the Foreign Office of the Japanese Government; and they
would have no hesitation in considering that a satisfactory
working basis for the continuation of the operations of the
Companies in Japan had been accorded to them if currently
they were receiving from the administrative departments of
the Japanese Government the consideration and actual treat-
ment which the assurances entitle them to expect. When,
however, they attempt to weigh the assurances given by the
Foreign Office against actual past practice of the administra-
tive departments they are immediately confronted with great
anxiety and disappointment: and they are forced to the opinion
that, unless some means can be found to give effect, in the
administrative departments concerned, to the assurances from
the Foreign Office, then it will not be possible for the
Companies, as business concerns, to accept the continuation
of present practice as a working basis for their future
trading

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
trading in Japan.
The Directors respectfully request the Japanese Government's
sympathetic consideration of a review of the position of
the Companies as it appears to them:-
In l933, the last year of free trade conditions before
Government control became operative, the Companies
had a combined share in the total Gasoline trade of
Japan Proper of 53%: in 1937 (January/June) their
combined shares are only 4l% of the total trade - a
reduction of 22-l/2%. Of the Gasoline trade in Chosen
the Companies had in 1933 a share of 64%: their share
of the quotas allocated for January/June 1937 is 22-1/2% -
a reduction of 65%.
Of the Heavy Oil trade in Japan, Chosen and Taiwan the
Companies had a share of 44% in 1933: their share of
the quotas allocated for 1937 (January/June) is 32% -
a reduction of 27%.
In regard to Kerosene and Lubricating Oil, while the
reduction of their share of the trade is not so heavy
as in the case of Gasoline and Heavy Oil, it is never-
theless material and sufficient to give the Directors
greatest anxiety.
All negotiations during the last three years have been based
on three broad principles:-
Firstly that the Companies should be given quotas that
would enable them to continue trade commensurate with
the capital they had invested in the country
Secondly that the trade of the Companies should be
reasonably remunerative
Thirdly that the Companies should be treated fairly in
the matter of taxes on the Petroloum trade; and that

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
they should not be forced out of business by discrimina-
tory treatment of Importers vis-a-vis local Refiners.
The Companies have never contested the principle that a
Government has the sole right to decide what taxes it
should apply for revenue purposes: but the Directors wish
to draw the attention of the Japanese Government to the
assurances they have given, through their representative
Ministry, concerning the relative application of import
duties to Refined Cils and Crude Oils and, in particular,
to the statement made by the then Vice-Minister of Com-
merce & Industry at an interview accorded to the Companies'
Japan representatives on 22nd September, 1936, when he
emphatically declared that never again would Importers
of finished products have cause to feel discrimination
in respect of import duty and stated that he had made
this special point with the Finance Department. The
Japanese Government subsequently have realized this to
some extent in that (in the proposed revision of the
Petroleum Import Tariff) they have placed 5 sen per A.
G. on the Gasoline content of Light Crude Oil; but
their intention to abolish duty-free privileges on
Heavy Oil and to increase the duty on refined kerosene
and Lubricating Oil is directly contrary to their re-
peated verbal assurances and will put the Companies
in a position where they will either have to bear a loss
or go out of the market.
On the subject of trade quotas in Japan Proper and
in Chosen the Directors regret that they cannot accept
the Japanese Government's reply as conclusive and they
ask for further consideration and for written replies
to

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
to the letters addressed by the Companies' Japan re-
presentatives to the Minister of Commerce & Industry
on 18th December 1936 and 9th October 1936 (1etter No. M.2)
and to the letter written under date of January 14th
1937 to the Governor General in Chosen, where they regard
the action of the Government as particularly serious.

7

that as the Korean sales quotas would be practically exhausted

materialized, we assumed it would meet with the Government's
approval if we were to continue to sell at our normal rat
FILED

SKH:
Referonce Standard-Vacuum Oil Company's
letter of May 24.
With regard to the Manchurian oil situ-
atron, it is felt that a statement made to
Mr. Parker should be along the lines of the
memorandum of May 25 attached to Tokyo's
telegram No. 135, May 24, 6 p.m.
With regard to the Korean oil situa-
tion, it would appear that the oil companies
propose submitting a strongly worded state-
ment of their position to their respective
embassies for reference to the Japanese
Minister for Foreign Affairs, and that the
oil companies desire that the American and
British Embassies be instructed to make
representations to the Japanese Foreign Of-
fice "requesting speedy settlement of the
whole Korean problem in the light of the
assurances given by the Foreign Ofrice".
(The assurances referred to would appear
to be those given by the Japanese Foreign
Office last January to the effect that the
interests of the companies would be ac-
corded fair treatment and that previous as-
surances with regard to quotas, prices, and
stocks would be respected.)
Tokyo's telegram No. 136, May 24, 7 p.m.,
states that the "assurances" referred to
above

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
above do not specifically mention Korea but
that the Foreign Office has never denied
that Korea is included in the assurances.
The telegram further states that the Embassy
perceives no reason why representations re-
questing a settlement of the Korean quota
question should not be made.
It is felt that the Embassy should be
authorized to make representations along
the lines requested by the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company and that the form those repre-
sentations should take should be left to
the discretion of the Ambassador. Thes
representations of course would be contingent
upon the British Embassy making similar and
concurrent representations.
Mr. Parker requests that Mr. Hornbeck
discuss the matter with him over the tele-
phone, or, if Mr. Hornbeck considers it ad-
visable, Mr. Parker will come to Washington
for a discussion of the matter. It is be-
lieved that the matter may be satisfactorily
handled by telephone conversation with Mr.
Parker, inasmuch as the matter does not
call for extended explanation or discussion.
It is felt that drafting of a telegram
to Tokyo should await Mr. Hornbeck's con-
versation with Mr. Parker.

in this country.

March 6, 1939
Tokyo's strictly confidential despatch
no. 3671 of February 14, 1939, reports con-
versations held by Mr. A. H. DeFriest of the
General Petroleum Corporation of California,
with representatives of the Mitsui interests,
Japanese business men, and Colonel Hori,
Army representative on the General Planning
Board, in charge of oil supplies.
The primary questions in which the repre-
sentatives of Mitsui and other business men
seemed interested were (a) probable effects
on Japan of the application of any neutrality
legislation enacted in the United States and
(b) the obtaining of credits in the United
States for exports to Japan, particularly for
o1l.
Colonel Hori indicated to Mr. DeFriest
the grave concern with which Japanese
authorities regard the question of new
neutrality legislation in the United States;
stated that another Panay incident might occur
at any time, an event which would bring about
new neutrality legislation which would have a
serious effect in Japan, particularly as oil
would undoubtedly be prohibited; asked whether
the California oil companies would fall in
with sanctions in case they became effective;
made

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
ade the contradictory remarks that Japan has
the ability to pay her way for a long time to
come, having plenty of gold, and that Japan 1s
short of money and needs to obtain credits
for from one to two years; claimed that
foreign rights in China would be respected;
then contradicted himself by saying that the
Japanese would take over the direction of the
oil market in China; and said that, in regard
to Japan, there would be further restrictions
there on the use by the public of petroleum
produots.

=== Page 472 === (confidence 1.00)
Conversation
February 8, 1939.
Mr. A. H. DeFriest, Vice President,
Director of Sales, General Petroleum
Corporation of California, Los Angeles,
California.
Mr. De Friest, who called on the Ambassador today
by appointment, later called on me to explain the purposes
of his present visit to Japan.
Mr. De Friest stated that he had come to Japan on
the invitation of Mitsui and Company to his Company to
send a representative out to discuss matters in connection
with the importation of California oil into Japan. As
his Company is affiliated with the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company, which has large contracts with Japanese interests
and the Japanese Government, he was cautioned by his
superiors mostly to listen to what the Mitsui and other
interests, as well as Government representatives, might
have to say, and to be careful not to prejudice in any
way the established positicn of the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company.
During the past two or three days, Mr. De Friest had
talked with representatives of the Mitsui interests and
other important Japanese businessmen. The primary questions
in which they all seemed to be very much interested were
the probable effects on Japan of the application of any
new neutrality legislation enacted in the United States,
and the securing of credits in the United States for
exports to Japan, particularly for oil.
Mr. De Friest

=== Page 473 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
Mr. De Friest went on to relate the substance of
a conversation he had yesterday afternoon with Colonel
Hori, Army representative on the General Planning Board,
in charge of oil supplies. Mr. De Friest stated that in
the beginning of the conversation Colonel Hori, who did
practically all of the talking, seemed particularly de-
sirous of impressing upon Mr. De Friest the determina-
tion of the Japanese to see the China Incident through,
and expressed the hope that r. De Friest would do what
he could to make this clear to those with whom he came
in contact on his return to America. Colonel Hori then
referred to the likelihood of ne neutrality legislation
being enacted in the United States and, according to Mr.
De Friest, clearly indicated that this question was a
matter of grave concern to the Japanese authorities. Mr.
De Friest said he was thereafter astounded to hear Colonel
Hori say that another PANAY incident, which, though it
would be purely accidental as had been the original one,
might occur at any time. Such an event would undoubtedly
bring about new neutrality legislation in the United States
which would have a most serious effect in Japan, particularly
as it would undoubtedly prohibit the shipment of American oil
to Japan. Colonel Hori went on to say that, should it come
to pass that moral sanctions against Japan became effeotive
through the concerted action of the American people, he
would like to know whether the California oil companies,
in view of their large business connections with Japan,
would go along with such sanctions or would endeavor to
ignore

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
ignore them.
Colonel Hori then referred to the question of credits
and stated that the working of the exchange control in Japan
had given the impression in some quarters that Japan was
unable to pay, lacked gold, and hence was a poor credit
risk. Colonel Hori stated that Japan had the ability to
pay her way for a long time to come and had plenty of
gold. On the other nand, without realizing the contra-
dictory nature of his statements, Colonel Hori said that
Japan was now short of money and that it would be necessary
to obtain credits of from one to two years, for which
every possible guarantee of payment could be produced,
to enable the carrying out of certain long-term projects.
Colonel Hori then went on to say that he could give
every assurance that foreign rights in China would be
respected, and he dwelt on this subject for some little
time. Mr. De Friest stated that he thereafter inquired
of Colonel Hori just what status the foreign oil companies
would have in China and was startled to hear Colonel Hori
advance an entirely contradictory theory, to the effect
that the Japanese would take over the airection of the oil
market in China. Colonel Hori said that it was useless,
until such time as the military situation was entirely
cleared up, to begin processing petroleum products in
China, as the direct import into China of the raw mater-
ials would to some extent betray their military positions,
but once military operations cease, the Japanese will
begin to install the necessary equipment to process the
raw materials and to assume the direction of the market.
Mr. De Friest

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Mr. De Friest then inquired concerning the situation
of the oil companies in Japan with respect to the future.
Colonel Hori said that there would be further restrictions
on the use by the public of petroleum products, particularly
oil and gasoline, and that such restrictions would be
continued for some time after the close of the China Incident.
He assured Mr. De Friest, however, that, even with de-
creased consumption and the likelihood of an increase in
local refining facilities, and particularly the use of the
hydrogenation process in the production of oil and gasoline,
the foreign oil companies would still have their proportion-
ate share in the raw materials imported.
J.. MCGTK.

In the course of the instruction the Department
states that it will be glad to be informed whether, in
the matter of requests for certified copies of the laws
of Jaian, a consular officer has at his disposal a
procedure by which he can, as a condition precedent to
certifying, require the filing of a supplementary
certification from a Japanese official over a seal known
to him. There are various Japanese officials who are
authorized to certify that given documents in Japanese
offered to their inspection are or are not among the laws
of Japan; and an American consular officer would be in
position to authenticate (directly or upon further proof)
the seal and capacity of the certifying Japanese official.
A firm of lawyers wnich is familiar with the procedure,
having obtaining certified copies of laws required in
connection with the issue of bonds in the United States,
is McIvor, Kauffman, Smith and Yamamoto, 12 Naka-dori,

from the Department that Terry, Cavin and Mills so desire,
the Eabassy will be glad to arrange f e obtaining of

McIvor, Kauffman, Smith and Ya amoto. The same firm is in

Embassy's previous experience with the firm is that its
charges are not unreasonable.

Tokyo, July 8, 1939.
No. 4023
SUBJECT:
SYNTHETIC PETROLEUM PROCESSES IN JAPAN.
A-M/C
PECOL
ONI
9746
39  2 48
M.D
q11ne
OFFICE OF THE ADS
94.6363/335
INTERNATIONAL 
AUG181939
AUS
DEPARTMCH OT SATE
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
F
Sir:
F/A
I have the honor to bring to the Department's
1/
attention an article which recently appeared in a
propaganda publication in Japan on the subject of
progress made in the production of synthetio petroleum
in this country. The article makes a number of ex-
travagant claims and no doubt is not correct in all
details, but nevertheless some points in it appear
worth recording for reference.
According

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
According to the article the oldest method of syn-
thetio petroleum production used in Japan is the low
temperature carbonization of coal method which is now
widely employed with increasingly satisfactory results.
Among the collieries enumerated as using this method are
Eian in Chosen, Uchihoro in Karafuto, Wanishi in the
Hokkaido, and Ube in Yamaguchi Prefecture. This process
it is stated has amply proved its superior efficiency.
The second method is the direct liquefaction of coal,
or hydrogenation (the adding of hydrogen to coal to bring
it to the composition of petroleum). It is stated that
under this process two plants were completed last year and
brought into operation, one at Agochi in Chosen under the
joint management of the Japanese Navy and the Chosen Nitrogenous
Manufacturing Company, and one at Fushun in Manchuria under
the joint management of the Japanese Navy and the South Man-
churia Railway Company. The Agochi factory was installed with
Japanese equipment which developed certain cons truotional
defects, the readjustment of which is not yet completed.
The Chosen Nitrogenous Company is said to have decided to
expand its plant at Agochi on a large scale and to erect
another large plant in Manchuria.
A process which combines certain features of the two
methods already cited is the hydrogenation of tar obtained
from low temperature carbonization. For this process a
plant has been constructed at Szepingkai in Manchuria, and
similar plants are under construction in Kawasaki by the
Nihon Yuka Kogyo Company, in Nagoya by the Tobo Kagaku
Kogyo Company, and in Wakamatsu by the Nissan Yekitai
Nenryo Company.
The

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The remaining method employed is the producing of
petroleum by a synthetic process from coal which has been
converted into a gaseous state, a process practiced in
Germany and known there as the Fischer method. The Mitsui
Bussan Kaisha, according to the article, is constructing
a plant at Miike in Kyushu and another at Kinken in Man-
churia to exploit this process; and a new company, the
Hokkaido Synthetic Petroleum Manufacturing Company, will
construct one plant at Ryusen and one plant at Tomae in
the Hokkaido for the annual production of l00,O00 kilograms
of artificial petroleum by this method.
The article states that the hydrogenation and synthetic
processes are still open to question, requiring further
study, and that there have been serious delays in the com-
mencing of operations of plants constructed. It is further
stated that the particular lack is a lack of adequate en-
gineering skill. It is admitted that production in the
industry has not approached a paying basis as a commercial
undertaking, and the price of synthetic petroleum in Japan
is given as roughly double that of imported petroleum.
The article is enclosed herewith.
Respectfully yours,
Eugene H. Dooman
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.
663.6
CC:C

1/ Bulletin of the South Sea Association,
6, Marunouchi 3-chome, Kojimachi-ku, Tokyo,
volume II, number 6, June 1939 (one copy only).

Mr. P. W. Parker, Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
Mr. Hornbeok

1-1493
Mr. Parker reviewed developments in the situation
in Japan with regard to the distribution and sale of
petroleum products. These developments are described
in a telegram addressed on June 27, 1939, to the head
office of the company by the Yokohama office. A copy
of the telegram, which was left by Mr. Parker, is
attached. Mr. Parker felt that the situation was such
that for the time being the company could continue to
do business in Japan on a somewhat more restricted basis
PLED
than previously.
Mr. Parker went on to say that the company was
facing problems not only in relation to questions pre-
F/A
sented by the Japanese Government's oil polioy, but also
in consequence of the action of this Government in giving
notioe

=== Page 510 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
notioe of termination of the commercial treaty with Japan.
with respect to the latter, he said that if the company
discontinued shipments of oil to Japan it might be faced
with the possibility that the Japanese Government might
thereafter withhold release of exchange for stocks already
in Japan. He said it had been the policy of the company
not to ship except upon the issuance of exchange permits
in an amount equivalent to the goods to be shipped, but
that in this procedure there was a time lag of about five
or six months.
Mr. Parker stated that Japan now has commercial
storage facilities for nearly 2,900,000 tons, which is
equivalent to almost one year's consumption and a ccording
to the best estimates available Japan has an additional
6,000,000 tons of storage facilities owned by the Japan-
ese Navy for naval requirements. He said that this amount
was large, not as compared with the storage facilities in
the United States, but as compared with many other countries,
Australia, for example. He alluded to the possibility
that if Japan were deprived of her normal oil supplies,
the Japanese Navy might be tempted to seize Borneo, a
matter which was already giving the Netherlands Government
concern.
Mr. Parker said it would be helpful if the Depart-
ment could give him information as to the prospects in
the

=== Page 511 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
the situation so that the company could be guided thereby
in formulating its plans. He said he would like to know,
for example, whether it would be desirable for the American
staffs in Japan to send their families home.
Mr. Parker was told that there was nothing that the
Department could now tell him in regard to the situation
created by the denunciation of the treaty. It was ex-
plained to him that the Secretary, in his statements to
the press and to others, had not gone beyond the observa-
tion that the notice of termination speaks for itself. As
regards the question of sending home from Japan the families
of American employees, it was suggested that there was
nothing in the situation which need prompt such action,
but that now was obvious ly not a good time for American
families in Japan to enter into arrangements which would
complicate their departure should such a course eventually
appear to be advisable.
Mr. Parker said that should in he future the Depart-
ment be in a position to give further information in
regard to the situation he would be glad to come to
Washington at any time.
m.m.
